On Multiplicative Weightings for Lie Groupoids and Lie Algebroids
Daniel Hudson

Abstract




\spacedallcaps

On Multiplicative Weightings for Lie Groupoids and Lie Algebroids
\spacedlowsmallcaps

by
\spacedlowsmallcaps

Daniel Hudson

A thesis submitted in conformity with

the requirements for the degree of

Doctor of Philosophy

Graduate Department of Mathematics

University of Toronto

© Copyright by Daniel Hudson 2024

Abstract

We present a thorough study of the differential geometry of weightings and develop the theory of weightings for vector bundles, Lie groupoids, and Lie algebroids.

We begin by extending the work of Loizides and Meinrenken in
[
LM23
]
on weighted manifolds. We define weighted submanifolds, weighted immersions, and weighted embeddings, and prove normal form theorems for these objects. We also study characterizations of weighted morphisms in terms of their graphs and in terms of weighted paths. We further extend the theory of weighted manifolds by developing a theory of linear weightings for vector bundles. Our work on linear weightings is the content of the pre-print
[
Hud23
]
.

Following this, we give three equivalent definitions of a multiplicative weighting for a Lie groupoid
G\rightrightarrows M
: one involving the structure maps for the Lie groupoid, one involving the graph of the groupoid multiplication, and one involving the weighted deformation space. We also include a discussion of weighted VB-groupoids, and prove some basic theorems involving these objects.

In the last two chapters of this thesis, we study infinitesimally multiplicative weightings for Lie algebroids. We characterize these in terms of linear Poisson structures and homological vector fields. We show that multiplicative weightings differentiate to infinitesimally multiplicative weightings and solve the integration problem for infinitesimally multiplicative weightings along wide Lie subalgebroids. In particular, we classify multiplicative weightings of a Lie groupoid along its units in terms of Lie filtrations of its Lie algebroid (cf.
[
VEY19
, Definition 67]
). We make progress towards a solution for the general integration problem by giving a condition for when an infinitesimally multiplicative weighting along a general Lie subalgebroid integrates.

†
†
margin:

1

Introduction

The purpose of this thesis is to give a detailed and thorough study of the theory of
weightings
in the categories of manifolds, vector bundles, Lie groupoids, and Lie algebroids, with the goal of unifying, clarify, and generalizing constructions of Connes
[
Con94
]
, Debord and Skandalis
[
DS14
]
, Higson and Yi
[
HY19
]
, Haj and Higson
[
SH18
]
, van Erp and Yuncken
[
VEY17
]
, and Ševera
[
Šev17
]
.

1
Background

1.1
Connes’ tangent groupoid

The story of weightings can be said to have started with the so-called
tangent groupoid
introduced by Connes in his book
[
Con94
]
. Given a smooth manifold
M
, the tangent groupoid
\mathbb{T}M
is defined as a set to be the disjoint union

\mathbb{T}M=TM\sqcup(M\times M\times\mathbb{R}^{\times}).

The topology on
\mathbb{T}M
is specified by declaring that
M\times M\times\mathbb{R}^{\times}
is an open set, and a sequence
(x_{n},y_{n},t_{n})\in M\times M\times\mathbb{R}^{\times}
with
t_{n}\to 0
converges to a tangent vector
X_{p}\in T_{p}M
if and only if

x_{n}\to p,\quad y_{n}\to p,\quad\frac{x_{n}-y_{n}}{t_{n}}\to X_{p},

where the last term is understood to take place in a local coordinate system. This admits the structure of a Lie groupoid
\mathbb{T}M\rightrightarrows M\times\mathbb{R}
. Alternatively, it can be understood as a family of Lie groupoids
\mathbb{T}M_{t}\rightrightarrows M
parameterized
t\in\mathbb{R}
, where

\mathbb{T}M_{t}=\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}\mathrm{Pair}(M)&t\neq 0\\
TM&t=0.\end{array}\right.

Connes uses this construction together with K-theory for groupoid
C^{*}
-algebras to give a geometric reformulation of the analytic index map of Atiyah and Singer and a proof of the Atiyah-Singer index theorem (see
[
Hig23
]
for a more thorough account of Connes’ argument).

1.2
The groupoid approach to pseudodifferential operators

There is a smooth action
\alpha:\mathbb{R}^{\times}\times\mathbb{T}M\to\mathbb{T}M
(the
zoom action
) defined on the open set
M\times M\times\mathbb{R}^{\times}
by

\alpha_{\lambda}(p,q,t)=(p,q,\lambda^{-1}t)

and on
TM
by scalar multiplication. Debord and Skandalis
[
DS14
]
observed that any order
k
differential operator
D
on
M
extends to a family of differential operators
\mathbb{D}_{t}
on
\mathbb{T}M
which is homogeneous of degree
k
, as follows. Suppose that in local coordinates
x_{a}
on
M
one has

D=\sum_{|\alpha|\leq k}a_{\alpha}(x)\partial^{\alpha}.

Let
D_{0}
be the differential operator on
TM
which, for
p\in M
, acts on the fibre
T_{p}M
as the constant coefficient differential operator

D_{0,p}=\sum_{|\alpha|=k}a_{\alpha}(p)\partial^{\alpha}.

Then
\mathbb{D}_{t}
is given by

\mathbb{D}_{t}=\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}t^{k}D&t\neq 0\\
D_{0}&t=0,\end{array}\right.

where
t^{k}D
acts on the first component of
\mathbb{T}M_{t}=M\times M\times\{t\}
. More generally, if
D
is a pseudodifferential operator on
M
of order
k
with Schwartz kernel
d\in\mathcal{D}^{\prime}(M\times M)
, then
d
extends to a distribution
\mathbb{D}
on
\mathbb{T}M
such that
\alpha_{\lambda}^{*}\mathbb{D}-\lambda^{k}\mathbb{D}
is a smooth density. It was then shown by van Erp and Yuncken that this property
characterizes
the pseudodifferential operators on
M
(
[
VEY19
, Theorem 2]
).

An attractive feature of this result is that it allows for a coordinate free definition of pseudodifferential operators. In particular, it provides an avenue for defining pseudodifferential operators and their principal symbols in situations where the Fourier transform is unavailable. The setting in which van Erp and Yuncken apply this principle is that of
filtered
(or
Carnot
) manifolds, first considered by Melin
[
Mel82
]
. A filtered manifold is a manifold
M
together with a filtration

TM=F_{-r}\supseteq F_{-r+1}\supseteq\cdots\supseteq F_{-1}\supseteq 0

of
TM
by subbundles
F_{-i}
such that

[\Gamma(F_{-i}),\Gamma(F_{-j})]\subseteq\Gamma(F_{-i-j}).

The Lie bracket condition ensures that the graded vector bundle

\mathfrak{t}_{F}M=\bigoplus_{i=0}^{r}F_{-i}/F_{-i+1}\to M

has the structure of a family of nilpotent Lie algebras. The corresponding family
T_{F}M=\exp(\mathfrak{t}_{F}M)
of simply connected nilpotent Lie groups fits into a smooth family of Lie groupoids

\mathbb{T}_{F}M=T_{F}M\sqcup(\mathrm{Pair}(M)\times\mathbb{R}^{\times})

generalizing Connes’ tangent groupoid (
[
CP15
,
VEY17
,
SH18
]
). The action of
\mathbb{R}^{\times}
on
\mathfrak{t}_{F}M
defined by

\alpha_{\lambda}(\xi)=\lambda^{i}\xi,\quad\text{for }\xi\in F_{-i}/F_{-i+1},

is by Lie algebroid automorphisms, hence integrates to an action on
T_{F}G
by Lie groupoid automorphisms, and this extends to a smooth action on
\mathbb{T}_{F}M
by Lie groupoid automorphisms; this is the zoom action on
\mathbb{T}_{F}M
. Using their characterization of pseudodifferential operators, van Erp and Yuncken then
define
the
F
-pseudodifferential calculus for a filtered manifold
M
by replacing
\mathbb{T}M
with
\mathbb{T}_{F}M
.

1.3
Euler-like vector fields and deformation to the normal cone

Around the same time as van Erp and Yuncken’s work on pseudodifferential operators, Bischoff, Bursztyn, Lima, and Meinrenken (
[
BLM19
,
Mei21
,
BBLM20
]
) had discovered that many linearization and normal form results could be deduced from the fact that a specific class of vector fields, called
Euler-like vector fields
, could be linearized.

Recall that the Euler-vector field
E
on a vector bundle
V\to M
is the vector field whose flow is given by scalar multiplication by
e^{t}
; if
x_{a},p_{b}
are local vector bundle coordinates for
V
then

E=\sum_{b}p_{b}\frac{\partial}{\partial p_{b}}.

A vector field
X\in\mathfrak{X}(M)
is called
Euler-like
with respect to a submanifold
N\subseteq M
if it vanishes along
N
and the induced vector field
\nu(X)
on the normal bundle
\nu(M,N)
is equal to the Euler vector field; if
x_{a},y_{b}
are local coordinates on
M
such that
N
is cut out by setting
y_{b}=0
, then
X\in\mathfrak{X}(M)
is Euler-like with respect to
N
if and only if

X=\sum_{a}f_{a}(x,y)\frac{\partial}{\partial x_{a}}+\sum_{b}(y_{b}+g_{b}(x,y))\frac{\partial}{\partial y_{b}}

where
f_{a}
vanishes along
N
and
g_{b}
vanishes to order 2 along
N
. The linearization theorem of Bursztyn, Lima, and Meinrenken (
[
BLM19
, Proposition 2.7]
) is that there is a 1-1 correspondence between (germs of) tubular neighbourhoods of
N
in
M
and vector fields on
M
which are Euler-like with respect to
N
. The connection between the linearization theorem of Bursztyn, Lima, and Meinrenken and Connes’ tangent groupoid was established by Haj and Higson (
[
SH18
]
), who use
\mathbb{T}M
to give a beautiful geometric explanation of this result.

1.4
Weightings

More generally, Haj and Higson also considered filtered manifolds. If
M
is a filtered manifold, then they define (cf.
[
SH18
, Definition 7.1]
)
filtered submanifolds
to be submanifolds
N\subseteq M
with the property that the intersections
TN\cap(F_{-i})|_{N}
are vector subbundles of
TM|_{N}
. Haj and Higson construct a smooth deformation to the normal cone is this setting, and explain how this construction generalizes the aforementioned
F
-tangent groupoid (
[
SH18
, Section 9]
). Motivated by this work, Loizides and Meinrenken (cf.
[
LM23
]
) determined what additional structure was needed along a submanifold to define these “exotic” deformation spaces. To this end, they introduced the concept of a
weighting
(cf.
[
LM23
, Definition 2.2]
); a similar definition was given by Melrose in
[
Mel96
]
under the name of a “quasi-homogeneous structure".

A weighting of a manifold
M
along a closed, embedded submanifold
N
in defined terms of a multiplicative filtration

C^{\infty}_{M}=C^{\infty}_{M,(0)}\supseteq C^{\infty}_{M,(1)}\supseteq C^{\infty}_{M,(2)}\supseteq\cdots

(1)

of the sheaf of smooth functions on
M
such that
C^{\infty}_{M,(1)}=\mathcal{I}_{N}
is the vanishing ideal of
N
(see
Definition 2.1
). Given a weighting of
M
along
N
, Loizides and Meinrenken define a fibre bundle

\nu_{\mathcal{W}}(M,N)\to N,

called the
weighted normal bundle
(
Definition 2.39
), generalizing the normal bundle of
M
in
N
. Additionally, they explain how the weighted normal bundle fits into a
weighted deformation space

\delta_{\mathcal{W}}(M,N)=\nu_{\mathcal{W}}(M,N)\sqcup(M\times\mathbb{R}^{\times})

(
Definition 2.46
). Loizides and Meinrenken explain in
[
LM22
]
how their constructions generalize the work of Haj and Higson, by showing that if
M
is a filtered manifold then
M\times M
has a canonical weighting along the diagonal and with respect to this one has
\delta_{\mathcal{W}}(M\times M,M)=\mathbb{T}_{F}M
.

2
Overview and Summary of Results

A key feature of Connes’ construction is that the tangent groupoid is, as the name suggests, a
groupoid
. Therefore a motivating question for this work is when a weighting of a Lie groupoid
G
along a subgroupoid
H
is compatible with the Lie groupoid structure in such a way that the groupoid structure of
G\times\mathbb{R}^{\times}
extends to
\delta_{\mathcal{W}}(G,H)
.

This question turns out to be more subtle than one might expect. Recall (or see
Section 14
) that the data of a Lie groupoid
G\rightrightarrows M
includes the submanifold
M\subseteq G
of units, two surjective submersions
s,t:G\to M
, a partially defined multiplication
\mathrm{mult}:G\times_{M}G\to G
, and an inversion map
G\to G
. Therefore, the reasonable first attempt at a definition of a “multiplicative weighting” would be to simply add the adjective “weighted” in front of everything. However, this already begs the question of what exactly one means by this: what is a weighted submanifold? What is a weighted submersion? In order to ask that multiplication be a weighted morphism, we need the fibre product
G\times_{M}G
to be weighted - is this automatic? Once the notion of weighted submanifold is understood, another reasonable definition of multiplicative weighting would be the requirement that the graph of the groupoid multiplication be a weighted submanifold of
G^{3}
. Do these two approaches amount to the same thing?

2.1
Overview of Chapter 2

This question thus demands a careful investigation into the weighted analogs of the basic notions of differential geometry, and to this we devote
Chapter 2
. We begin in
Section 3
by reviewing the basics of weightings, as described in
[
LM23
]
. In particular, we recall how a weighting of
M
along
N
defines a filtration

TM|_{N}=(TM|_{N})_{(-r)}\supseteq\cdots\supseteq(TM|_{N})_{(-1)}\supseteq(TM|_{N})_{(0)}=TN

(2)

by subbundles. In
Section 4
, we define the notion of weighted submanifolds, give several examples, and explain how weighted submanifolds become weighted manifolds in their own right.

A weighted morphism is a smooth map between weighted manifolds whose pull-back respects the filtrations of smooth functions (
1
). We devote
Section 5
to an in depth study of weighted morphisms, where we define weighted embeddings (
Definition 2.14
) and weighted submersions (
Definition 2.20
) using the filtration (
2
). The weighted analogues of the classical normal form theorems for these maps are the content of
Theorem 2.16
and
Theorem 2.22
.

It is not always easy to check whether or not a map is a weighted morphism, and so an important result in this chapter is the following.

Theorem

(=
Theorem 2.32
)
.

Suppose that
(M,N)
and
(M^{\prime},N^{\prime})
are weighted pairs and
F:(M,N)\to(M^{\prime},N^{\prime})
is a smooth map of pairs. Then
F
is a weighted morphism if and only if

(a)

the graph
\Gamma(F)\subseteq M^{\prime}\times M
is a weighted submanifold and

(b)

the tangent map
TF:TM|_{N}\to TM^{\prime}|_{N^{\prime}}
is filtration preserving.

This theorem combined with our work on weighted embeddings is a valuable tool for our work on multiplicative weightings. We also include another characterization of weighted morphisms, this time using
weighted paths
(
Definition 2.34
), which is based on valuable discussions with Beiner, Loizides, and Meinrenken.

Theorem

(=
Theorem 2.35
)
.

(a)

We have
f\in C^{\infty}(M)_{(i)}
if and only if

f(\gamma(t))=O(t^{i})

for every weighted path
\gamma:\mathbb{R}\to M
.

(b)

A smooth map
F:(M,N)\to(M^{\prime},N^{\prime})
between weighted pairs is a weighted morphism if and only if it takes weighted paths to weighted paths.

In
Section 6
and
Section 7
, we review the aforementioned weighted normal bundle and weighted deformation space and their functorial properties. In particular, we show that our definitions of weighted immersions and weighted submersions are exactly the correct ones:

Theorem

(=
Theorem 2.49
)
.

Suppose that
F:(M,N)\to(M^{\prime},N^{\prime})
is a weighted morphism. Then

(a)

F
is a weighted immersion if and only if
\delta_{\mathcal{W}}(F):\delta_{\mathcal{W}}(M,N)\to\delta_{\mathcal{W}}(M^{\prime},N^{\prime})
is an immersion.

(b)

F
is a weighted submersion if and only if
\delta_{\mathcal{W}}(F):\delta_{\mathcal{W}}(M,N)\to\delta_{\mathcal{W}}(M^{\prime},N^{\prime})
is a submersion.

This chapter ends with
Section 8
, which is a review of singular Lie filtrations; these constitute an important method of constructing weightings.

2.2
Overview of Chapter 3

With a more thorough understanding of the differential geometry of weightings, we turn our attention to linear weightings for vector bundles. In addition to Connes’ tangent groupoid and the
F
-tangent bundle of Choi-Ponge and van Erp-Yuncken, another example of a weighted deformation space appearing in the literature is the
re-scaled spinor bundle
of Higson and Yi
[
HY19
]
, which we now recall. Suppose that
M
is a Riemannian spin manifold with spinor bundle
S\to M
. In order to understand Getzler’s approach (
[
BGV03
,
Get83
]
) to the index theorem from the perspective Connes’ tangent groupoid, Higson and Yi introduce their rescaled spinor bundle
\mathbb{S}
, which is a vector bundle over the tangent groupoid
\mathbb{T}M=TM\sqcup(M\times M\times\mathbb{R}^{\times})
. It can be understood as a family of vector bundles parameterized by
\mathbb{R}
, given by

\mathbb{S}_{t}=\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}S\boxtimes S^{*}&t\neq 0\\
\pi^{*}(\wedge^{\bullet}TM)&t=0,\end{array}\right.

where
\pi:TM\to M
is the vector bundle projection and
S\boxtimes S^{*}
is the vector bundle over
M\times M
with fibre
(S\boxtimes S^{*})_{(m_{1},m_{2})}=S_{m_{1}}\otimes S^{*}_{m_{2}}
.

We unify the constructions of the rescaled spinor bundle
\mathbb{S}
and the weighted deformation space
\delta_{\mathcal{W}}(M,N)
by introducing
linear weightings
. If
V\to M
is a vector bundle over a weighted manifold, then a linear weighting of
V
is defined in terms of a
\mathbb{Z}
-graded filtration

\cdots\supseteq\Gamma_{V,(i)}\supseteq\Gamma_{V,(i+1)}\supseteq\cdots

(3)

of the sheaf of sections of
V
which is compatible with the filtration of
C^{\infty}_{M}
, see
Definition 3.1
. In particular, we allow the filtration (
3
) to be non-trivial in negative degree, in contrast with (
1
); this was originally suggested by Beiner
[
Bei22
]
.

We give the basic definitions, examples, and constructions of linear weightings in
Section 10
. A specific result we obtain (
Theorem 3.8
) says that linear weightings may equivalently be defined in terms of a multiplicative filtration

\cdots\supseteq C^{\infty}_{pol,V,(i)}\supseteq C^{\infty}_{pol,V,(i+1)}\supseteq\cdots,

where
C^{\infty}_{pol,V}
denotes the sheaf of fiber-wise polynomial functions on
V
. We use this result in
Section 11
and
Section 12
to extend the definition of the weighted normal bundle and weighted deformation space to linear weightings. This gives
vector bundles

\nu_{\mathcal{W}}(V)\to\nu_{\mathcal{W}}(M,N)\quad\text{and}\quad\delta_{\mathcal{W}}(V)\to\delta_{\mathcal{W}}(M,N)

such that
\delta_{\mathcal{W}}(V)
can be understood as a family of vector bundles

\textstyle{\delta_{\mathcal{W}}(V)=\nu_{\mathcal{W}}(V)\sqcup(V\times\mathbb{R}^{\times})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\delta_{\mathcal{W}}(M,N)=\nu_{\mathcal{W}}(M,N)\sqcup(M\times\mathbb{R}^{\times});}

see
Theorem 3.21
and
Theorem 3.34
. We also give the following explicit description of the sections of
\nu_{\mathcal{W}}(V)\to\nu_{\mathcal{W}}(M,N)
and
\delta_{\mathcal{W}}(V)\to\delta_{\mathcal{W}}(M,N)
.

Theorem

(=
Theorem 3.29
and
Theorem 3.40
)
.

If
V\to M
is a linearly weighted vector bundle then

(a)

\Gamma(\nu_{\mathcal{W}}(V))=C^{\infty}(\nu_{\mathcal{W}}(M,N))\otimes_{\mathrm{gr}(C^{\infty}(M))}\mathrm{gr}(\Gamma(V))
and

(b)

\Gamma(\delta_{\mathcal{W}}(V))=C^{\infty}(\delta_{\mathcal{W}}(M,N))\otimes_{\mathrm{Rees}(C^{\infty}(M))}\mathrm{Rees}(\Gamma(V))
.

In
Section 13
how our construction captures the rescaled spinor bundle of Higson and Yi (
[
HY19
]
), as well as a related construction by Ševera in his letters to Weinstein (
[
Šev17
]
).

2.3
Overview of Chapter 4

In
Chapter 4
we return to the issue of multiplicative weightings for Lie groupoids. We begin by reviewing the basics of Lie groupoids, including VB-groupoids. After this, equipped with correct definitions of weighted submanifolds and weighted submersions, we give the following definition:

Definition

(=
Definition 4.8
)
.

A weighting of
G
along
H\subseteq G
is said to be
multiplicative
if

(a)

The units
M\subseteq G
are a weighted submanifold,

(b)

the source map is a weighted submersion,

(c)

multiplication
m:G^{(2)}\to G
is a weighted morphism, and

(d)

inversion is a weighted morphism.

We devote
Section 15
to exploring this definition and examples thereof. We prove, in particular, that it is
not
enough to simply ask that the graph of multiplication be weighted.

Theorem

(=
Theorem 4.12
)
.

A weighting of
G\rightrightarrows M
along
H\subseteq G
is multiplicative if and only if

(a)

M
is a weighted submanifold,

(b)

the graph of multiplication is a weighted submanifold, and

(c)

the filtration of
TG|_{H}
is by subgroupoids

(TG|_{H})_{(i)}\rightrightarrows(TM|_{N})_{(i)}

This characterization of multiplicative weightings is particularly useful when applied to VB-groupoids, which we shall use for the problem of differentiating multiplicative weightings (
Theorem 5.20
). We close this chapter by showing that our definition of multiplicative weightings is exactly the right one, by proving the following theorem:

Theorem

(=
Theorem 4.22
)
.

A weighting of a Lie groupoid
G
along
H
is multiplicative if and only if the groupoid structure
G\times\mathbb{R}^{\times}\to M\times\mathbb{R}^{\times}
extends to

\delta_{\mathcal{W}}(G,H)\rightrightarrows\delta_{\mathcal{W}}(M,N).

2.4
Overview of Chapter 5

Chapter 5
is devoted to the study of the infinitesimal analogue of multiplicative weightings, called
infinitesimally multiplicative weightings
. We being with a review of the basics of Lie algebroids, including the Lie functor from Lie groupoids to Lie algebroids, as well as the descriptions of Lie algebroids as linear Poisson manifolds and 1-shifted vector bundles equipped with a homological vector field.

In
Section 18
we give the following definition:

Definition

(=
Definition 5.11
)
.

An
infinitesimally multiplicative weighting
of
A\Rightarrow M
is a linear weighting of
A
with the additional properties that

(a)

the anchor
a:A\to TM
is a weighted morphism, and

(b)

for all
\sigma\in\Gamma(A)_{(i)}
and
\tau\in\Gamma(A)_{(j)}
, we have

[\sigma,\tau]\in\Gamma(A)_{(i+j)}.

In
Section 19
we prove the following alternative characterizations of infinitesimally multiplicative weightings.

Theorem

(=
Theorem 5.15
)
.

Let
A\Rightarrow M
be a Lie algebroid endowed with a linear weighting. Then the following are equivalent.

(a)

A
is a weighted Lie algebroid,

(b)

the Poisson bivector field
\pi\in\mathfrak{X}^{2}(A^{*})
has filtration degree zero,

(c)

the differential
\mathrm{d}_{A}:\Gamma(\wedge A^{*})\to\Gamma(\wedge A^{*})
is filtration preserving.

We use this, as well as the theory of weighted VB-groupoids, in
Section 21
to prove that multiplicative weightings can be differentiated to infinitesimally multiplicative weightings.

Theorem

(=
Theorem 5.20
)
.

Let
G\rightrightarrows M
be a weighted Lie groupoid with Lie algebroid
\mathrm{Lie}(G)\Rightarrow M
. Then

\Gamma(\mathrm{Lie}(G)|_{U})_{(i)}=\{\sigma\in\Gamma(\mathrm{Lie}(G)|_{U}):\sigma^{L}\in\mathfrak{X}^{L}(G|_{U})_{(i)}\}

(4)

defines an infinitesimally multiplicative weighting of
\mathrm{Lie}(G)
such that

\mathrm{Lie}(\nu_{\mathcal{W}}(G,H))=\nu_{\mathcal{W}}(\mathrm{Lie}(G))\quad\text{and}\quad\mathrm{Lie}(\delta_{\mathcal{W}}(G,H))=\delta_{\mathcal{W}}(\mathrm{Lie}(G)).

In
Section 22
we prove the following partial converse to the previous theorem.

Theorem

(=
Theorem 5.23
)
.

Suppose that
G\rightrightarrows M
is a Lie groupoid and

A=A_{-r}\supseteq A_{-r+1}\supseteq\cdots A_{-1}\supseteq 0

(5)

is a Lie filtration of
A=\mathrm{Lie}(G)
. Suppose that
H\subseteq G
is a wide,
s
-connected Lie subgroupoid. If
B=\mathrm{Lie}(H)
is such that

(a)

[\Gamma(B),\Gamma(A_{-i})]\subseteq\Gamma(A_{-i})
for all
i

(b)

the assignment
m\mapsto\dim(B_{m}+A_{-i}|_{m})
is constant as a function on
M

then (
5
) defines a multiplicative weighting of
G
along
H
such that the induced weighting of
A
defined by
Theorem 5.20
is given by the filtration

A=A_{-r}+B\supseteq A_{-r+1}+B\supseteq\cdots A_{-1}+B\supseteq B.

Using this, we show that a filtered Lie groupoid (cf.
[
VEY19
, Definition 67]
) is the same thing as a Lie groupoid with a multiplicative weighting along its objects (
Theorem 5.28
).

2.5
Overview of Chapter 6

In
Chapter 6
we use a description of weightings as graded subbundles of the higher jet bundles to prove another partial converse the differentiation theorem,
Theorem 5.20
.

We begin by reviewing the
r
-th order tangent bundle
T_{r}M\to M
, and review Loizides and Meinrenken’s work (
[
LM23
, Section 7]
relating weightings of
M
along
N
and graded subbundles
Q\subseteq T_{r}M
over
N
. We characterize multiplicative and infinitesimally multiplicative weightings in terms of the graded bundle
Q
.

Theorem

(=
Theorem 6.12
and
Theorem 6.15
)
.

(a)

Let
G\rightrightarrows M
be weighted along
H\subseteq G
. The weighting is multiplicative if and only if the graded subbundle
Q_{G}\subseteq T_{r}G
is a Lie subgroupoid
Q_{G}\rightrightarrows Q_{M}
of
T_{r}G\rightrightarrows T_{r}M
.

(b)

Let
A\Rightarrow M
be linearly weighted along
B\Rightarrow N
. The weighting is infinitesimally multiplicative if and only if the corresponding graded subbundle
Q_{A}\subseteq T_{r}A
is a Lie subalgebroid
Q_{A}\Rightarrow Q_{M}
of
T_{r}A\Rightarrow T_{r}M
.

Taking advantage of this characterization of multiplicative and infinitesimally multiplicative weightings, we show that the spray exponential (
[
CMS20
, Definition 3.20]
) corresponding to any Lie algebroid spray of filtration degree zero is a (partially defined) weighted morphism. The main result of
Chapter 6
is following theorem.

Theorem

(=
Theorem 6.23
)
.

Suppose that
G\rightrightarrows M
is a
s
-connected Lie groupoid and
H\rightrightarrows N
is a
s
-connected Lie subgroupoid with Lie algebroids
A=\mathrm{Lie}(G)
and
B=\mathrm{Lie}(H)
, respectively, and suppose that
A
is infinitesimally multiplicatively weighted along
B
. Let
Q_{A}\Rightarrow Q_{M}
be the graded subbundle of
T_{r}A\Rightarrow T_{r}M
corresponding to the weighting of
A
along
B
. If
Q_{A}
integrates to an
s
-connected subgroupoid
Q_{G}\rightrightarrows Q_{M}
of
T_{r}G\rightrightarrows T_{r}M
, then
Q_{G}
is a graded subbundle of
T_{r}G\rightrightarrows T_{r}M
which defines a multiplicative weighting of
G
along
H
.

2.6
Overview of Chapter 7

We end this thesis with some possible future directions we would like to pursue. First, we propose a definition of
linear singular Lie filtrations
(
Definition 7.1
) and put forward the problem of determining when a linear singular Lie filtration defines a linear weighting, extending the work of Loizides and Meinrenken on singular Lie filtrations (
[
LM22
]
) to vector bundles.

Next, we briefly review
associative algebroids
in the sense of Ševera (
[
Šev17
, Letter 6]
) and present the task of studying deformation spaces in this setting, with a possible application the work of van Erp and Yuncken to pseudodifferential operators acting on sections of vector bundles.

Following this, we summarily explain
k
-multifiltered manifolds and multiweightings. We ask for a connection between the two in a way that generalizes
Theorem 5.23
. In particular, we make a conjecture that the right object for the "tangent groupoid" for a 2-multifiltered manifold should be a double groupoid of the form

\textstyle{\delta_{\mathcal{W}}(\mathrm{Pair}^{2}(M),\Delta^{h},\Delta^{v})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\mathbb{T}_{F^{h}}M\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\mathbb{T}_{F^{v}}M\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{M\times\mathbb{R},}

where
F^{v}
and
F^{h}
are Lie filtrations of
M
coming from the 2-multifiltration. We conclude by asking what type of
C^{*}
-algebraic information could be obtained from such an object.

†
†
margin:

2

The Differential Geometry of Weightings

Weightings were first introduced by Melrose under the name of “quasi-homogeneous structures" in order to do analysis on manifolds with corners (
[
Mel96
]
). Independently, they were later considered by Loizides and Meinrenken (
[
LM23
]
) in order to understand the local information necessary to construct the exotic deformation spaces appearing in the work of Haj and Higson on linearization theorems for Euler-like vector fields with weights (
[
SH18
]
). Our exposition follows the work by Loizides and Meinrenken.

3
Basics of Weightings

Let
M
be a smooth manifold and
N\subseteq M
an embedded submanifold. Associated to
N
is a filtration of the sheaf of smooth functions
C^{\infty}_{M}
by ideals

C^{\infty}_{M}\supseteq\mathcal{I}_{N}\supseteq\mathcal{I}_{N}^{2}\supseteq\cdots

where

\mathcal{I}_{N}(U)=\{f\in C^{\infty}(U):f|_{U\cap N}=0\};

that is,
\mathcal{I}_{N}^{k}
is the subsheaf of smooth functions on
M
vanishing to order
k
along
N
. This filtration has the following special property: if
x_{1},\dots,x_{m}
is a coordinate system defined on
U\subseteq M
such that

N\cap U=\{x_{1}=\cdots=x_{n}=0\}

then
\mathcal{I}_{N}^{k}(U)
is the ideal generated by the monomials

x_{1}^{s_{1}}\cdots x_{n}^{s_{n}}\quad\text{where}\quad\sum s_{i}\geq k.

(6)

A
weighting
of a manifold
M
along a submanifold
N
is a modified notion of order of vanishing along
N
. More specifically, a weighting of
M
along
N
multiplicative filtration of the sheaf smooth functions on
M
satisfying a local property generalizing (
6
). We recall their definition now.

3.1
Definition of a Weighting

A
weight vector

w=(w_{1},\dots,w_{m})\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}^{m}
is an
m
-tuple of non-negative integers. Any integer
r\in\mathbb{Z}
satisfying
r\geq\max_{i}\{w_{i}\}
will be called the
order
of the weight vector. Given an open subset
U\subseteq R^{m}
, let
C^{\infty}(U)_{(i)}\subseteq C^{\infty}(U)
be the ideal generated by the monomials

x_{1}^{s_{1}}\cdots x_{m}^{s_{m}}\quad\text{where}\quad\sum_{a}w_{a}s_{a}\geq i.

This defines a filtration

C^{\infty}(U)=C^{\infty}(U)_{(0)}\supseteq C^{\infty}(U)_{(1)}\supseteq C^{\infty}(U)_{(2)}\supseteq\cdots

(7)

Definition 2.1

(
[
LM23
, Definition 2.2]
)
.

A
weighting
of
M
is a multiplicative filtration

C^{\infty}_{M}=C^{\infty}_{M,(0)}\supseteq C^{\infty}_{M,(1)}\supseteq C^{\infty}_{M,(2)}\supseteq\cdots

(8)

of the sheaf of smooth functions on
M
with the property that each point
p\in M
has an open neighbourhood
U\subseteq M
with coordinates
x_{1},\dots,x_{n}
defined on
U
so that the filtration of
C^{\infty}_{M}(U)=C^{\infty}(U)
is given by (
7
).

The coordinates
x_{a}
will be called
weighted coordinates
. Given a weighting of
M
, let
N\subseteq M
denote the set of points for which the filtration (
8
) is non-trivial. Then
[
LM23
, Lemma 2.4]
implies that
N
is a closed submanifold and
C^{\infty}_{M,(1)}=\mathcal{I}_{N}
. If
N
is given in advance, then we will say that
M
is
weighted along

N
and refer to
(M,N)
as a
weighted manifold pair
. We note that, by definition, weighted coordinates serve as submanifold coordinates for
N
.

If
(M,N)
and
(M^{\prime},N^{\prime})
are weighted pairs, a
weighted morphism
from
(M,N)
to
(M^{\prime},N^{\prime})
is a smooth map
F:M\to M^{\prime}
such that

F^{*}C^{\infty}_{M^{\prime},(i)}\subseteq C^{\infty}_{M,(i)}

for all
i\geq 0
.

Remark 2.2
.

In this thesis we will work in the
C^{\infty}
-category unless explicitly stated otherwise. Therefore we may take advantage of the existence of partitions of unity to avoid the use of sheaves, working instead with the filtration of global functions

C^{\infty}(M)=C^{\infty}(M)_{(0)}\supseteq C^{\infty}(M)_{(1)}\supseteq C^{\infty}(M)_{(2)}\supseteq\cdots.

Basic Examples

We now give several examples of weightings.

Examples 2.3
.

(a)

As already discussed, if
N
is a closed submanifold of
M
then order of vanishing defines a weighting of
M
along
N
:

C^{\infty}_{M}\supseteq\mathcal{I}_{N}\supseteq\mathcal{I}_{N}^{2}\supseteq\cdots.

We refer to this weighting as the
trivial weighting
of
M
along
N
. There are two extreme cases we want to point out:
N=M
and
N=\emptyset
. In these cases, the trivial weighting is given by

C^{\infty}(M)\supseteq 0\supseteq 0\supseteq\cdots,

and

C^{\infty}(M)\supseteq C^{\infty}(M)\supseteq C^{\infty}(M)\supseteq\cdots,

respectively.

(b)

Define a weighting of
\mathbb{R}^{2}
along the origin by declaring that
x
has weight 1 and
y
has weight 3. The resulting filtration is given by

C^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{2})\supseteq\langle x,y\rangle\supseteq\langle x^{2},y\rangle\supseteq\langle x^{3},y\rangle\supseteq\langle x^{4},xy,y^{2}\rangle\supseteq\cdots.

As simple as it is, this example will shed a considerable amount of light on some of the concepts later in this chapter.

(c)

[
LM23
, Examples 2.9 (c)]
A nested sequence

M=N_{r+1}\supseteq N_{r}\supseteq\cdots\supseteq N_{1}\supseteq N_{0}=N

of embedded submanifolds
N_{i}
(with
N
closed) defines a weighting of order
r
by

C^{\infty}_{M,(i)}=\sum_{k\geq 0}\sum_{i_{1}+\cdots+i_{k}=i}\mathcal{I}_{N_{i_{1}}}\cdots\mathcal{I}_{N_{i_{k}}}.

Weighted coordinates near point
p\in N
are given by coordinates
x_{a}
which are submanifold coordinates for each of the
N_{i}
.

(d)

[
LM23
, Secton 2.2]
If
(M,N)
and
(M^{\prime},N^{\prime})
, then
M\times M^{\prime}
is naturally weighted along
N\times N^{\prime}
by letting
C^{\infty}(M\times M^{\prime})_{(i)}
be the ideal generated by

\sum_{i_{1}+i_{2}=i}C^{\infty}(M)_{(i_{1})}\otimes C^{\infty}(M)_{(i_{2})}.

If
x_{a}
are local weighted coordinates for
M
and
y_{b}
are local weighted coordinates for
M^{\prime}
, then
x_{a},y_{b}
for a local weighted coordinate system for
M\times M^{\prime}
.

3.2
Filtration of the Tangent and Cotangent Bundles

It was observed (
[
LM23
, Proposition 2.6]
) the a weighting of
M
along
N
determines a filtration

\nu(M,N)=F_{-r}\supseteq F_{-r+1}\supseteq\cdots\supseteq F_{0}=0

(9)

of the normal bundle of
N
in
M
by subbundles
F_{-i}\to N
. This filtration is induced by a filtration of
TM|_{N}
, which is in turn defined by “dualizing” a filtration of
T^{*}M|_{N}
. Through our study of weighted manifolds, we found it to be fruitful to consider the filtrations of
TM|_{N}
and
T^{*}M|_{N}
in their own right. We recall their constructions now.

Let

(T^{*}M|_{N})_{(i)}=\mathrm{span}_{C^{\infty}(N)}\{\mathrm{d}f|_{N}:f\in C^{\infty}(M)_{(i)}\}.

If
x_{a}
is a weighted coordinate system defined on
U\subseteq M
, then
\{\mathrm{d}x_{a}|_{U\cap N}:w_{a}\geq i\}
is a frame for
T^{*}M|_{U\cap N}
, hence each
(T^{*}M|_{N})_{(i)}
is a subbundle of
T^{*}M|_{N}
. This defines the filtration

T^{*}M|_{N}=(T^{*}M|_{N})_{(0)}\supseteq(T^{*}M|_{N})_{(1)}\supseteq\cdots\supseteq(T^{*}M|_{N})_{(r)}\supseteq 0.

(10)

We remark, in particular, that
(T^{*}M)_{(1)}=\mathrm{ann}(TN)
and

\mathrm{rank}(\mathrm{gr}(T^{*}M|_{N})_{(i)})=\#\{a:w_{a}=i\},

where

\mathrm{gr}(T^{*}M|_{N})_{(i)}=(T^{*}M|_{N})_{(i)}/(T^{*}M|_{N})_{(i+1)}.

Lemma 2.4
.

Let
(M,N)
be a weighted pair and
p\in N
. Let
x_{1},\dots,x_{k}
be functions of filtration degrees
w_{1},\dots,w_{k}
defined near
p
. Suppose that for all
i
the image of the set
\{\mathrm{d}_{p}x_{a}:w_{a}=i\}
in
\mathrm{gr}(T^{*}_{p}M)_{(i)}
is linearly independent. Then the functions
x_{a}
are contained in a system of weighted coordinates on an open neighbourhood
U
of
p
.

Proof.

Let
y_{a}
be any system of weighted coordinates near
p
. The image of
\{\mathrm{d}_{p}y_{a}:w_{a}=i\}
in
\mathrm{gr}(T^{*}_{p}M)_{(i)}
is a basis for
\mathrm{gr}(T^{*}_{p}M)_{(i)}
, hence by the replacement theorem from linear algebra there is an index set
J_{i}
such that the image of

\{\mathrm{d}_{p}x_{a}:w_{a}=i\}\cup\{\mathrm{d}_{p}y_{a}:w_{a}=i,a\in J_{i}\}

in
\mathrm{gr}(T^{*}_{p}M)_{(i)}
is a basis for
\mathrm{gr}(T^{*}_{p}M)_{(i)}
. Letting
J=\bigcup_{i}J_{i}
it follows that
\{\mathrm{d}_{p}x_{a}\}\cup\{\mathrm{d}_{p}y_{a}:a\in J\}

is a basis for
T_{p}^{*}M
, hence

\{x_{a}\}\cup\{y_{b}:b\in J\}

is the required coordinate system.
∎

The filtration of
TM|_{N}
,

TM|_{N}=(TM|_{N})_{(-r)}\supseteq\cdots\supseteq(TM|_{N})_{(0)}\supseteq 0,

(11)

is the “dual” of (
10
), given by

(TM|_{N})_{(-i)}=\mathrm{ann}((T^{*}M|_{N})_{(i+1)}).

In a local weighted coordinate system
x_{a}
defined near
p\in N
we have

(T_{p}M)_{(-i)}=\mathrm{span}\left\{\frac{\partial}{\partial x_{a}}\bigg|_{p}:w_{a}\leq i\right\};

note in particular that
(TM|_{N})_{(0)}=TN
. As we will see in the forthcoming sections, the tangent bundle for the weighted pair
(M,N)
should be
TM
together with the filtration of
TM|_{N}
.

Examples 2.5
.

(a)

If
M
is given the trivial weighting along
N
, then the filtration (
11
) is just

TM|_{N}\supseteq TN.

(b)

If
\mathbb{R}^{2}
is weighted by declaring that
\mathrm{wt}(x)=1
and
\mathrm{wt}(y)=3
as in
2.3
(b), then the filtration of
T_{0}\mathbb{R}^{2}=\mathbb{R}^{2}
is

\mathbb{R}^{2}\supseteq\{y=0\}\supseteq\{y=0\}\supseteq\{0\}

(c)

If
M
is given the weighting defined by a nested sequence of submanifolds
M=N_{r+1}\supseteq N_{r}\supseteq\cdots\supseteq N_{1}\supseteq N_{0}=N
as in
2.3
(c), the filtration of
TM|_{N}
is given by

TM|_{N}\supseteq TN_{r}|_{N}\supseteq TN_{r-1}|_{N}\supseteq\cdots\supseteq TN.

(d)

If
(M_{i},N_{i})
,
i=1,2
are weighted pairs, then the filtration of
T(M_{1}\times M_{2})=TM_{1}\times TM_{2}
is given by

(T(M_{1}\times M_{2})|_{N_{1}\times N_{2}})_{(i)}=(TM_{1}|_{N_{1}})_{(i)}\times(TM_{2}|_{N_{2}})_{(i)}

4
Weighted Submanifolds

Recall that submanifolds of
M
are subsets which locally look like coordinate subspaces. Modifying this definition by requiring one use weighted coordinates gives the definition of a submanifold in the category of weighted manifolds.

Definition 2.6
.

Let
(M,N)
be a weighted pair. A submanifold
R\subseteq M
is called a
weighted submanifold
if there exists a weighted atlas of submanifold charts. Such a choice of coordinates will be called
weighted submanifold coordinates
.

That is, at each point
p\in N\cap R
there exist local coordinates which are simultaneously submanifold coordinates for
R
and weighted coordinates for
N
.

Examples 2.7
.

(a)

Let
(M,N)
be a weighted pair. If
R\cap N=\emptyset
then
R
is a weighted submanifold of
(M,N)
. Any submanifold
R\subseteq N
is a weighted submanifold.

(b)

Any point
p\in M
is a weighted submanifold.

(c)

Recall submanifolds
N,R\subseteq M
are said to
intersect cleanly
if
N\cap R
is a submanifold and
T(N\cap R)=TN\cap TR
. This is equivalent to
M
admitting an atlas of submanifold charts for
N
and
R
simultaneously (see
[
Hör07
, Proposition C.3.1]
, for instance). Thus, if
M
is trivially weighted along
N
, then the weighted submanifolds are exactly the submanifolds of
M
intersecting
N
cleanly.

(d)

If
(M,N)
is a weighted pair then any submanifold
R
which is transverse to
N
is a weighted submanifold. Indeed, let
p\in R\cap N
. Choosing weighted coordinates
x_{a}
near
p
, then
N
is, by definition, given near
p
by
\{x_{a}=0:w_{a}\geq 1\}
. If
R
is given near
p
by
\{y_{b}=0\}
, then transversality ensures that each
y_{b}\in C^{\infty}(M)_{(0)}
and the image of the set
\{\mathrm{d}_{p}y_{b}\}
in
\mathrm{gr}(T^{*}_{p}M)_{(0)}
is independent. Thus,

\{y_{b}\}\cup\{x_{a}:w_{a}\geq 1\}

can be completed to a weighted coordinate system near
p
by
Lemma 2.4
.

(e)

If
\mathbb{R}^{n}
is weighted by assigning weights to the standard coordinates
x_{a}
, then any linear subspace
V
is a weighted submanifold. To see this, let
e_{i}
be the standard basis for
\mathbb{R}^{n}
. The weighting defines a filtration

\mathbb{R}^{n}=W_{(-r)}\supseteq W_{(-r+1)}\supseteq\cdots\supseteq W_{(0)}\supseteq 0,

where

W_{(-i)}=\mathrm{span}\{e_{a}:w_{a}\leq i\}.

Letting
V_{(-i)}=V\cap W_{(-i)}
, we can inductively find subspaces

V^{\prime}_{(-r)}\supseteq V^{\prime}_{(-r+1)}\supseteq\cdots\supseteq V^{\prime}_{(-1)}\supseteq V^{\prime}_{(0)}\supseteq 0

such that
W_{(-i)}=V_{(-i)}\oplus V^{\prime}_{(-i)}
. Let

\mathcal{B}_{(i)}=\{v_{a}^{(i)}:a=0,\dots,\dim(V_{(-i)}/V_{(-i+1)})\}

be a linearly independent set with the property that
\bigcup_{i=0}^{j}\mathcal{B}_{(i)}
is a basis for
V_{(j)}
; in particular,
\mathcal{B}=\bigcup_{i=0}^{r}\mathcal{B}_{(i)}
is a basis for
V
. Letting
\mathcal{B}^{\prime}
be an analogously constructed basis for
V^{\prime}
, the linear coordinates for
\mathbb{R}^{n}
defined by
\mathcal{B}\cup\mathcal{B}^{\prime}
define weighted submanifold coordinates for
V
.

(f)

Define a weighting of
\mathbb{R}^{2}
along the origin by declaring that
\mathrm{wt}(x)=1
and
\mathrm{wt}(y)=3
, as in
2.3
(b). Then the weighted submanifolds of
(\mathbb{R}^{2},\{0\})
are precisely those which:

(a)

do not pass through the origin

(b)

submanifolds
S
passing through the origin which are not tangent to the x-axis.

(c)

submanifolds
R
passing through the origin with at least third order tangency with the
x
-axis. In particular, the curve
y=x^{2}
is
not
a weighted submanifold.

The first case is obvious. For the second case, by the implicit function theorem we can locally describe
S
as
x=f(y)
for some function
f
with
f(0)=0
. In this case, the weighted submanifold coordinates can be given by
\tilde{x}=x-f(y)
and
\tilde{y}=y
. For the third case, the submanifold
R
is given near the origin by
y=f(x)
with
f(0)=0
and
f^{\prime}(x)=0
(i.e. tangent to the
x
-axis). Suppose that
\tilde{x}
and
\tilde{y}
are weighted coordinates so that the curve
y=f(x)
has the standard form
\tilde{y}=0
. Then

\tilde{y}=a(y-f(x))+O_{f}(4)

where
O_{f}(4)
are terms of filtration order at least 4. However, this has filtration order 3 if and only if only if
f=O(x^{3})
.

Remarks 2.8
.

(a)

Note that
2.7
shows that for a weighted pair
(M,N)
, submanifolds
R\subseteq M
containing
N

need not
be weighted submanifolds.

(b)

A weighted submanifold
R
of a weighted pair
(M,N)
is
not
simply a submanifold with a weighting along
R\cap N
such that the inclusion
R\hookrightarrow M
is a weighted morphism.

For example, consider the weighting of
\mathbb{R}^{2}
defined by declaring
x
to have weight 1 and
y
to have weight 3. If we give the curve
y=x^{2}
the doubled trivial weighting along
\{0\}
then the inclusion into
\mathbb{R}^{2}
is a weighted morphism, but
y=x^{2}
is
not
a weighted submanifold.

Proposition 2.9
.

If
R
is a weighted submanifold of the weighted pair
(M,N)
, then
R
inherits a natural weighting along
R\cap N
. With respect to this weighting, the inclusion
R\hookrightarrow M
is a weighted morphism, and we have moreover that

(TR|_{R\cap N})_{(i)}=TR\cap(TM|_{N})_{(i)}.

(12)

Proof.

Recall that restriction to
R
determines an isomorphism

C^{\infty}(M)/\mathcal{I}_{R}\cong C^{\infty}(R).

Using this, we define

C^{\infty}(R)_{(i)}=C^{\infty}(M)_{(i)}/(C^{\infty}(M)_{(i)}\cap\mathcal{I}_{N});

the restriction of weighted submanifold coordinates to
R
yields weighted coordinates for
R
, hence this defines a weighting. Since the inclusion
R\hookrightarrow M
induces the restriction map
C^{\infty}(M)\to C^{\infty}(R)
, it is clear that it is a weighted morphism.

For the statement about filtration of tangent spaces, let
p\in N\cap R
and let

x_{a},y_{b}
be a weighted submanifold coordinate system defined on
U\subseteq M
containing
p
so that
R\cap U=\{y_{b}=0\}
. As a subbundle of
T^{*}_{p}M
,

\mathrm{ann}((T_{p}R)_{(i)})=\mathrm{span}\{\mathrm{d}_{p}x_{a}:w_{a}\geq i\}+\mathrm{span}\{\mathrm{d}_{p}y_{b}\}.

On the other hand, we have that

\displaystyle\mathrm{ann}(T_{p}R)

\displaystyle=\mathrm{span}\{\mathrm{d}_{p}y_{a}\}\quad\text{and}

\displaystyle T^{*}_{p}M_{(i)}

\displaystyle=\mathrm{span}\{\mathrm{d}_{p}x_{a},\ \mathrm{d}_{p}y_{b}:w_{a}\geq i,w_{b}\geq i\},

hence
\mathrm{ann}((TR|_{R\cap N})_{(i)})=\mathrm{ann}(TR)+(T^{*}M|_{N})_{(i)}
. Therefore,

\displaystyle(TR|_{R\cap N})_{(i)}

\displaystyle=\mathrm{ann}(\mathrm{ann}((TR|_{R\cap N})_{(-i+1)})

\displaystyle=\mathrm{ann}(\mathrm{ann}(TR)+(T^{*}M|_{N})_{(-i+1)})=TR\cap(TM|_{N})_{(i)}.\qed

Proposition 2.10
.

Let
(M,N)
be a weighted pair. Given
p\in M
, any subspace of
T_{p}M
is realized as the tangent space to a weighted submanifold
S
.

Proof.

A choice of weighted coordinates
x_{a}
on
U\subset M
allows us to identify
U
with
T_{p}U=\mathbb{R}^{n}
as weighted manifolds, where
\mathbb{R}^{n}
is weighted as in
2.7
(e), from which the result follows.
∎

We close our discussion with a weighted version of
[
KMS13
, Theorem 1.13]
.

Proposition 2.11
.

Let
(M,N)
be a weighted pair, and suppose that
p:M\to M
is a weighted morphism satisfying
p\circ p=p
. Then
R=p(M)
is a weighted submanifold.

Before proceeding to the proof, we need a lemma from linear algebra.

Lemma 2.12
.

Let
V
be a finite dimensional vector space and
P:V\to V
be a projection. If
\{v_{1},\dots,v_{n}\}
is any basis for
V
, then there exists an index set
\mathcal{I}\subseteq\{1,2,\dots,n\}
with
|\mathcal{I}|=\dim(\mathrm{Im}(P))
such that

\{Pv_{a}:a\in\mathcal{I}\}\cup\{(1-P)v_{b}:b\in\mathcal{I}^{c}\}

is a basis for
V
.

Proof.

For the existence of
\mathcal{I}
, by an inductive argument it is enough to show that
\{Pv_{1},v_{2},\dots v_{n}\}
or
\{(1-P)v_{1},v_{2},\dots,v_{n}\}
is a basis for
V
. If
Pv_{1}=0
or
(1-P)v_{1}=0
then there is nothing to prove, so assume that
Pv_{1}\neq 0
and
(1-P)v_{1}\neq 0
. If neither of these sets are linearly independent then
Pv_{1}\in\mathrm{span}\{v_{2},\dots v_{n}\}
and
(1-P)v_{1}\in\mathrm{span}\{v_{2},\dots v_{n}\}
. However, this implies that
v_{1}\in\mathrm{span}\{v_{2},\dots v_{n}\}
, which is a contradiction.

To see that
|\mathcal{I}|=\dim(\mathrm{Im}(P))
, note that we obviously have
|\mathcal{I}|\leq\dim(\mathrm{Im}(P))
. On the other hand, since
P
is a projection,

n-|\mathcal{I}|=|\mathcal{I}^{c}|\leq\dim(\mathrm{Im}(1-P))=n-\dim(\mathrm{Im}(P))

hence
\dim(\mathrm{Im}(P))\leq|\mathcal{I}|
.
∎

Proof of
2.11
.

By
[
KMS13
, Theorem 1.13]
,
p
has constant rank near
R=p(M)
. Let
q\in R
and let
x_{a}
be a weighted coordinate system defined near
q
. Since the cotangent map
T^{*}_{q}p:T^{*}_{q}M\to T^{*}_{q}M
is a projection,
Lemma 2.12
implies that there is an index set
\mathcal{I}
with
|\mathcal{I}|=\mathrm{rank}_{q}(p)=\dim(R)
such that

\{p^{*}x_{a}:a\in\mathcal{I}\}\cup\{x_{b}-p^{*}x_{b}:b\in\mathcal{I}^{c}\}

(13)

is a coordinate system. In fact, since
p
is a weighted morphism, it is a weighted coordinate system near
q
. Note as well that for any
q^{\prime}\in R
sufficiently close to
q
we have that

(x_{b}-p^{*}x_{b})(q^{\prime})=x_{b}(q^{\prime})-x_{b}(p(q^{\prime}))=x_{b}(q^{\prime})-x_{b}(q^{\prime})=0;

since
|\mathcal{I}^{c}|=\mathrm{codim}(R)
it follows that (
13
) is a weighted submanifold coordinate system for
R
.
∎

5
Weighted Morphisms

We now turn our attention to weighted morphisms. In this section we will establish the weighted analogues of embeddings, submersions, and transversality. In practice, it can be hard to check whether a given map is a weighted morphism or not, so we also give two characterizations of weighted morphisms - one in terms of its graph and one in terms of “weighted paths”. Let us begin be giving some examples of weighted morphisms.

Examples 2.13
.

(a)

Let
(M,N)
and
(M^{\prime},N^{\prime})
be weighted pairs, and let
F:M\to N
be a smooth map. If
M
and
M^{\prime}
are both trivially weighted, then
F
is a weighted morphism if and only if
F(N)\subseteq N^{\prime}
. In general, if
F
is weighted then
F(N)\subseteq N^{\prime}
.

(b)

With the set up as above, if
N=\emptyset
or
N^{\prime}=M^{\prime}
, then
F
is a weighted morphism.

(c)

Suppose that
(M,N)
and
(M^{\prime},N^{\prime})
are weighted manifolds. Then both of the projections
M\times M^{\prime}\to M
and
M\times M^{\prime}\to M
are weighted morphisms. If
p\in M^{\prime}
, then the inclusion
M\to M\times M^{\prime}
,
m\mapsto(m,p)
is a weighted morphism if and only if
p\in N^{\prime}
. On the other hand, the inclusion
M\times\{p\}\hookrightarrow M\times M^{\prime}

is
a weighted morphism for any
p\in M^{\prime}
since
M\times\{p\}
is a weighted submanifold of
M\times M^{\prime}
; in the case when
p\notin N^{\prime}
,
M\times\{p\}
is weighted along the empty set, hence is
not
isomorphic to
M
as a weighted manifold.

5.1
Weighted Embeddings

We now discuss
weighted embeddings
. Recall that an immersion is a smooth map whose tangent map is an isomorphism onto its image. Taking into account the philosophy that the tangent bundle of a weighted pair
(M,N)
is
TM
together with the filtration (
11
) of
TM|_{N}
, we arrive at the following definition.

Definition 2.14
.

A weighted morphism
f:(M,N)\to(M^{\prime},N^{\prime})
is called a
weighted immersion
if

(a)

f:M\to M^{\prime}
is an immersion,

(b)

Tf:TM\to TM^{\prime}
restricts to injections
(TM|_{N})_{(i)}\to(TM^{\prime}|_{N^{\prime}})_{(i)}
with image

Tf(TM)\cap(TM^{\prime}|_{N^{\prime}})_{(i)}.

A weighted
embedding
is defined analogously.

In particular, the intersections
Tf(TM)\cap(TM^{\prime}|_{N^{\prime}})_{(i)}
are subbundles of
TM^{\prime}|_{N^{\prime}}
and

Tf:TM\to Tf(TM)\cap(TM^{\prime}|_{N^{\prime}})_{(i)}

is a vector bundle isomorphism.

Examples 2.15
.

(a)

If
R
is a weighted submanifold of the weighted pair
(M,N)
, then the inclusion
\iota:(R,R\cap N)\hookrightarrow(M,N)
is a weighted embedding by
2.9
.

(b)

If
(M,N)
and
(M^{\prime},N^{\prime})
are both given the trivial weighting, then a weighted morphism
f:(M,N)\to(M^{\prime},N^{\prime})
is a weighted embedding if and only if
f
is an embedding and
f(M)
intersects
N^{\prime}
cleanly, with intersection
f(N)
. Indeed, for
i=1
the bundles in question are the zero bundles, so we have a diffeomorphism

N\cong f(M)\cap N^{\prime},

which, in particular, says that
f(M)\cap N^{\prime}
is a manifold with
T(f(M)\cap N^{\prime})=Tf(TN)
. For
i=0
this says that

TN\cong Tf(TM)\cap TN^{\prime},

hence
f(M)
intersects
N^{\prime}
cleanly.

(c)

Let
M=\mathbb{R}
with
\mathrm{wt}(x)=2
and
M^{\prime}=\mathbb{R}
with
\mathrm{wt}(x)=1
. Then the identity map
M\to M^{\prime}
is a weighted morphism, but it is
not
a weighted embedding, since
(T_{0}M)_{(-1)}=0
but
(T_{0}M^{\prime})_{(-1)}=\mathbb{R}
.

Theorem 2.16

(Normal Form for Weighted Immersions)
.

Let
i:M\to M^{\prime}
be a smooth map between weighted manifolds of dimensions
n\leq n^{\prime}
. Then
i
is a weighted immersion if and only if for all
p\in M
, with image
p_{0}=i(p)
, there exist weighted coordinates
x_{1},\dots,x_{n}
near
p_{0}
such that
i^{*}x_{1},\dots,i^{*}x_{n}
are weighted coordinates near
p
and
i^{*}x_{n+1}=\cdots=i^{*}x_{n^{\prime}}=0
.

We refer to the coordinates
x_{1},\dots,x_{n^{\prime}}
in
Theorem 2.16
as
immersion coordinates
for the map
i
.

Proof.

The direction
(\Leftarrow)
follows by direct examination in weighted immersion coordinates. For the converse, suppose that
\iota
is a weighted immersion. To build weighted submanifold coordinates near
p_{0}\in f(M)\cap N^{\prime}
, choose a system of weighted coordinates
x_{1},\dots,x_{n^{\prime}}
. We may choose a subset of coordinates, re-indexed as
x_{1},\dots,x_{n}
, such that the image of the set
\{\mathrm{d}_{p_{0}}x_{a}:w_{a}=i\}
in
\mathrm{gr}(T^{*}_{p_{0}}M^{\prime})_{(i)}
pulls back to a basis of
\mathrm{gr}(T^{*}_{p}M)_{(i)}
. In particular,
y_{a}=\iota^{*}x_{a}
, for
a=1,\dots,n
, are weighted coordinates on
M
. If the pull-backs of remaining coordinates vanish, we are done. Otherwise, consider a coordinate
x_{a}
with
a>n
and
i=w_{a}
as large as possible that such that
\iota^{*}x_{a}\neq 0
. Then
\iota^{*}x_{a}
has filtration degree
i
, so it can be written as
\iota^{*}x_{a}=h(y_{1},\dots,y_{n})
, where
h
is a function with
h(t^{w_{1}}c_{1},\dots,t^{w_{n}}c_{n})=O(t^{i})
for all
c\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
. By replacing
x_{a}
with
x_{a}-h(x_{1},\dots,x_{n})
, we can arrange that
\iota^{*}x_{a}=0
. Proceeding in this way, we obtain the required weighted coordinate system.
∎

Applying this to the case that our weighted immersion is a weighted embedding yields the following.

Corollary 2.17
.

If
i:(M,N)\to(M^{\prime},N^{\prime})
is a weighted embedding, then
i(M)
is a weighted submanifold of
(M^{\prime},N^{\prime})
such that
i(M)\cap N^{\prime}=i(N)
and
(M,N)
is isomorphic to
(i(M),i(N))
as weighted manifolds.

Applying this to the inclusion of a submanifold
R\subseteq M
yields the following characterization of weighted submanifolds, which generalizes clean intersection.

Corollary 2.18
.

If
(M,N)
is a weighted pair, then a submanifold
R\subseteq M
is a weighted submanifold if and only if

(a)

R
is weighted along
R\cap N
,

(b)

the inclusion
R\hookrightarrow M
is a weighted morphism, and

(c)

(TR|_{R\cap N})_{(i)}=TR\cap(TM|_{N})_{(i)}
.

Remark 2.19
.

Note that this says that we could have taken the conclusion of
2.9
as a definition of weighted submanifolds. In particular, we could use this to give a define immersed weighted submanifolds.

5.2
Weighted Submersions

We now discuss
weighted submersions
. Since a submersion is a smooth map whose tangent map is fibrewise surjective, the discussion at the beginning of the last section leads us to the following definition.

Definition 2.20
.

Let
(M,N)
and
(M^{\prime},N^{\prime})
be weighted pairs. A submersion
\pi:M\to M^{\prime}
is called a
weighted submersion
if it is a weighted morphism such that for each
i
the map

(TM|_{N})_{(i)}\to(TM^{\prime}|_{N^{\prime}})_{(i)}

is fibrewise surjective.

Examples 2.21
.

(a)

If
(M_{1},N_{1})
and
(M_{2},N_{2})
are weighted pairs, then both of the projections
\pi_{i}:M_{1}\times M_{2}\to M_{i}
are weighted submersions.

(b)

If
M=\mathbb{R}^{2}
is weighted by
\mathrm{wt}(x)=1
and
\mathrm{wt}(y)=3
, and
M^{\prime}=\mathbb{R}^{2}
is given the trivial weighting along the curve
y=x^{2}
, then the identity map
M\to M^{\prime}
is a weighted morphism and a submersion, but it is not a weighted submersion. Indeed, we have that
(T_{0}M)_{(-i)}=\mathrm{span}\{\partial_{x}\}
, but
(T_{0}M^{\prime})_{(-1)}=\mathrm{span}\{\partial_{x},\partial_{y}\}

(c)

If
(M,N)
and
(M^{\prime},N^{\prime})
are both trivially weighted, then the weighted submersions are the submersions
\pi:M\to M^{\prime}
which restrict to submersions
\pi|_{N}:N\to N^{\prime}
.

Theorem 2.22

(Normal Form for Weighted Submersions)
.

Let
\pi:M\to M^{\prime}
be a smooth map between weighted manifolds of dimensions
n\geq n^{\prime}
. Then
\pi
is a weighted submersion if and only if for all
p\in M
, with image
p_{0}=\pi(p)
, there are weighted coordinates
x_{1},\dots,x_{n}
around
p
such that
x_{1},\dots,x_{n^{\prime}}
are
\pi
-basic and descend to weighted coordinates near
p_{0}
.

We refer to the coordinates
x_{1},\dots,x_{n}
in
Theorem 2.22
as
submersion coordinates
for the map
\pi
.

Proof.

The direction
(\Leftarrow)
follows by direct examination in weighted submersion coordinates. For the opposite direction, note that the condition implies that for
p\in N
, with image
p_{0}=\pi(p)
, the map

\mathrm{gr}(T^{*}_{p_{0}}M^{\prime})_{(i)}\to\mathrm{gr}(T^{*}_{p}M)_{(i)}

is injective for each
i
. Let
\{y_{b}\}
be coordinates on an open neighborhood
U_{0}
of
p_{0}
. Then the set
\{\mathrm{d}_{p_{0}}y_{b}:\mathrm{wt}(y_{b})=i\}
defines a basis of
\mathrm{gr}(T^{*}_{p_{0}}M^{\prime})_{(i)}
. Their pullbacks under
\pi
are hence linearly independent in
\mathrm{gr}(T^{*}_{p}M)_{(i)}
. By
Lemma 2.4
, the functions
\pi^{*}y_{b}
are part of a system of weighted coordinates near
p
.
∎

Put differently, this result says that given a weighted submersion we can always find weighted coordinates which are simultaneously submersion coordinates. In light of this, we have the following weighted versions of the standard results from differential geometry.

Corollary 2.23
.

Let
(M,N)
and
(M^{\prime},N^{\prime})
be weighted pairs and
\pi:(M,N)\to(M^{\prime},N^{\prime})
a weighted submersion. Then the pre-image of any weighted submanifold is a weighted submanifold. In particular, the fibres of a weighted submersion are weighted submanifolds.

5.3
Weighted Transversality

We may also consider the weighted analogue of transversality. Let
(M,N)
,
(M^{\prime},N^{\prime})
, and
(M^{\prime\prime},N^{\prime\prime})
be weighted pairs and
f:M\to M^{\prime\prime}
,
g:M^{\prime}\to M^{\prime\prime}
be weighted morphisms.

Definition 2.24
.

We say that
f
and
g
are
weighted transverse
if they are transverse and

T_{p}f(\mathrm{gr}(T_{p}M)_{(i)})+T_{q}g(\mathrm{gr}(T_{q}M^{\prime})_{(i)})=\mathrm{gr}(T_{f(p)}M^{\prime\prime})_{(i)}

for all
(p,q)\in N\times N^{\prime}
such that
f(p)=g(q)
.

Example 2.25
.

A weighted submersion is weighted transverse to any weighted morphism.

Remark 2.26
.

If
f
and
g
are weighted transverse, then their restrictions to
N
and
N^{\prime}
remain transverse. Hence
N\times_{N^{\prime\prime}}N^{\prime}
is a smooth manifold.

Theorem 2.27
.

If
f:M_{0}\to M
and
g:M_{1}\to M
are weighted transverse, then their fibre product

M_{1}\times_{M}M_{0}=\{(q,p)\in M_{1}\times M_{0}:f(p)=g(q)\}

is a weighted submanifold of
M_{1}\times M_{0}
, with induced weighted along
N_{1}\times_{N}N_{0}
. Moreover, the projections

\mathrm{pr}_{i}:M_{1}\times_{M}M_{0}\to M_{i},\quad i=0,1,

are both weighted morphisms.

Proof.

Since
f
and
g
are transverse, it follows that
M_{1}\times_{M}M_{0}
is a submanifold of
M_{1}\times M_{0}
. Therefore, we must produce weighted submanifold coordinates near any
(p,q)\in(M_{1}\times_{M}M_{0})\cap(N_{1}\times N_{0})
.

Let
U\subseteq M
be an open neighbourhood of the point
r=f(p)=g(q)\in M
and let
x_{1},\dots,x_{m}
be a weighted coordinate system defined on
U
. Consider the functions

\displaystyle y_{a}:M_{1}\times M_{0}

\displaystyle\to\mathbb{R}

\displaystyle(q,p)

\displaystyle\mapsto(f^{*}x_{a})(p)-(g^{*}x_{a})(q).

Since
f
and
g
are weighted morphisms, and since
x_{a}
has weight
w_{a}
, it follows that
y_{a}\in C^{\infty}(M_{1}\times M_{0})_{(w_{a})}
. Moreover, since

\displaystyle\mathrm{d}y_{a}=f^{*}\mathrm{d}x_{a}-g^{*}\mathrm{d}x_{a}

the weighted transversality assumption ensures that the image of the set
\{\mathrm{d}_{(q,p)}y_{a}:w_{a}=i\}
in
\mathrm{gr}(T_{(q,p)}^{*}(M_{1}\times M_{0}))_{(i)}
is linearly independent. Thus,
Lemma 2.4
ensures that
y_{a}
can be extended to a weighted coordinate system. Since
M_{1}\times_{M}M_{1}
is locally cut out by the
y_{a}
, it follows that the completed coordinate system are the required weighted submanifold coordinates. Since the projections factor as the composition

M_{1}\times_{M}M_{0}\hookrightarrow M_{1}\times M_{0}\to M_{i},

it is clear that they are weighted morphisms.
∎

Corollary 2.28
.

If two weighted submanifolds intersect in a weighted transverse manner (i.e. the inclusions are weighted transverse), then their intersection is again a weighted submanifold.

Remark 2.29
.

In fact, if the intersection of two weighted submanifolds
R
and
R^{\prime}
is weighted transverse, then by modifying the standard argument appropriately one finds that any point
p\in R\cap R^{\prime}
is contained in an open neighbourhood
U\subseteq M
on which there is a local weighted coordinate system
x_{a}
such that

R\cap U=\{x_{1}=\cdots=x_{r}=0\}\quad\text{and}\quad R^{\prime}\cap U=\{x_{r+1}=\cdots=x_{r^{\prime}}=0\}.

5.4
Characterizations of Weighted Morphisms

It can be difficult in practice to verify if a given map between weighted manifolds is a weighted morphism. It is therefore desirable to have alternative characterizations that one can appeal to in specific examples.

Let
(M,N)
and
(M^{\prime},N^{\prime})
be weighted manifolds. One might guess that weighted morphisms are the smooth maps of pairs
f:(M,N)\to(M^{\prime},N^{\prime})
for which the tangent map
Tf:TM|_{N}\to TM^{\prime}|_{N^{\prime}}
is filtration preserving. However, this is not sufficient, as the following example shows.

Example 2.30
.

Consider
\mathbb{R}
with the trivial weighting along the origin, and
\mathbb{R}^{2}
weighted along the origin by declaring that
\mathrm{wt}(x)=1
and
\mathrm{wt}(y)=3
. Consider the function

\displaystyle f:\mathbb{R}

\displaystyle\to\mathbb{R}^{2}

\displaystyle x

\displaystyle\mapsto(x,x^{2});

this is not a weighted morphism because
y
has weight 3, but
f^{*}y=x^{2}
has weight 2. However,
Tf_{0}:T_{0}\mathbb{R}\to T_{0}\mathbb{R}^{2}
is a filtration preserving map.

For another possible approach, recall that morphisms of manifolds with some additional structure can be characterized in terms of their graphs. For instance,

(a)

linear maps between vector spaces are exactly the maps
\varphi:V\to W
whose graph

\Gamma(\varphi)=\{(\varphi(v),v):v\in V\}\subseteq W\times V

is a linear subspace of
W\times V
;

(b)

Lie algebroid morphisms are the vector bundle morphisms
\varphi:A\to B
between Lie algebroids whose graphs are Lie subalgebroids of
B\times A
;

(c)

Lie groupoid morphisms are exactly the smooth maps
\varphi:G\to H
between Lie groupoids whose graphs are Lie subgroupoids of
H\times G
.

In light of this, one might guess that weighted morphisms are exactly the smooth maps whose graph is a weighted submanifold of the product. It turns out that this is also not sufficient, as the following example shows.

Example 2.31
.

Let
(M,N)=(\mathbb{R}^{2},\{0\})
be the trivially weighted pair, and let
(M^{\prime},N^{\prime})=(\mathbb{R}^{2},\{0\})
be weighting pair given by assigning
x
weight
1
and
y
weight 3. Then the identity map
\mathrm{id}:(M,N)\to(M^{\prime},N^{\prime})
is not a weighted morphism, but its graph is a weighted submanifold of
(M^{\prime}\times M,N^{\prime}\times N)
. Indeed, this follows because
\mathrm{id}:(M^{\prime},N^{\prime})\to(M,N)

is
a weighted morphism.

The following theorem says that these two conditions together are enough to characterize weighted morphisms.

Theorem 2.32
.

Suppose that
(M,N)
and
(M^{\prime},N^{\prime})
are weighted pairs and
f:(M,N)\to(M^{\prime},N^{\prime})
is a smooth map of pairs. Then
f
is a weighted morphism if and only if

(a)

the graph
\Gamma(f)\subseteq M^{\prime}\times M
is a weighted submanifold and

(b)

the tangent map
Tf:TM|_{N}\to TM^{\prime}|_{N^{\prime}}
is filtration preserving.

Proof.

First, suppose that
f:(M,N)\to(M^{\prime},N^{\prime})
is a weighted morphism. Let
x_{a}
be weighted coordinates near
p\in N
and
y_{b}
be weighted coordinates near
q=f(p)\in N^{\prime}
. Then weighted submanifold coordinates for
\Gamma(f)
near
(q,p)
are given by
\tilde{x_{a}}=x_{a}
and
\tilde{y_{b}}=y_{b}-f^{*}y_{b}
. To see that
Tf|_{N}
is filtration preserving, let
p\in N
. Since
\partial_{x_{a}}(f^{*}y_{b})\in C^{\infty}(M)_{(w^{\prime}_{b}-w_{a})}
we have that

w^{\prime}_{b}>w_{a}\implies\frac{\partial(f^{*}y_{b})}{\partial x_{a}}\bigg|_{p}=0.

Therefore,

T_{p}f\left(\frac{\partial}{\partial x_{a}}\bigg|_{p}\right)=\sum_{b\ :\ w^{\prime}_{b}\leq w_{a}}\frac{\partial(y_{b}^{*}f)}{\partial x_{a}}\bigg|_{p}\frac{\partial}{\partial y_{b}}\bigg|_{f(p)}\in(T_{f(p)}M^{\prime})_{(-w_{a})}.

For the converse, suppose that (a) and (b) are satisfied. The map
f
factors as a composition of the diffeomorphism

\displaystyle M\to\Gamma(f),\quad x\mapsto(f(x),x),

(14)

the inclusion
\Gamma(f)\to M^{\prime}\times M
, and the projection
M^{\prime}\times M\to M^{\prime}
. Since the composition of weighted morphisms is a weighted morphism, it suffices to show that the map (
14
) is a weighted diffeomorphism (i.e. a weighted morphism with weighted inverse). The inverse map
\Gamma(f)\to M
is a weighted morphism, since it is the restriction of the projection
M^{\prime}\times M\to M
to a weighted submanifold. By
Theorem 2.22
it is enough to show that the map

T\Gamma(f)|_{\Gamma(f)\cap N}\to TM|_{N}

is an isomorphism of filtered vector bundles. By
2.9
, the filtration on
T\Gamma(f)=T\Gamma(f)\cap(N^{\prime}\times N)
is defined by the intersections

(T\Gamma(f)|_{\Gamma(f)\cap N})|_{(i)}=T\Gamma(f)\cap((TM^{\prime}|_{N^{\prime}})_{(i)}\times(TM|_{N})_{(i)}).

This intersection consists of all
(Tf(v),v)
such that
v\in(TM|_{N})_{(i)}
and
Tf(v)\in(TM^{\prime}_{N^{\prime}})_{(i)}
, hence it maps isomorphically onto
(TM|_{N})_{(i)}
if and only if
Tf
is filtration preserving.
∎

Example 2.33
.

For any weighted manifold
M
, the diagonal
\Delta_{M}\subseteq M\times M
is a weighted submanifold.

Another characterization of weighted morphisms is given by the notion of a
weighted path
. Let
(M,N)
be a weighted pair.

Definition 2.34
.

A path
\gamma:\mathbb{R}\to M
is called a
weighted path
if it is a weighted morphism for the trivial weighting of
\mathbb{R}
along the origin.

If
x_{a}
is a system of weighted coordinates, with weight
w_{a}
, then
\gamma
is of the form

\gamma(t)=(x_{1}(t),\dots,x_{n}(t))=(O(t^{w_{1}}),\dots,O(t^{w_{n}})).

We can completely recover the weighting of
M
by knowledge of the weighted paths.

Theorem 2.35
.

(a)

We have
f\in C^{\infty}(M)_{(i)}
if and only if

f(\gamma(t))=O(t^{i})

for every weighted path
\gamma:\mathbb{R}\to M
.

(b)

A smooth map
\phi:(M,N)\to(M^{\prime},N^{\prime})
between weighted pairs is a weighted morphism if and only if it takes weighted paths to weighted paths.

Proof.

(a)

We have to show the "only if" direction. Suppose that
f\in C^{\infty}(M)
has the property that
f(\gamma(t))=O(t^{i})
for every weighted path
\gamma:\mathbb{R}\to M
has filtration degree
i
.

Let
p\in N
and let
x_{a}
be weighted coordinates defined on
U\subseteq M
. Consider the Taylor expansion of
f
with respect to these coordinates:

f(x)=\sum_{I\cdot w<i}c_{I}x^{I}+g(x)=\sum_{j=1}^{i-1}p_{j}(x)+g(x),

where
I=(i_{1},\dots,i_{n})
is a multi-index,
g\in C^{\infty}(M)_{(i)}
, and
p_{j}
are weighted homogeneous of degree
j
. Let
\lambda=(\lambda_{1},\dots,\lambda_{n})\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
and consider the weighted path
\gamma(t)=(\lambda_{1}t^{w_{1}},\dots,\lambda_{n}t^{w_{n}})
. Using that the
p_{j}
are weighted homogeneous of degree
j
, we have, by assumption, that

f(\gamma(t))=\sum_{j=0}^{i-1}p_{j}(\gamma(t))+g(\gamma(t))=\sum_{j=0}^{i-1}t^{j}p_{j}(\lambda)+g(\gamma(t))=O(t^{i}).

Since
g(\gamma(t))=O(t^{i})
, this implies that
t^{j}p_{j}(\lambda)=O(t^{i})
, whence
p_{j}(\lambda)=0
for all
\lambda\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
. Thus,
f=g\in C^{\infty}(M)_{(i)}
.

(b)

Since the composition of weighted morphisms is weighted, a weighted morphism takes weighted paths to weighted paths. For the converse, suppose that
\phi
takes weighted paths to weighted paths. Let
f\in C^{\infty}(M^{\prime})_{(i)}
and let
\gamma:\mathbb{R}\to M
be a weighted path. Then
\phi\circ\gamma:\mathbb{R}\to M^{\prime}
is a weighted path and

(\phi^{*}f)(\gamma(t))=f(\phi(\gamma(t)))=O(t^{i}),

hence
\phi^{*}f\in C^{\infty}(M)_{(i)}
by part (a). ∎

6
The Weighted Normal Bundle

Let us motivate this section by the following observation, which we learned from Haj and Higson (
[
SH18
]
). Suppose that
N\subseteq M
is a closed submanifold, and let

\nu(M,N)=TM|_{N}/TN

be the normal bundle of
N
in
M
. Any
f\in C^{\infty}(M)
defines a fibrewise constant function
f^{[0]}\in C^{\infty}_{[0]}(\nu(M,N))=C^{\infty}(N)
by restriction to
N
followed by pull back, and the assignment
f\mapsto f^{[0]}
defines an isomorphism

C^{\infty}(M)/\mathcal{I}_{N}\to C^{\infty}_{[0]}(\nu(M,N)).

If
f\in\mathcal{I}_{N}
, the differential
\mathrm{d}f
vanishes along
TN
, hence defines a fibrewise linear map

f^{[1]}\in C^{\infty}_{[1]}(\nu(M,N))=\Gamma(\nu(M,N)^{*}),\quad X_{p}\mapsto\mathrm{d}_{p}f(X_{p}),

and the assignment
f\mapsto f^{[1]}
defines an isomorphism

\mathcal{I}_{N}/\mathcal{I}_{N}^{2}\to C^{\infty}_{[1]}(\nu(M,N)).

This extends uniquely to an isomorphism of graded algebras

\bigoplus_{k=0}^{\infty}\mathcal{I}_{N}^{k}/\mathcal{I}_{N}^{k+1}\to C^{\infty}_{pol}(\nu(M,N)),

where
C^{\infty}_{pol}(\nu(M,N))
denotes the graded algebra of fibrewise polynomial functions on
\nu(M,N)
(see (
16
)). It can be shown (see, for instance,
[
GR09
]
) that the map

\displaystyle\nu(M,N)\to\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathrm{alg}}(C^{\infty}_{pol}(\nu(M,N)),\mathbb{R}),\quad X_{p}\mapsto\mathrm{ev}_{X_{p}}

is a bijection of sets. Even more, one can naturally define a vector bundle structure on
\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathrm{alg}}(C^{\infty}_{pol}(\nu(M,N)),\mathbb{R})
in such a way that the evaluation map is an isomorphism of vector bundles. From this observation, we deduce that we can recover the normal bundle of
N
in
M
from the filtration

C^{\infty}(M)\supseteq\mathcal{I}_{N}\supseteq\mathcal{I}_{N}^{2}\supseteq\cdots.

Loizides and Meinrenken repeated this construction with general weightings in
[
LM23
]
, calling the resulting object the
weighted normal bundle

\nu_{\mathcal{W}}(M,N)\to N
, which is the topic of this section.

6.1
Graded Bundles

In contrast to the normal bundle, the weighted normal bundle is not naturally a vector bundle; instead it is a
graded bundle
, in the sense of Grabowski and Rotkiewicz,
[
GR12
]
.

Definition 2.36

(
[
GR12
]
)
.

A
graded bundle
is a smooth manifold
E
with a smooth monoid action of
(\mathbb{R},\cdot)
,

\kappa:\mathbb{R}\times E\to E,\quad(t,x)\mapsto\kappa_{t}(x).

A
morphism of graded bundles
if a smooth map between graded bundles which intertwines the
(\mathbb{R},\cdot)
-action.

Note in particular that
\kappa_{0}\circ\kappa_{0}=\kappa_{0}
, hence
N=\kappa_{0}(E)
is a submanifold of
E
; in fact, Grabowski and Rotkiewicz showed in
[
GR12
]
that
E
is a locally trivial fibre bundle over
N
.

Examples 2.37
.

(a)

A vector bundle
V\to M
is a graded bundle, with the monoid action of
\mathbb{R}
given by scalar multiplication. In fact, Grabowski and Rotkiewicz showed in
[
GR09
]
that vector bundles are precisely the graded bundle for which the map

V\to TE,\quad v\mapsto\frac{d}{dt}\bigg|_{t=0}\kappa_{t}(v)

(15)

is injective.

(b)

A negatively graded vector bundle
E=E_{-r}\oplus E_{-r+1}\oplus\cdots\oplus E_{-1}\to N
is a graded bundle, where
\mathbb{R}
acts on the factor
E_{-i}
as scalar multiplication by
t^{i}
. If
E\to N
is a graded bundle, then
\nu(E,N)
is a graded vector bundle and there is a non-canonical isomorphism

E\cong\nu(E,N)

as graded bundles (see
[
GR12
]
). Thus, any graded bundle is
non-canonically
the total space of a vector bundle.

(c)

A graded Lie groupoid is a Lie groupoid
G\rightrightarrows M
with a monoid action of
(\mathbb{R},\cdot)
by Lie groupoid morphisms. In the case that the scalar multiplication satisfies (
15
), then this recovers the notion of a VB-groupoid. On the other hand, if
G\rightrightarrows M
is a vector bundle, then this recovers the notion of a graded vector bundle.

Given a graded bundle
\pi:E\to N
, let
C^{\infty}_{[n]}(E)=\{f\in C^{\infty}(E):\kappa_{t}^{*}f=t^{n}f\}
and let

C^{\infty}_{pol}(E)=\bigoplus_{n\geq 0}C^{\infty}_{[n]}(E)

(16)

be the space of polynomial functions on
E
; note that
C^{\infty}_{[0]}(E)=C^{\infty}(N)
. Let
k_{0}=\mathrm{dim}(N)
and
k_{i}=\mathrm{rank}(\nu(E,N)_{-i})
. Given an open set
U\subseteq N
, coordinate systems
x_{a}
defined on
\pi^{-1}(U)\subseteq E
are called
graded bundle coordinates
if
x_{a}\in C^{\infty}_{[i]}(E)
for
k_{i-1}<a\leq k_{i}
. A
graded subbundle
is a submanifold
F\subseteq E
which is closed under the monoid action of
\mathbb{R}
; graded subbundles are always locally cut out by graded coordinates.

Example 2.38
.

If
E\to N
is a vector bundle then
C^{\infty}_{[1]}(E)\cong\Gamma(E^{*})
. More generally, we have

C^{\infty}_{pol}(E)\cong\mathrm{Sym}(\Gamma(V^{*}))

as graded algebras. Graded coordinate systems for
E
are vector bundle coordinates systems, consisting of coordinates for the base and linear coordinates on the fibres.

As shown in
[
GJR17
]
, graded bundles are completely determined by their polynomial functions, in the sense that

E=\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathrm{alg}}(C^{\infty}_{pol}(E),\mathbb{R}).

(17)

6.2
Definition of the weighted normal bundle

In light of (
17
), one could
define
a graded bundle by declaring what its polynomial functions are (cf.
[
SH18
]
). Motivated by the observation that
C^{\infty}_{pol}(\nu(M,N))=\bigoplus_{k\geq 0}\mathcal{I}_{N}^{k}/\mathcal{I}_{N}^{k+1}
, let
(M,N)
be a weighted pair and let
\mathrm{gr}(C^{\infty}(M))
be the graded algebra with graded components

\mathrm{gr}(C^{\infty}(M))_{(i)}=C^{\infty}(M)_{(i)}/C^{\infty}(M)_{(i+1)}.

Definition 2.39

(
[
LM23
]
)
.

The
weighted normal bundle
of the weighted pair
(M,N)
is the set

\nu_{\mathcal{W}}(M,N)=\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathrm{alg}}(\mathrm{gr}(C^{\infty}(M)),\mathbb{R}).

Given
f\in C^{\infty}(M)_{(i)}
, let
f^{[i]}
denote its class in
\mathrm{gr}(C^{\infty}(M))_{(i)}
. Evaluation by
f^{[i]}
determines a map, which we also denote by
f^{[i]}
,

f^{[i]}:\nu_{\mathcal{W}}(M,N)\to\mathbb{R}\quad\varphi\mapsto\varphi(f^{[i]})

which we refer to as the
i
-th homogeneous approximation
of
f
. The
C^{\infty}
-structure of
\nu_{\mathcal{W}}(M,N)
is defined by declaring that
f^{[i]}
is smooth for any
f\in C^{\infty}(M)_{(i)}
.

Theorem 2.40

(
[
LM23
, Theorem 4.2]
)
.

(a)

The weighted normal bundle
\nu_{\mathcal{W}}(M,N)
has a unique structure as a graded bundle over
N
, of dimension equal to that of
M
, in such a way that

C^{\infty}_{pol}(\nu_{\mathcal{W}}(M,N))=\mathrm{gr}(C^{\infty}(M))

as graded algebras.

(b)

Given weighted coordinates
x_{1},\dots,x_{n}
on
U\subseteq M
, the homogeneous approximations
x_{a}^{[w_{a}]}
for
a=1,\dots,n
serve as graded bundle coordinates on
\nu_{\mathcal{W}}(M,N)|_{U\cap N}=\nu_{\mathcal{W}}(U,U\cap N)
. Moreover,
C^{\infty}_{pol}(\nu_{\mathcal{W}}(U,U\cap N))
is generated as an algebra over
C^{\infty}(U\cap N)
by
x_{a}^{[w_{a}]}
with
w_{a}\geq 1
.

This construction is functorial for weighted morphisms. That is, any weighted morphism
\varphi:(M,N)\to(M^{\prime},N^{\prime})
induces a morphism of graded bundles

\nu_{\mathcal{W}}(\varphi):\nu_{\mathcal{W}}(M,N)\to\nu_{\mathcal{W}}(M^{\prime},N^{\prime}).

Note as well that for any
f\in C^{\infty}(M^{\prime})_{(i)}
we have

\nu_{\mathcal{W}}(\varphi)^{*}f^{[i]}=(\varphi^{*}f)^{[i]}.

Examples 2.41
.

(a)

If
M
is trivially weighted along
N
then
\nu_{\mathcal{W}}(M,N)=\nu(M,N)
, as explained at the beginning of this section. In particular,
\nu_{\mathcal{W}}(M,M)=M
and
\nu_{\mathcal{W}}(M,\emptyset)=\emptyset

(b)

If
(M,N)
and
(M^{\prime},N^{\prime})
are weighted pairs then the canonical map

\nu_{\mathcal{W}}(M\times M^{\prime},N\times N^{\prime})\to\nu_{\mathcal{W}}(M,N)\times\nu_{\mathcal{W}}(M^{\prime},N^{\prime})

is an isomorphism of graded bundles, as can be seen by considering local graded bundle coordinates. Moreover if
\varphi:(M,N)\to(R,Q)
and
\psi:(M^{\prime},N^{\prime})\to(R^{\prime},Q^{\prime})
are weighted morphisms, then under this identification
\nu_{\mathcal{W}}(\varphi\times\psi)=\nu_{\mathcal{W}}(\varphi)\times\nu_{\mathcal{W}}(\psi)
.

(c)

If
\mathbb{R}^{2}
is weighted be declaring that
\mathrm{wt}(x)=1
and
\mathrm{wt}(y)=3
, then
\nu_{\mathcal{W}}(\mathbb{R},\{0\})=\mathbb{R}^{2}
. The monoid action of
\mathbb{R}
on
\mathbb{R}^{2}
is given by

\kappa_{t}(x,y)=(tx,t^{3}y).

(d)

If
E\to N
is a graded bundle, then
E
is canonically weighted along
N
by

C^{\infty}(E)_{(i)}=\{f\in C^{\infty}(E):\kappa_{t}^{*}f=O(t^{i})\};

weighted coordinates are then given by graded bundle coordinates. With respect to this weighted,
\nu_{\mathcal{W}}(E,N)=E
canonically.

6.3
Properties of the weighted normal bundle

Proposition 2.42
.

If
\iota:(R,Q)\hookrightarrow(M,N)
is a weighted embedding then

\nu_{\mathcal{W}}(\iota):\nu_{\mathcal{W}}(R,Q)\to\nu_{\mathcal{W}}(M,N)

is an embedding.

Proof.

By
Theorem 2.16
we may assume that
R
is a weighted submanifold of
M
and
Q=R\cap N
. We will show that any choice of weighted submanifold coordinates for
R
define submanifold coordinates for the image of
\nu_{\mathcal{W}}(R,R\cap N)
in
\nu_{\mathcal{W}}(M,N)
.

Recall that the weighting on
R
is defined so that
\iota:R\hookrightarrow M
defines a surjection
\mathrm{gr}(C^{\infty}(M))\to\mathrm{gr}(C^{\infty}(R))
, hence an injection
\nu_{\mathcal{W}}(R,R\cap N)\to\nu_{\mathcal{W}}(M,N)
. The image of
\nu_{\mathcal{W}}(R,R\cap N)
in
\nu_{\mathcal{W}}(M,N)
consists of algebra morphisms
\varphi:\mathrm{gr}(C^{\infty}(M))\to\mathbb{R}
with the property that the value of
\varphi(f^{[i]})
depends only on
(f|_{R})^{[i]}\in C^{\infty}(R)_{(i)}/C^{\infty}(R)_{(i+1)}
. Therefore, if
x_{a}
are weighted submanifold coordinates defined on
U\subseteq M
such that

R\cap U=\{x_{1}=\cdots=x_{r}=0\},

and
\varphi\in\nu_{\mathcal{W}}(U,U\cap N)
is in the image of
\nu_{\mathcal{W}}(R\cap U,R\cap N\cap U)
, then since
(x_{a}|_{R})^{[w_{a}]}=0
for
a=1,\dots,r
we have
x_{a}^{[w_{a}]}(\varphi)=0
. This shows that

\nu_{\mathcal{W}}(R\cap U,R\cap N\cap U)\subseteq\{x_{1}^{[w_{1}]}=\cdots=x_{r}^{[w_{r}]}=0\}.

For the reverse inclusion, let
\varphi\in\{x_{1}^{[w_{1}]}=\cdots=x_{r}^{[w_{r}]}=0\}
. Given
f\in C^{\infty}(U)_{(i)}\cap\mathcal{I}_{R\cap U}
, we want to show that
\varphi(f^{[i]})=0
. Since
f
vanishes along
R\cap U
we can write
f=\sum_{a=1}^{r}f_{a}x_{a}
with
f_{a}\in C^{\infty}(U)_{(i-w_{a})}
, hence

\varphi(f^{[i]})=\sum_{a=1}^{r}x_{a}^{[w_{a}]}(\varphi)\cdot f_{a}^{[i-w_{a}]}(\varphi)=0,

as needed.
∎

Proposition 2.43
.

There is a canonical isomorphism

T\nu_{\mathcal{W}}(M,N)|_{N}=\mathrm{gr}(TM|_{N}).

If
\varphi:(M,N)\to(M^{\prime},N^{\prime})
is a weighted morphism, then with respect to this identification one has

T\nu_{\mathcal{W}}(\varphi)|_{N}=\mathrm{gr}(T\varphi|_{N})

Proof.

Using that
\nu_{\mathcal{W}}(M,N)
is a graded bundle and
[
LM23
, Proposition 4.4]
, we have that

\displaystyle T\nu_{\mathcal{W}}(M,N)|_{N}=TN\oplus\mathrm{gr}(\nu(M,N)).

The filtration of
\nu(M,N)
is given by
\nu(M,N)_{(i)}=(TM|_{N})_{(i)}/TN
, so that

\displaystyle T\nu_{\mathcal{W}}(M,N)|_{N}

\displaystyle=TN\oplus\bigoplus_{i=1}^{r}\nu_{\mathcal{W}}(M,N)_{(-i)}/\nu_{\mathcal{W}}(M,N)_{(-i+1)}

\displaystyle=TN\oplus\bigoplus_{i=1}^{r}(TM|_{N})_{(-i)}/(TM|_{N})_{(-i+1)}

\displaystyle=\mathrm{gr}(TM|_{N}),

as claimed.
∎

Corollary 2.44
.

If
F:M\to M^{\prime}
is a weighted submersion between weighted pairs
(M,N)
and
(M^{\prime},N^{\prime})
, then
\nu_{\mathcal{W}}(F)
is a submersion.

Proof.

By
2.43
,
\nu_{\mathcal{W}}(F)
is a submersion along
N\subseteq\nu_{\mathcal{W}}(M,N)
hence near
N
. Since
\nu_{\mathcal{W}}(F)
is a morphism of graded bundles it follows that it is a submersion everywhere.
∎

Proposition 2.45
.

Suppose that
F:M\to M^{\prime\prime}
and
G:M^{\prime}\to M^{\prime\prime}
are weighted transverse. Then

(a)

\nu_{\mathcal{W}}(F):\nu_{\mathcal{W}}(M,N)\to\nu_{\mathcal{W}}(M^{\prime\prime},N^{\prime\prime})
and
\nu_{\mathcal{W}}(G):\nu_{\mathcal{W}}(M^{\prime},N^{\prime})\to\nu_{\mathcal{W}}(M^{\prime\prime},N^{\prime\prime})
are transverse, and

(b)

the canonical map

\nu_{\mathcal{W}}(M\times_{M^{\prime\prime}}M^{\prime},N\times_{N^{\prime\prime}}N^{\prime})\to\nu_{\mathcal{W}}(M,N)\times_{\nu_{\mathcal{W}}(M^{\prime\prime},N^{\prime\prime})}\nu_{\mathcal{W}}(M^{\prime},N^{\prime})

is an isomorphism of graded bundles.

Here the graded bundle structure on the fibre product is as a graded subbundle of
\nu_{\mathcal{W}}(M,N)\times\nu_{\mathcal{W}}(M^{\prime},N^{\prime})\to N\times N^{\prime}
,

Proof.

(a)

By modifying the standard argument appropriately, one finds that
F
and
G
are weighted transverse if and only if the graph
\Gamma(F\times G)
of
F\times G
is weighted transverse to the diagonal
\Delta_{M^{\prime\prime}\times M^{\prime\prime}\times M\times M^{\prime}}=\{(m^{\prime\prime},m^{\prime\prime},m,m^{\prime})\}\subseteq M^{\prime\prime}\times M^{\prime\prime}\times M\times M^{\prime}
. It follows by considering weighted coordinates adapted to this transverse intersection that
\Gamma(\nu_{\mathcal{W}}(F\times G))=\Gamma(\nu_{\mathcal{W}}(F)\times\nu_{\mathcal{W}}(G))
is transverse to the diagonal in
\nu_{\mathcal{W}}(M^{\prime\prime}\times M^{\prime\prime}\times M\times M^{\prime},N^{\prime\prime}\times N^{\prime\prime}\times N\times N^{\prime})
, which establishes the claim.

(b)

This follows by considering graded bundle coordinates, since it is a graded bundle morphism which is the identity along the base. ∎

7
The Weighted Deformation Space

Motivated by the construction of Haj and Higson
[
SH18
]
, Loizides and Meinrenken show that a weighted manifold
M
can be "deformed" to its weighted normal bundle in a smooth way. The construction is known in algebraic geometry as the deformation to the normal cone, which we now review.

7.1
Construction of the Weighted Deformation Space

Let
(M,N)
be a weighted pair, and let
