\mathrm{Rees}(C^{\infty}(M))=\left\{\sum_{i\in\mathbb{Z}}f_{i}z^{-i}:f\in C^{\infty}(M)_{(i)}\right\}\subseteq C^{\infty}(M)[z^{-1},z]

denote the Rees algebra associated to the weighting of
M
.

Definition 2.46

(
[
LM23
]
)
.

The
weighted deformation space
of the weighted pair
(M,N)
is the set

\delta_{\mathcal{W}}(M,N)=\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathrm{alg}}(\mathrm{Rees}(C^{\infty}(M)),\mathbb{R}).

We now explain how to endow
\delta_{\mathcal{W}}(M,N)
with a smooth structure. Given
f\in C^{\infty}(M)_{(i)}
, its
i
-th homogeneous interpolation
is the function

\widetilde{f}^{[i]}:\delta_{\mathcal{W}}(M,N)\to\mathbb{R},\quad\varphi\mapsto\varphi(fz^{-i});

note that if
f\in C^{\infty}(M)_{(i)}
and
g\in C^{\infty}(M)_{(j)}
then
\widetilde{fg}^{[i+j]}=\widetilde{f}^{[i]}\widetilde{g}^{[j]}
. The map
\pi=\widetilde{1}^{[-1]}:\delta_{\mathcal{W}}(M,N)\to\mathbb{R}
is a surjection with fibres

\delta_{\mathcal{W}}(M,N)_{t}=\pi^{-1}(\{t\})=\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}M&t\neq 0\\
\nu_{\mathcal{W}}(M,N)&t=0,\end{array}\right.

(18)

see
[
SH18
,
LM23
]
. Given
f\in C^{\infty}(M)_{(i)}
one has

\displaystyle\begin{split}\widetilde{f}^{[i]}|_{\pi^{-1}(\{t\})}:\delta_{\mathcal{W}}(M,N)_{t}\to\mathbb{R},\quad x\mapsto\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}t^{-i}f(x)&t\neq 0\\
f^{[i]}(x)&t=0.\end{array}\right.\end{split}

(19)

Loizides and Meinrenken prove the following.

Theorem 2.47

(
[
LM23
, Theorem 5.1]
)
.

(a)

There is a unique
C^{\infty}
-structure on
\delta_{\mathcal{W}}(M,N)
, as a manifold of dimension
\dim(M)+1
, such that the maps (
19
) are smooth for each
f\in C^{\infty}(M)_{(i)}
. In terms of this structure, the map
\pi:\delta_{\mathcal{W}}(M,N)\to\mathbb{R}
is a surjective submersion and the identifications (
18
) are diffeomorphisms.

(b)

If
x_{a}
is a weighted coordinate system on
U\subseteq M
, then the homogeneous interpolations
\widetilde{x}_{a}^{[w_{a}]}
, together with the coordinate
t=\pi:\delta_{\mathcal{W}}(M,N)\to\mathbb{R}
are coordinates on
\delta_{\mathcal{W}}(U,U\cap N)=\nu_{\mathcal{W}}(U,U\cap N)\sqcup(U\times\mathbb{R}^{\times})
.

Examples 2.48
.

(a)

If
M
is trivially weighted along
N
, then
\delta_{\mathcal{W}}(M,N)
is the standard deformation to the normal cone. In particular,
\delta_{\mathcal{W}}(M\times M,M)
is the tangent groupoid
\mathbb{T}M
of Connes (cf.
[
Con94
,
SH18
]
).

(b)

If
M
is weighted along itself then
\delta_{\mathcal{W}}(M,M)=M\times\mathbb{R}
.

(c)

If
E
is a graded bundle over
N
, then
\delta_{W}(E,N)
is canonically isomorphic to
E\times\mathbb{R}
. The isomorphism is given by the family of maps

\displaystyle\delta_{\mathcal{W}}(E,N)_{t}

\displaystyle\to E

\displaystyle e

\displaystyle\mapsto\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}\kappa_{t^{-1}}e&t\neq 0\\
e&t=0,\end{array}\right.

where on the
t=0
we are identifying
\nu_{\mathcal{W}}(E,N)
with
E
.

(d)

If
(M,N)
and
(M^{\prime},N^{\prime})
are weighted pairs, then
\delta_{\mathcal{W}}(M\times M^{\prime},N\times N^{\prime})=\delta_{\mathcal{W}}(M,N)\times_{\mathbb{R}}\delta_{\mathcal{W}}(M^{\prime},N^{\prime})
.

7.2
Properties of the Weighted Deformation space

We now list some properties of the weighted normal bundle. Most of the proofs are similar as for the weighted normal bundle, so we will be brief.

(a)

The weighted deformation space is functorial for weighted morphisms. That is, any weighted morphism
F:(M,N)\to(M^{\prime},N^{\prime})
induces a smooth map
\delta_{\mathcal{W}}(F):\delta_{\mathcal{W}}(M,N)\to\delta_{\mathcal{W}}(M^{\prime},N^{\prime})
. In terms of the identifications (
18
), one has

\delta_{\mathcal{W}}(F)|_{\pi^{-1}(\{t\})}:x\mapsto\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}F(x)&t\neq 0\\
\nu_{\mathcal{W}}(F)(x)&t=0.\end{array}\right.

Moreover, given
f\in C^{\infty}(M^{\prime})_{(i)}
we have that
\delta_{\mathcal{W}}(F)^{*}\widetilde{f}^{[i]}=\widetilde{F^{*}f}^{[i]}
.

(b)

If
R
is a weighted submanifold of the weighted pair
(M,N)
, then
\delta_{\mathcal{W}}(R,R\cap N)
is a submanifold of
\delta_{\mathcal{W}}(M,N)
. In particular,
\delta_{\mathcal{W}}(N,N)=N\times\mathbb{R}
is a submanifold of
\delta_{\mathcal{W}}(M,N)
. As with the weighted normal bundle, if
x_{a}
are weighted submanifold coordinates for
R
on
U\subseteq M
, then
\widetilde{x}_{a}^{[w_{a}]}
together with
t
, are submanifold coordinates for
\delta_{\mathcal{W}}(R,R\cap N)
on
\delta_{\mathcal{W}}(U,U\cap N)
.

(c)

If
F:(M,N)\to(M^{\prime\prime},N^{\prime\prime})
and
G:(M^{\prime},N^{\prime})\to(M^{\prime\prime},N^{\prime\prime})
are weighted transverse, then
\delta_{\mathcal{W}}(F):\delta_{\mathcal{W}}(M,N)\to\delta_{\mathcal{W}}(M^{\prime\prime},N^{\prime\prime})
and
\delta_{\mathcal{W}}(G):\delta_{\mathcal{W}}(M^{\prime},N^{\prime})\to\delta_{\mathcal{W}}(M^{\prime\prime},N^{\prime\prime})
are transverse and the canonical map

\delta_{\mathcal{W}}(M\times_{M^{\prime\prime}}M^{\prime},N\times_{N^{\prime\prime}}N^{\prime})\to\delta_{\mathcal{W}}(M,N)\times_{\delta_{\mathcal{W}}(M^{\prime\prime},N^{\prime\prime})}\delta_{\mathcal{W}}(M^{\prime},N^{\prime})

is a diffeomorphism.

(d)

(
[
LM23
, Section 5.2]
) Given
u\in\mathbb{R}^{\times}
, consider the algebra morphism of
\mathrm{Rees}(C^{\infty}(M))

\kappa_{u}:\sum_{i}f_{i}z^{-i}\mapsto\sum_{i}f_{i}u^{i}z^{-i}.

This defines a smooth action of the group
\mathbb{R}^{\times}
on
\delta_{\mathcal{W}}(M,N)
called the
zoom action
. Given
f\in C^{\infty}(M)_{(i)}
, the function
\widetilde{f}^{[i]}
is homogeneous of degree
i
; in particular,
\pi=\widetilde{1}^{[-1]}
is homogeneous of degree -1. Thus,

\kappa_{u}:\delta_{\mathcal{W}}(M,N)_{t}\to\delta_{\mathcal{W}}(M,N)_{u^{-1}t}.

On the open set
M\times\mathbb{R}^{\times}=\pi^{-1}(\mathbb{R}^{\times})\subseteq\delta_{\mathcal{W}}(M,N)
the action is given by

\kappa_{u}:(m,t)\mapsto(m,u^{-1}t),

whereas on
\nu_{\mathcal{W}}(M,N)=\delta_{\mathcal{W}}(M,N)_{0}
the action is the graded bundle multiplication. If
F:(M,N)\to(M^{\prime},N^{\prime})
is a weighted morphism, then
\delta_{\mathcal{W}}(F):\delta_{\mathcal{W}}(M,N)\to\delta_{\mathcal{W}}(M^{\prime},N^{\prime})
is
\mathbb{R}^{\times}
-equivariant.

We have, furthermore, the following theorem which justifies our definitions of weighted immersions and weighted submersions.

Theorem 2.49

(Characterization of Weighted Immersions and Submersions)
.

A weighted morphism
F:(M,N)\to(M^{\prime},N^{\prime})
is a weighted immersion (resp. submersion) if and only if the induced map

\delta_{\mathcal{W}}(F):\delta_{\mathcal{W}}(M,N)\to\delta_{\mathcal{W}}(M^{\prime},N^{\prime})

is an immersion (resp. submersion).

Proof.

We prove the statement for weighted immersions, the proof for weighted submersions being entirely analogous.

If
F
is a weighted immersion, then
\delta_{\mathcal{W}}(F)
is an immersion as can be seen by considering weighted immersion coordinates. On the other hand, if
\delta_{\mathcal{W}}(F)
is an immersion, then clearly
F
is a immersion. To see that the map
(TM|_{N})_{(i)}\to(TM^{\prime}|_{N^{\prime}})_{(i)}
is fibrewise injective, we remark that

T\delta_{\mathcal{W}}(M,N)|_{N\times\{0\}}=\mathrm{gr}(TM|_{N})\times\mathbb{R}

and with respect to this

T_{p}\delta_{\mathcal{W}}(F)=\begin{bmatrix}T_{p}\nu_{\mathcal{W}}(F)&\partial_{t}\widetilde{(F^{*}y)}^{[w^{\prime}]}(p)\\
0&1\end{bmatrix}

for any
p\in N\subseteq\delta_{\mathcal{W}}(M,N)|_{\pi^{-1}(\{0\})}
, where

\partial_{t}\widetilde{(F^{*}y)}^{[w^{\prime}]}(p)=[\partial_{t}\widetilde{(F^{*}y_{1})}^{[w^{\prime}_{1}]}(p),\dots,\partial_{t}\widetilde{(F^{*}y_{m})}^{[w^{\prime}_{m}]}(p)]^{\intercal}.

In particular,
T_{p}\delta_{\mathcal{W}}(F)
is injective only if
T_{p}\nu_{\mathcal{W}}(F)
is, which happens if only if the map
(TM|_{N})_{(i)}\to(TM^{\prime}|_{N^{\prime}})_{(i)}
is fibrewise injective. It remains to show that
TF(TM)\cap(TM^{\prime}|_{N^{\prime}})_{(i)}\subseteq TF((TM|_{N})_{(i)})
(the other inclusion is automatic since
F
is a weighted morphism). Let
p\in N
and
X_{F(p)}\in TF(T_{p}M)\cap(T_{F(p)}M^{\prime})_{(i)}
. Since
F
is an immersion, there exists a unique
Y_{p}\in T_{p}M
such that
TF(Y_{p})=X_{F(p)}
, and furthermore, because
T\nu_{\mathcal{W}}(F)|_{N}=\mathrm{gr}(TF|_{N})
is injective, we see that
Y_{p}\in(T_{p}M)_{(i)}
, as needed.
∎

8
Singular Lie Filtrations

In this section we review
singular Lie filtrations
, which are the main technique for constructing weightings. Singular Lie filtrations are filtrations of the sheaf of vector fields on a smooth manifold by locally finitely generated
C^{\infty}_{M}
-submodules which are compatible with Lie brackets (see
Definition 2.50
), generalization both Lie filtrations and singular foliations in the sense of Androulidakis and Skandalis
[
AS09
]
. They recently made an appearance in the work of Androulidakis, Mohsen, and Yuncken on maximally hypoelliptic operators
[
AMY22
]
.

Every weighted manifold has a canonical singular Lie filtration associated to the weighting. Conversely, Loizides and Meinrenken showed in
[
LM22
]
that a singular Lie filtration on a manifold
M
together with a submanifold
N
satisfying a cleanness assumption canonically defines a weighting of
M
along
N
, generalizing the work of Haj and Higson
[
SH18
]
. This has the practical application that, in examples, it is more natural to define a singular Lie filtration than a weighting. In this section, we review their work.

8.1
Filtration of vector fields

Let
M
have an order
r
weighting along
N
. Define
\mathfrak{X}(M)_{(i)}
to be the collection of vector fields
X\in\mathfrak{X}(M)
whose Lie derivative shifts filtration degree by
i
; that is,

f\in C^{\infty}(M)_{(j)}\implies Xf\in C^{\infty}(M)_{(i+j)}.

This defines a filtration

\mathfrak{X}(M)=\mathfrak{X}(M)_{(-r)}\supseteq\mathfrak{X}(M)_{(-r+1)}\supseteq\cdots

(20)

which is compatible with Lie brackets in the sense that if
X\in\mathfrak{X}(M)_{(i)}
and
Y\in\mathfrak{X}(M)_{(j)}
then
[X,Y]\in\mathfrak{X}(M)_{(i+j)}
. If
x_{a}
is a system of weighted coordinates on
U
, then
X\in\mathfrak{X}(U)_{(i)}
if and only if

X=\sum_{a}f_{a}\frac{\partial}{\partial x_{a}},

with
f\in C^{\infty}(U)_{(i-w_{a})}
. In particular, for any
p\in N
we have

T_{p}M_{(i)}=\mathrm{span}\{X_{p}:X\in\mathfrak{X}(M)_{(i)}\}.

More generally, the weighting of
M
determines a filtration

\cdots\supseteq\mathrm{DO}(M)_{(q)}\supseteq\mathrm{DO}(M)_{(q+1)}\supseteq\cdots

of the graded algebra of differential operators on
M
, where
\mathrm{DO}(M)_{(q)}
is the operators
D\in\mathrm{DO}(M)
with the property that

f\in C^{\infty}(M)_{(i)}\implies Df\in C^{\infty}(M)_{(i+q)}.

One has that
D\in\mathrm{DO}(M)_{(q)}
if locally
D
is given by a linear combination of terms of the form

X_{1}\cdots X_{k},

where
X_{j}\in\mathfrak{X}(U)_{(i_{j})}
with
i_{1}+\cdots+i_{k}=q
; in particular, we note that the filtration of
\mathrm{DO}^{k}(M)_{(q)}
starts in degree
-kr

\mathrm{DO}^{k}(M)=\mathrm{DO}^{k}(M)_{(-kr)}\supseteq\mathrm{DO}^{k}(M)_{(-kr+1)}\supseteq\cdots

where
r
is the order of the weighting. With respect to the filtration of differential operators we have

C^{\infty}(M)_{(i)}=\{f\in C^{\infty}(M):-q<i,\ D\in\mathrm{DO}(M)_{(q)}\implies Df|_{N}=0\}.

(21)

8.2
Singular Lie filtrations

Equation 21
implies, in particular, that we can recover the weighting of
M
from the filtration of
\mathfrak{X}(M)
and the submanifold
N
. It is reasonable, therefore, to ask when a filtration of
\mathfrak{X}(M)
defines a weighting of
M
. An answer to this question was given by Loizides and Meinrenken in
[
LM22
]
, which we now summarize.

Definition 2.50

(
[
LM22
]
)
.

Let
M
be a smooth manifold.

(a)

A
singular distribution
on
M
is a sheaf
\mathcal{D}
of
C^{\infty}_{M}
-submodules of
\mathfrak{X}_{M}
which is locally finitely generated. That is, each point
p\in M
is contained in an open neighbourhood
U
such that
\mathcal{D}(U)
is a finitely generated
C^{\infty}(U)
-module.

(b)

A
singular foliation
is a singular distribution
\mathcal{F}
on
M
which is involutive:

[\mathcal{F},\mathcal{F}]\subseteq\mathcal{F}.

(c)

A
singular Lie filtration
is a filtration

\mathfrak{X}_{M}=\mathcal{F}_{-r}\supseteq\mathcal{F}_{-r+1}\supseteq\cdots\supseteq\mathcal{F}_{0}

of
\mathfrak{X}_{M}
by singular distributions
\mathcal{F}_{i}
with the property that

[\mathcal{F}_{i},\mathcal{F}_{j}]\subseteq\mathcal{F}_{i+j}.

Examples 2.51
.

(a)

If
(M,N)
is a weighted pair then
\mathfrak{X}(M)_{(0)}
is a singular foliation and the filtration of vector fields (
20
) is a singular Lie filtration.

(b)

A filtered manifold is a manifold
M
together with a filtration of
TM
by subbundles

TM=F_{-r}\supseteq F_{-r+1}\supseteq\cdots\supseteq F_{-1}\supseteq 0

satisfying
[\Gamma(F_{i}),\Gamma(F)_{j}]\subseteq\Gamma(F_{i+j})
; this called a (regular)
Lie filtration
. Filtered manifolds appear in CR geometry, contact geometry, and parabolic geometry (see
[
CP15
,
SH18
,
VEY19
,
Yun18
]
and the references therein).

Definition 2.52

(
[
LM22
]
)
.

Let
M
be a smooth manifold.

(a)

Let
\mathcal{D}
be a singular distribution on
M
. A (closed) submanifold
N\subseteq M
is called
\mathcal{D}
-clean
if the function

\displaystyle N\to\mathbb{N},\quad p\mapsto\dim(\mathcal{D}_{p}+T_{p}N)

is a constant.

(b)

Let
\mathcal{F}_{\bullet}
be a singular Lie filtration on
M
. A (closed) submanifold
N\subseteq M
is called
\mathcal{F}_{\bullet}
-clean
if it is
\mathcal{F}_{i}
-clean for each
i
.

Examples 2.53
.

(a)

If
(M,N)
is a weighted pair and
\mathfrak{X}_{(\bullet)}
is the corresponding singular Lie filtration, then
N
is
\mathfrak{X}(M)_{(\bullet)}
-clean.

(b)

If
\mathcal{H}_{\bullet}
is a singular Lie filtration on
M
, then
\mathcal{F}_{\bullet}\times\mathcal{F}_{\bullet}
is a singular Lie filtration on
M\times M
. The diagonal
\Delta_{M}\subseteq M\times M
is
\mathcal{F}_{\bullet}\times\mathcal{F}_{\bullet}
-clean if and only if the
\mathcal{F}_{\bullet}
is a regular Lie filtration.

(c)

Let
\mathcal{D}
be the submodule of
\mathfrak{X}(\mathbb{R}^{2})
generated by the vector fields
x\frac{\partial}{\partial x}
and
\frac{\partial}{\partial y}
. Then the
x
-axis is
\mathcal{D}
-clean, whereas the
y
-axis is not.

The theorem relating singular Lie filtrations with weightings is the following.

Theorem 2.54

(
[
LM22
, Theorem 4.1]
)
.

Let
\mathcal{F}_{\bullet}
be a singular Lie filtration on a manifold
M
, and let
N\subseteq M
be an
\mathcal{F}_{\bullet}
-clean, closed submanifold. The filtration defined by
C^{\infty}_{M,(1)}=\mathcal{I}_{N}
and

C^{\infty}(U)_{(i)}=\{f\in C^{\infty}(U):X\in\mathcal{F}_{j}(U),\ 0<j<i\implies Xf\in C^{\infty}(U)_{(i-j)}\}

for
i>1
defines a weighting of
M
along
N
such that

(TM|_{N})_{(i)}=\mathcal{F}_{i}|_{N}+TN.

Before moving on, we record the following simple proposition about weighted morphisms, the proof of which is obvious from the construction of the weighting in
Theorem 2.54
.

Proposition 2.55
.

Let
\mathcal{F}_{\bullet}
be a singular Lie filtration on
M
and let
N\subseteq M
be an
\mathcal{F}_{\bullet}
-clean submanifold. Suppose that
(M^{\prime},N^{\prime})
is a weighted pair, and that
F:(M,N)\to(M^{\prime},N^{\prime})
is a map of pairs with the property that for all
X\in\mathcal{F}(U)_{(i)}
there exists some
Y\in\mathfrak{X}(M^{\prime})_{(i)}
such that
X\sim_{F}Y
. Then
F
is a weighted morphism with respect to the weighting of
M
defined in
Theorem 2.54
.

9
Inner Automorphisms of Weighted Manifolds

Let
(M,N)
be a weighted pair. We close this chapter by showing that vector fields
X\in\mathfrak{X}(M)_{(0)}
are exactly those whose flow is an automorphism of the weighted manifold. We begin with a lemma.

Lemma 2.56

(
[
AS09
,
GY19
,
LGLR22
, Propositions 1.6, 3.1, 1.6.9]
)
.

Let
\mathcal{D}
be a singular distribution on
M
and let
X\in\mathfrak{X}(M)
be a vector field whose flow
\phi_{t}
is defined for
|t|<\epsilon
. If
[X,\mathcal{D}]\subseteq\mathcal{D}
, then

\phi_{t}^{*}(\mathcal{D})\subseteq\mathcal{D}.

Proposition 2.57
.

Let
(M,N)
be a weighted pair, and let
X\in\mathfrak{X}(M)
be a vector field whose time flow
\phi_{t}
is defined for
|t|<\epsilon
. Then the following are equivalent:

(a)

X\in\mathfrak{X}(M)_{(0)}
,

(b)

[X,\mathfrak{X}(M)_{(i)}]\subseteq\mathfrak{X}(M)_{(i)}
for all
i
,

(c)

\phi_{t}
is a weighted diffeomorphism for all
|t|<\epsilon
.

Proof.

Recall that the filtration of
\mathfrak{X}(M)
is compatible with brackets in the sense that if
Y\in\mathfrak{X}(M)_{(i)}
and
Z\in\mathfrak{X}(M)_{(j)}
then
[Y,Z]\in\mathfrak{X}(M)_{(i+j)}
. Thus, (a) implies (b).

Now suppose that (b) holds. By applying
Lemma 2.56
to the singular distribution
\mathcal{D}=\mathfrak{X}(M)_{(i)}
, we have that

\phi_{t}^{*}(\mathfrak{X}(M)_{(i)})\subseteq\mathfrak{X}(M)_{(i)}

for all
|t|<\epsilon
. By
2.55
, this implies that
\phi_{t}
is a weighted diffeomorphism.

Finally, suppose that (c) holds and let
f\in C^{\infty}(M)_{(i)}
. Since

\phi_{t}^{*}Xf=\frac{d}{dt}\phi_{t}^{*}f\in C^{\infty}(M)_{(i)},

it follows that
Xf\in C^{\infty}(M)_{(i)}
, hence
X\in\mathfrak{X}(M)_{(0)}
.
∎

†
†
margin:

3

Linear weightings

10
Weighted Vector Bundles

We now turn our attention to weightings for vector bundles. In
[
LM23
]
, Loizides and Meinrenken define a
linear weighting
of a vector bundle
V
to be one for which scalar multiplication is a weighted morphism. We found this definition to be too restrictive in the sense the it excludes some basic examples. For instance, there is no natural weighting of the cotangent bundle of a weighted pair
(M,N)
even though the tangent bundle is canonically weighted. Moreover, since vector bundles can be defined in terms of their sheaf of sections, it is desirable to have a definition of linear weighting in these terms.

We define linear weightings in terms of a
\mathbb{Z}
-graded filtration

\cdots\supseteq\Gamma_{V,(i)}\supseteq\Gamma_{V,(i+1)}\supseteq\cdots

filtration of the sheaf of sections of a vector bundle, satisfying a local condition modelled after
Definition 2.1
. To ensure our definition contains naturally arising examples, we allow for the filtration of the sections of
V
to be non-trivial in both positive and negative degree. If
V\to M
is a linearly weighted vector bundle, then
M
is a weighted manifold and the constructions of the weighted normal bundle and the weighted deformation space still go through yielding vector bundles over the weighted normal bundle and weighted deformation space of
M
, respectively.

We let
\Gamma(V)
denote the (smooth) sections of
V
. If
W\to N
is a subbundle of
V\to M
then
\Gamma(V,W)=\{\sigma\in\Gamma(V):\sigma|_{N}\in W\}
.

10.1
Definition of linear weightings

A linear weighting of a vector space
V
is a filtration of
V
by subspaces. Therefore, we define a weighted vector bundle to be one which is locally the product of a weighted manifold with a filtered vector space. This is formalized as follows.

Let
(M,N)
be a weighted pair and
M\times\mathbb{R}^{k}\to M
the trivial bundle of rank
k
over
M
. A
vertical weight vector
for
M\times\mathbb{R}^{k}
is a
k
-tuple of integers
(v_{1},\dots,v_{k})\in\mathbb{Z}^{k}
. A choice of vertical weight vector determines a filtration of
\Gamma(M\times\mathbb{R}^{k})
by
C^{\infty}(M)
-submodules

\Gamma(M\times\mathbb{R}^{k})_{(i)}=\sum_{a=1}^{k}C^{\infty}(M)_{(i-v_{a})}\sigma_{a},

(22)

where
\sigma_{1},\dots,\sigma_{k}
is the standard basis for
\mathbb{R}^{k}
.

Definition 3.1
.

A
linear weighting
of a rank
k
vector bundle
V
over the weighted pair
(M,N)
is a
\mathbb{Z}
-graded filtration

\cdots\supseteq\Gamma_{V,(i)}\supseteq\Gamma_{V,(i+1)}\supseteq\cdots

of the sheaf of sections
\Gamma_{V}
by
C^{\infty}_{M}
-submodules such that for every point
p\in M
there exists an open neighbourhood
U\subseteq M
containing
p
and a frame
\sigma_{1},\dots,\sigma_{k}\in\Gamma(V|_{U})
such that
\Gamma(V|_{U})_{(i)}
is given by (
22
). The frame
\sigma_{a}
is called a
weighted frame
. We refer to a vector bundle with a linear weighting as a
weighted vector bundle
.

Remarks 3.2
.

(a)

If
v_{1},\dots,v_{k}
is the vertical weight sequence for
V
, then
\Gamma_{V,(i)}=\Gamma_{V}
for
i\leq\min_{a}\{v_{a}\}
.

(b)

By definition,
\Gamma_{V}
is a filtered module over the filtered algebra
C^{\infty}_{M}
. That is, for all
i,j\in\mathbb{Z}
,

C^{\infty}_{M,(i)}\cdot\Gamma_{V,(j)}\subseteq\Gamma_{V,(i+j)}.

(c)

Using that we are working in the
C^{\infty}
-category, we may take advantage of the existence of partitions of unity to avoid the use of sheaves, working instead with the filtration of global sections

\cdots\supseteq\Gamma(V)_{(i)}\supseteq\Gamma(V)_{(i+1)}\supseteq\cdots

Examples 3.3
.

(a)

If
M
is weighted along itself, then a filtration of
V

\cdots\supseteq V_{(i)}\supseteq V_{(i+1)}\supseteq\cdots

by subbundles
V_{i}\to M
via defines a linear weighting of
V
via correspondence

\Gamma(V)_{(i)}=\Gamma(V_{(i)}).

(b)

If
M
is a weighted manifold and
V
is a weighted vector space, then the trivial bundle
M\times V
is a weighted vector bundle.

(c)

Let
(M,N)
be a weighted pair and let

\cdots\supseteq\mathfrak{X}(M)_{(i)}\supseteq\mathfrak{X}(M)_{(i+1)}\supseteq\cdots

(23)

be the induced filtration of
\mathfrak{X}(M)
(see
Section 8
). If
x_{a}
is a local weighted coordinate system on
M
, then the local coordinate vector fields
\frac{\partial}{\partial x_{a}}
define a local weighted frame for
TM
. Thus, (
23
) is a linear weighting of
TM
. If
w_{1},\dots,w_{n}
are the weights for
M
, then the vertical weights for
TM
are given by
-w_{1},-w_{2},\dots,-w_{n}
.

(d)

With the same setting as in (c), let
\Omega^{1}(M)_{(i)}
denote the 1-forms
\omega\in\Omega^{1}(M)
on
M
with the property that

X\in\mathfrak{X}(M)_{(j)}\implies\langle\omega,X\rangle\in C^{\infty}(M)_{(i+j)}.

If
x_{a}
is a weighted coordinate system on
M
, then the differentials
\mathrm{d}x_{a}
form a weighted frame for
T^{*}M
. Thus,
T^{*}M
is a weighted vector bundle. If
w_{1},\dots,w_{n}
are the weights for
M
, then the vertical weights for
T^{*}M
are given by
w_{1},\dots,w_{n}
as well. Note that the map
\mathrm{d}:C^{\infty}(M)\to\Omega^{1}(M)
is filtration preserving.

(e)

Let
N\subseteq M
be a closed submanifold with vanishing ideal
\mathcal{I}_{N}
. The trivial weighting of
M
along
N
is given by order of vanishing,

C^{\infty}(M)_{(j)}=\mathcal{I}_{N}^{j}.

If
W\to N
is a subbundle of
V\to M
, then the filtration

\Gamma(V)_{(i)}=\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}\Gamma(V)&i\leq-1,\\
\Gamma(V,W)&i=0,\\
\mathcal{I}_{N}^{i}\cdot\Gamma(V)&i\geq 1,\end{array}\right.

determines a linear weighting of
V
. If
M
is trivially weighted along
N
, this recovers the tangent weighting of
TM
from the previous example for
W=TN

Recall from
Section 3
that a weighting of
M
along
N
determines a filtration of
TM|_{N}
be subbundles. The analogue of this in the context of linear weightings is the following.

Proposition 3.4
.

Let
(M,N)
be a weighted pair. A linear weighting of
V\to M
with vertical weights
v_{1},\dots,v_{k}
determines a filtration of
V|_{N}

\cdots\supseteq(V|_{N})_{(i)}\supseteq(V|_{N})_{(i+1)}\supseteq\cdots

by subbundles
(V|_{N})_{i}\to N
of rank
k_{i}=\#\{a:v_{a}\geq i\}
. In fact,
\Gamma((V|N)_{(i)})
is given by the image of
\Gamma(V)_{(i)}
in

\Gamma(V)/(\mathcal{I}_{N}\cdot\Gamma(V))=\Gamma(V|_{N})

under the quotient map.

Proof.

Let
p\in N
be contained in the open neighbourhood
U\subseteq M
, and let
\sigma_{a}
be a weighted frame for
V|_{U}
. Then the image of
\Gamma(V|_{U})_{(i)}
in the quotient
\Gamma(V|_{U})/(\mathcal{I}_{N\cap U}\cdot\Gamma(V|_{U}))
is freely generated by

\{\sigma_{a}|_{N\cap U}:v_{a}\geq i\}.\qed

Remark 3.5
.

This shows that if
M
is weighted along itself then a linear weighting of
V\to M
is
equivalent
to a filtration of
V
by subbundles, as in
3.3
(a). In particular, a
weighted vector space
is equivalent to a filtered vector space.

Examples 3.6
.

(a)

If
(M,N)
is a weighted pair and
TM
and
T^{*}M
are weighted as in
3.3
(c) and (d), then the filtrations of
TM|_{N}
and
T^{*}M|_{N}
are the ones defined in
Section 3
.

(b)

If
V\to M
is trivially weighted along
W\to N
as in
3.3
(e), then the filtration of
V|_{N}
is

V|_{N}\supseteq W\supseteq 0.

(c)

Let
\mathbb{R}
be given the trivial weighting along the origin, so that the coordinate
x
has weight
1
. Let
V=\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{R}^{2}\overset{\mathrm{pr}_{1}}{\longrightarrow}\mathbb{R}
be the trivial bundle of rank 2 over
\mathbb{R}
, and let
\sigma_{1},\sigma_{2}\in\Gamma(V)
be standard frame. The linear weightings

\displaystyle\Gamma(V)_{(i)}

\displaystyle=C^{\infty}(\mathbb{R})_{(i)}\cdot\sigma_{1}+C^{\infty}(\mathbb{R})_{(i+2)}\cdot\sigma_{2}

\displaystyle\Gamma(V)^{\prime}_{(i)}

\displaystyle=C^{\infty}(\mathbb{R})_{(i)}\cdot(\sigma_{1}+x\sigma_{2})+C^{\infty}(\mathbb{R})_{(i+2)}\cdot\sigma_{2}

determine the same filtration of
V_{0}=\{0\}\times\mathbb{R}^{2}
, but they are different weightings. Indeed,
\sigma_{1}+x\sigma_{2}\in\Gamma(V)^{\prime}_{(0)}
, but
\sigma_{1}+x\sigma_{2}\notin\Gamma(V)_{(0)}
. In particular, this example shows that the filtration of
V|_{N}
alone is not enough to recover the weighting of
V
.

10.2
Constructions

We now explain how various constructions with vector bundles work in the weighted setting.

(a)

(
Dual weighting)
The dual of a weighted vector bundle is linearly weighted by

\tau\in\Gamma(V^{*})_{(i)}\iff\forall\sigma\in\Gamma(V)_{(j)},\ \langle\tau,\sigma\rangle\in C^{\infty}(M)_{(i+j)}.

If
\sigma_{a}
is a weighted frame for
V|_{U}
then the corresponding dual frame
\tau_{a}
is a weighted frame for
V^{*}|_{U}
. In particular, if
v_{a}
are the vertical weights for
V
then
-v_{a}
are the vertical weights for
V^{*}
and
V=(V^{*})^{*}
as weighted vector bundles. Furthermore, the corresponding filtration of
V^{*}|_{N}
is given by

(V^{*}|_{N})_{(i)}=\mathrm{ann}((V|_{N})_{(-i+1)}).

This generalizes the weighting of
T^{*}M
defined in
3.3
(d).

(b)

(
Direct sums, tensor products, etc.)
If
V\to M
and
W\to M
are weighted vector bundles over the weighted pair
(M,N)
, then
V\oplus W
,
V\otimes W
,
\mathrm{Hom}(V,W)
,
\wedge^{n}V
, and
\mathrm{Sym}^{n}(V)
all inherit linear weightings in a canonical way.

For example, the linear weighting on
V\otimes W
is given by

\Gamma(V\otimes W)_{(k)}=\sum_{i+j=k}\Gamma(V)_{(i)}\otimes_{C^{\infty}(M)}\Gamma(W)_{(j)}.

The linear weighting on
\mathrm{Hom}(V,W)
is given by the identification
\mathrm{Hom}(V,W)\cong V^{*}\otimes W
. With respect to this weighting we have that, for all
i,j\in\mathbb{Z}
,

\Gamma(\mathrm{Hom}(V,W))_{(i)}\times\Gamma(V)_{(j)}\to\Gamma(W)_{(i+j)}.

(c)

(
Shifted weighting
) Given a linear weighting of
V\to M
we denote by
V[k]
the vector bundle
V
linearly weighted by

\Gamma(V[k])_{(i)}=\Gamma(V)_{(i+k)}.

Note that:

(i)

(V[k]|_{N})_{(i)}=(V|_{N})_{(i+k)}
, and

(ii)

V[k]^{*}=V^{*}[-k]
.

(d)

(
Pullbacks
) Suppose that
(M,N)
and
(M^{\prime},N^{\prime})
are weighted pairs and that
\varphi:M^{\prime}\to M
is a weighted morphism.

Proposition 3.7
.

If
V\to M
is a weighted vector bundle, then

\Gamma(\varphi^{*}V)_{(i)}=\sum_{j\geq 0}C^{\infty}(M^{\prime})_{(j)}\cdot\varphi^{*}\Gamma(V)_{(i-j)}.

(24)

defines a linear weighting of the pullback bundle
\varphi^{*}V\to M^{\prime}
.

Proof.

Let
U\subseteq M
be open and let
\sigma_{1},\dots,\sigma_{k}
be a weighted frame for
V|_{U}
. We claim that the pullbacks
\varphi^{*}\sigma_{a}\in\Gamma(\varphi^{*}(V|_{U}))
define a weighted frame for
\varphi^{*}(V|_{U})
. Indeed, we have that

\displaystyle\Gamma(\varphi^{*}(V)

\displaystyle|_{\varphi^{-1}(U)})_{(i)}=\sum_{j\geq 0}C^{\infty}(\varphi^{-1}(U))_{(j)}\cdot\varphi^{*}\Gamma(V|_{U})_{(i-j)}

\displaystyle=\sum_{a}\left(\sum_{j\geq 0}C^{\infty}(\varphi^{-1}(U))_{(j)}\cdot\varphi^{*}C^{\infty}(U)_{(i-j-v_{a})}\right)\varphi^{*}\sigma_{a}.

To complete the proof, we must show that

\sum_{j\geq 0}C^{\infty}(\varphi^{-1}(U))_{(j)}\cdot\varphi^{*}C^{\infty}(U)_{(i-j-v_{a})}=C^{\infty}(\varphi^{-1}(U))_{(i-v_{a})};

note that there is nothing to show if
i-v_{a}<0
. We have that
\varphi^{*}C^{\infty}(U)_{(i-j-v_{a})}\subseteq C^{\infty}(\varphi^{-1}(U))_{(i-j-v_{a})}
as
\varphi:M^{\prime}\to M
is a weighted morphism, hence the left hand side is contained in the right hand side. The other inclusion follows by putting
j=i-v_{a}
in the sum.
∎

10.3
Linear Weightings as a Filtration of Polynomial Functions

We now relate linear weighting to the weightings discussed in
Chapter 2
. For this, we replace the algebra of smooth functions on
M
with the algebra of
fibrewise polynomial
functions on the total space of
V
. Recall that the space of space of fibrewise polynomial functions on a vector bundle
V\to M
is the direct sum

C^{\infty}_{pol}(V)=\bigoplus_{n\geq 0}C^{\infty}_{[n]}(V),

where
C^{\infty}_{[n]}(V)
are the functions of homogeneity
n
. This perspective allows for a quick definition of the weighted normal bundle and weighted deformation bundles in
Section 11
and
Section 12
.

Theorem 3.8

(Polynomial Characterization of Linear Weightings)
.

Let
V
be a rank
k
vector bundle over the
m
-dimensional manifold
M
. There is a one to one correspondence between linear weightings of
V
and multiplicative filtrations

\cdots\supseteq C^{\infty}_{pol}(V)_{(i)}\supseteq C^{\infty}_{pol}(V)_{(i+1)}\supseteq\cdots

of the sheaf of polynomial functions on
V
with the following property: there exist tuples
(w_{1},\dots,w_{m})\in\mathbb{Z}^{m}_{\geq 0}
and
(v_{1},\dots,v_{k})\in\mathbb{Z}^{k}
such that for each
\xi\in V
, there is an open set
U\subseteq M
with
\xi\in V|_{U}
and vector bundle coordinates
x_{a}\in C^{\infty}_{[0]}(V|_{U})
,
p_{b}\in C^{\infty}_{[1]}(V|_{U})
such that
C^{\infty}_{pol}(V|_{U})_{(i)}
is generated as a
C^{\infty}(U)
-module by the monomials

x^{s}p^{t}=x_{1}^{s_{1}}\cdots x_{m}^{s_{m}}\cdot p_{1}^{t_{1}}\cdots p_{k}^{t_{k}}

(25)

such that
s\cdot w-t\cdot v=\sum_{i=1}^{m}s_{i}w_{1}-\sum_{i=1}^{k}t_{i}v_{i}\geq i
.

We refer to the coordinate system
x_{a},p_{b}
described in
Theorem 3.8
as
weighted vector bundle coordinates
. We stress that while the base coordinates
x_{a}
have non-negative weight, the fibre coordinates
y_{b}
can have both positive
and
negative weight.

Proof.

Suppose that
V
is linearly weighted. For each
n\geq 0
we will define a filtration of
C^{\infty}_{[n]}(V)
and define filtration of
C^{\infty}_{pol}(V)
by taking the direct sum. Given
n\geq 0
, recall that there is an identification

C^{\infty}_{[n]}(V)\cong\Gamma(\mathrm{Sym}^{n}(V^{*})).

(26)

Since
\mathrm{Sym}^{n}(V^{*})
inherits a linear weighting from the weighting of
V
(see
Section 10.2
(b)), we use the identification (
26
) to define the filtration of
C^{\infty}_{[n]}(V)
. Explicitly, for any open set
U\subseteq M
, elements of
C^{\infty}_{[n]}(V|_{U})_{(i)}
are functions
f\in C^{\infty}_{[n]}(V|_{U})
such that for any
\sigma\in\Gamma(V|_{U})_{(j)}
one has

f\circ\sigma\in C^{\infty}(U)_{(i+nj)}.

In particular,
C^{\infty}_{[0]}(V)_{(i)}=C^{\infty}(M)_{(i)}
and
C^{\infty}_{[1]}(V)_{(i)}=\Gamma(V^{*})_{(i)}
. Let

C^{\infty}_{pol}(V)_{(i)}=\bigoplus_{n\geq 0}C^{\infty}_{[n]}(V)_{(i)}.

To get weighted vector bundle coordinates, let
U\subseteq M
be an open subset over which
V|_{U}
is trivialized, let
x_{a}
be weighted coordinates on
U
and let
\sigma_{b}
be a weighted frame for
V|_{U}
. Let
\tau_{b}\in\Gamma(V^{*}|_{U})
denote the dual frame and
p_{b}\in C^{\infty}_{[1]}(V|_{U})
the corresponding linear coordinates. The
n
-fold symmetric products of the
\tau_{b}
form a weighted frame for
\mathrm{Sym}^{n}(V|_{U}^{*})
, hence the functions
x_{a},p_{b}
define the necessary vector bundle coordinate system. Note that if the section
\sigma_{b}
has weighted
v_{b}
, then the corresponding fibre coordinate
p_{b}
has weight
-v_{b}
.

Conversely, suppose that we are given a filtration

\cdots\supseteq C^{\infty}_{pol}(V)_{(i)}\supseteq C^{\infty}_{pol}(V)_{(i+1)}\supseteq\cdots

as described in the statement of the theorem. For
U\subseteq M
open, define

\displaystyle C^{\infty}(U)_{(i)}

\displaystyle=C^{\infty}_{[0]}(V|_{U})\cap C^{\infty}_{pol}(V|_{U})_{(i)}\quad\text{and}

(27)

\displaystyle\Gamma(V|_{U}^{*})

\displaystyle=C^{\infty}_{[1]}(V|_{U})\cap C^{\infty}_{pol}(V|_{U})_{(i)}.

(28)

The requirement that
C^{\infty}_{pol}(V|_{U})
be generated as a
C^{\infty}(U)
-module by terms of the form (
25
) ensures that (
27
) defines a weighting of
M
and (
28
) defines a linear weighting of
V^{*}
. By taking the dual weighting, we have thus also defined a linear weighting of
V
. These constructions are clearly inverse to one another, and so we have proved the theorem.
∎

Example 3.9
.

If
W\to N
is a vector subbundle of
V\to M
, then order of vanishing defines a linear weighting of
V
, which we refer to as the trivial weighting along
W
. This agrees with the linear weighting defined in
3.3
(c).

Remarks 3.10
.

(a)

One can use to conclusion of
Theorem 3.8
to define
graded bundle weightings
, using graded bundle coordinates in place of vector bundle coordinates.

(b)

Let
V\to M
be a linearly weighted vector bundle. Recall that the weighting of
V
determines a filtration

\cdots\supseteq(V|_{N})_{(i)}\supseteq(V|_{N})_{(i+1)}\supseteq\cdots

of
V|_{N}
by wide subbundles. In local vector bundle coordinates
x_{1},\dots,x_{m}\in C^{\infty}(U)
,
p_{1},\dots,p_{k}\in C^{\infty}_{[1]}(V|_{U})
,
(V|_{N\cap U})_{(i)}
is cut out by the equations

\displaystyle x_{a}=0

\displaystyle\text{ for }w_{a}>0,

\displaystyle p_{b}=0

\displaystyle\text{ for }v_{b}<i.

(c)

As a consequence of
Theorem 3.8
, one finds that a linear weighting of
V
naturally defines a filtration of
TV|_{N}
by subbundles. In terms of the canonical decomposition
TV|_{N}=TM|_{N}\oplus V|_{N}
, one has

(TV|_{N})_{(i)}=(TM|_{N})_{(i)}\oplus(V|_{N})_{(i)}.

(d)

We say that a linear weighting of
V\to M
is
concentrated in non-positive degree
if

(V|_{N})_{(i)}=0\quad\text{for}\quad i>0.

In this case,
Theorem 3.8
shows that a linear weighting is a weighting in the sense of
Definition 2.1
along the subbundle

W=(V|_{N})_{(0)},

and we say that
V
is linearly weighted along
W
. For example, if
(M,N)
is a weighted pair then the linear weighting of
TM
is concentrated in non-positive degree and
TM
is linearly weighted along
TN
.

Weighted subbundles

Now that we have a characterization of linear weightings in terms of polynomial functions, we can define weighted subbundles.

Definition 3.11
.

Let
V\to M
be a weighted vector bundle. A subbundle
W\to R
is a
weighted subbundle
if there exists a weighted atlas of subbundle coordinates for
W
.

That is,
W
is a weighted subbundle if and only if for every
w\in W
there exist weighted vector bundle coordinates
x_{a}\in C^{\infty}_{[0]}(V|_{U})
and
p_{b}\in C^{\infty}_{[1]}(V|_{U})
such that
W
is locally defined by the vanishing of a subset of the coordinates. Coordinates with this property are called
weighted subbundle coordinates
.

Examples 3.12
.

(a)

If
V
is a linearly weighted vector bundle over the weighted pair
(M,N)
, then
V|_{N}
is a weighted subbundle. Any system of weighted vector bundle coordinates are weighted subbundle coordinates for
V|_{N}
.

(b)

If
V
is linearly weighted vector space then any subspace is a weighted subspace (cf.
2.7
(e)).

Proposition 3.13
.

Let
V\to M
be a linearly weighted vector bundle. If
W\to R
is a weighted subbundle,

(a)

R
is a weighted submanifold of
(M,N)
,

(b)

W
inherits a linear weighting and the filtration of
W|_{R\cap N}
is given by

(W|_{R\cap N})_{(i)}=W\cap(V|_{N})_{(i)}.

Proof.

One proceeds as in the proof of
2.9
using weighted subbundle coordinates.
∎

10.4
Weighted vector bundle morphisms

We now discuss morphisms of weighted vector bundles. The easiest definition uses the characterization of linear weightings in terms of polynomial functions.

Definition 3.14
.

A vector bundle morphism
\varphi:V\to V^{\prime}
between linearly weighted vector bundles
V\to M
and
V^{\prime}\to M^{\prime}
is called
weighted
if
\varphi^{*}C^{\infty}_{pol}(V^{\prime})_{(i)}\subseteq C^{\infty}_{pol}(V)_{(i)}
for all
i\in\mathbb{Z}
.

Recall that a vector bundle map
\varphi:V\to V^{\prime}
induces a module map
\varphi^{*}:\Gamma((V^{\prime})^{*})\to\Gamma(V^{*})
. In terms of this perspective, one has the following characterization of weighted vector bundle morphisms.

Proposition 3.15
.

Let
\varphi:V\to V^{\prime}
be a vector bundle morphism between linearly weighted vector bundles
V\to M
and
V^{\prime}\to M^{\prime}
, respectively. Then
\varphi
is weighted if and only if

(a)

the base map
\varphi_{M}:M\to M^{\prime}
is weighted and

(b)

for all
i\in\mathbb{Z}
,
\varphi^{*}:\Gamma((V^{\prime})^{*})_{(i)}\to\Gamma(V^{*})_{(i)}
.

Proof.

Suppose that
\varphi:V\to V^{\prime}
is a weighted vector bundle morphism. Then,

\displaystyle\varphi_{M}^{*}C^{\infty}(M^{\prime})_{(i)}

\displaystyle=\varphi_{M}^{*}(C^{\infty}_{pol}(V^{\prime})_{(i)}\cap C^{\infty}_{[0]}(W))

\displaystyle\subseteq C^{\infty}_{pol}(V)_{(i)}\cap C^{\infty}_{[0]}(V)=C^{\infty}(M)_{(i)},

whence
\varphi_{M}:M\to M^{\prime}
is weighted. Similarly, for any
\sigma\in\Gamma((V^{\prime})^{*})
, let
f_{\sigma}\in C^{\infty}_{[1]}(V)
be the corresponding function under the identification
C^{\infty}_{[1]}(V)=\Gamma(V^{*})
. Then

\varphi^{*}(\sigma)=\varphi^{*}f_{\sigma}\in C^{\infty}_{[1]}(V)_{(i)}=\Gamma(V^{*})_{(i)}.

The converse follows by a similar argument, using that
\varphi^{*}:C^{\infty}_{pol}(V^{\prime})\to C^{\infty}_{pol}(V)
is an algebra morphism and
C^{\infty}_{pol}(V)_{(i)}
is locally generated as a filtered algebra by
C^{\infty}(M)
and
C^{\infty}_{[1]}(V)=\Gamma(V^{*})
.
∎

Example 3.16
.

If
f:M\to M^{\prime}
is a weighted morphism between weighted pairs
(M,N)
and
(M^{\prime},N^{\prime})
, then the tangent map
Tf:TM\to TM^{\prime}
is a weighted vector bundle morphism.

In the case that
V
and
V^{\prime}
are vector bundles over a common base the definition of a weighted vector bundle morphism reduces to the one that one might expect.

Proposition 3.17
.

Suppose that that
V
and
V^{\prime}
and linearly weighted vector bundles over a common weighted pair
(M,N)
and
\varphi:V\to V^{\prime}
is a vector bundle morphism covering the identity. Then
\varphi
is a weighted vector bundle morphism if and only if
\varphi(\Gamma(V)_{(i)})\subseteq\Gamma(V^{\prime})_{(i)}
for all
i
.

Proof.

Suppose that
\varphi^{*}(\Gamma((V^{\prime})^{*})_{(i)})\subseteq\Gamma(V^{*}_{(i)})
, and let
\sigma\in\Gamma(V)_{(i)}
. Let
\tau\in\Gamma((V^{\prime})^{*})_{(j)}
be arbitrary, and note that

\langle\tau,\varphi(\sigma)\rangle=\langle\varphi^{*}(\tau),\sigma\rangle\in C^{\infty}(M)_{(i+j)},

since
\varphi^{*}(\tau)\in\Gamma(V^{*})_{(j)}
; here the angular brackets denote the pairing between a vector bundle and its dual. This implies that
\varphi(\sigma)\in\Gamma(V^{\prime})_{(i)}
, proving one inclusion. The opposite inclusion follows similarly.
∎

We state the following observation, whose proof is obvious, which allows us to apply the results of
Section 5
to vector bundle morphisms.

Lemma 3.18
.

Let
V\to M
and
V^{\prime}\to M^{\prime}
be weighted vector bundles, and
k\in\mathbb{Z}
. A vector bundle morphism
\varphi:V\to V^{\prime}
is a weighted vector bundle morphism if and only if
\varphi:V[k]\to V^{\prime}[k]
is weighted.

Note that by taking
k
to be sufficiently large, the weighting of
V[k]
is concentrated in negative degree. In particular,
V[k]
is weighted along a closed submanifold
N\subseteq M
in the sense of
Chapter 2
. In light of
Lemma 3.18
, all of the definitions and results in
Section 5
hold for vector bundle morphisms, replacing
TM|_{N}
with
TV|_{N}=V|_{N}\oplus TM|_{N}
. Let us illustrate this with the following result (cf.
Theorem 2.32
).

Theorem 3.19
.

Suppose that
V\to M
and
V^{\prime}\to M^{\prime}
are weighted vector bundles and
\varphi:V\to V^{\prime}
is a vector bundle morphism whose base map is a smooth map of pairs
(M,N)\to(M^{\prime},N^{\prime})
. Then
\varphi
is a weighted VB-morphism if and only if

(a)

the graph
\Gamma(\varphi)\subseteq V^{\prime}\times V
is a weighted subbundle and

(b)

the maps
V|_{N}\to V^{\prime}|_{N^{\prime}}
and
TM|_{N}\to TM^{\prime}|_{N^{\prime}}
are filtration preserving.

Proof.

By replacing
V
and
V^{\prime}
with
V[k]
and
V^{\prime}[k]
for sufficiently large
k
, we may assume the weightings are concentrated in negative degree. By
Theorem 2.32
,
\varphi:V\to V^{\prime}
is a weighted morphism if and only if its graph is a weighted subbundle of
V^{\prime}\times V
and the map
T\varphi|_{N}:TV|_{N}\to TV^{\prime}|_{N^{\prime}}
is filtration preserving. With respect to the canonical decomposition
TV|_{N}=TM|_{N}\oplus V|_{N}
, the map
T\varphi|_{N}
decomposes as
T\varphi|_{N}=T\varphi_{M}|_{N}\oplus\varphi|_{N}
hence
T\varphi|_{N}
is filtration preserving if and only if the maps
V|_{N}\to V^{\prime}|_{N^{\prime}}
and
TM|_{N}\to TM^{\prime}|_{N^{\prime}}
are filtration preserving. Therefore,
\varphi:V\to V^{\prime}
is a weighted morphism.
∎

11
The Weighted Normal Bundle

We now define the weighted normal bundle of a linearly weighted vector bundle. Since we have defined weightings in terms of filtrations of fibrewise polynomial functions on
V
, much of the discussion in
Section 6
carries over, almost verbatim, to linear weightings.

11.1
Definition

Let
V\to M
be a linearly weighted vector bundle. Let
\mathrm{gr}(C^{\infty}_{pol}(V))
be the graded algebra associated to the filtered algebra, with graded components given by

\mathrm{gr}(C^{\infty}_{pol}(V))_{i}=C^{\infty}_{pol}(V)_{(i)}/C^{\infty}_{pol}(V)_{(i+1)}.

Definition 3.20
.

The
weighted normal bundle
of the linearly weighted vector bundle
V\to M
is the character spectrum

\nu_{\mathcal{W}}(V)=\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathrm{alg}}(\mathrm{gr}(C^{\infty}_{pol}(V)),\mathbb{R})

Given a function
f\in C^{\infty}_{pol}(V)_{(i)}
, let
f^{[i]}
denote the class of
f
in
\mathrm{gr}(C^{\infty}_{pol}(V))_{i}
. We think of
f^{[i]}
as a function on
\nu_{\mathcal{W}}(V)
, defined by evaluation

\displaystyle f^{[i]}:\nu_{\mathcal{W}}(V)\to\mathbb{R},\quad\varphi\mapsto\varphi(f^{[i]}).

Let
\pi:V\to M
and
\kappa_{\lambda}:V\to V
denote the bundle projection and scalar multiplication by
\lambda\in\mathbb{R}
, respectively. Since these maps are filtration preserving, they induce maps

\nu_{\mathcal{W}}(\pi):\nu_{\mathcal{W}}(V)\to\nu_{\mathcal{W}}(M,N)\quad\text{and}\quad\nu_{\mathcal{W}}(\kappa_{\lambda}):\nu_{\mathcal{W}}(V)\to\nu_{\mathcal{W}}(V),

which we take to be our vector bundle projection and scalar multiplication, respectively, in the following theorem.

Theorem 3.21
.

Let
V\to M
be a weighted vector bundle.

(a)

The weighted normal bundle
\nu_{\mathcal{W}}(V)
has the unique structure of a
C^{\infty}
-vector bundle over
\nu_{\mathcal{W}}(M,N)
of rank equal to that of
V
, such that for all
n\geq 0

\mathrm{gr}(C^{\infty}_{[n]}(V))\subseteq C^{\infty}_{[n]}(\nu_{\mathcal{W}}(V)).

(b)

Given weighted vector bundle coordinates
x_{a}
and
p_{b}
on
V|_{U}
, the functions
x_{a}^{[w_{a}]}
,
p_{b}^{[v_{b}]}
serve as vector bundle coordinates on
\nu_{\mathcal{W}}(V|_{U})=\nu_{\mathcal{W}}(V)|_{\nu_{\mathcal{W}}(U,U\cap N)}
.

(c)

This construction is functorial: any weighted vector bundle morphism
\varphi:V\to W
between linearly weighted vector bundles defines a vector bundle morphism
\nu_{\mathcal{W}}(\varphi):\nu_{\mathcal{W}}(V)\to\nu_{\mathcal{W}}(W)
.

The proof mirrors that of
[
LM23
, Theorem 4.2]
. We include it for completeness and to explain the use of polynomial functions.

Proof.

Let
U\subseteq M
and let
x_{1},\dots,x_{m},p_{1},\dots,p_{k}\in C^{\infty}(V|_{U})
be a system of weighted vector bundle coordinates. We will show that the homogeneous approximations
y_{a}=x_{a}^{[w_{a}]}
,
q_{b}=p_{b}^{[-v_{b}]}
serve as vector bundle coordinates on
\nu_{\mathcal{W}}(\pi)^{-1}(\nu_{\mathcal{W}}(U,U\cap N))=\nu_{\mathcal{W}}(V|_{U})
. By
Theorem 2.40
, the map

(y_{1},\dots,y_{m}):\nu_{\mathcal{W}}(U,U\cap N)\to\mathbb{R}^{m}

is a diffeomorphism onto an open set
\overline{U}\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{m}
. Since
\mathrm{gr}(C^{\infty}_{pol}(V|_{U}))
is generated as an algebra over
\mathrm{gr}(C^{\infty}(U))
by the elements
q_{1},\dots,q_{k}
it follows that the map

(y_{1},\dots,y_{m},q_{1},\dots,q_{k}):\nu_{\mathcal{W}}(\pi)^{-1}(\nu_{\mathcal{W}}(U,U\cap N))\to\mathbb{R}^{m+k}

is a bijection onto an open set of the form
\overline{U}\times\mathbb{R}^{k}
. This defines a smooth structure on
\nu_{\mathcal{W}}(V|_{U})
. We now show that if
f\in C^{\infty}_{pol}(V|_{U})_{(i)}
, then
f^{[i]}:\nu_{\mathcal{W}}(V|_{U})\to\mathbb{R}
is smooth. We may assume that
f(x,p)=g(x)p_{1}^{s_{1}}\cdots p_{k}^{s_{k}}
with
g\in C^{\infty}(U)_{(j)}
,
s\cdot v=\sum s_{b}v_{b}=i-j
, and
\sum_{b}s_{b}=n
, in which case

f^{[i]}(y,q)=g^{[j]}(y)q_{1}^{s_{1}}\cdots q_{k}^{s_{k}}

which is evidently smooth and homogeneous of degree
n
with respect to the scalar multiplication
\nu_{\mathcal{W}}(\kappa_{t})
. In particular, if
y^{\prime}_{1},\dots,y^{\prime}_{k}
is a different choice of weighted linear coordinates on
V|_{U}
then
(y^{\prime}_{b})^{[-v_{b}]}
is smooth as a function of
y_{a}
and
q_{b}
. The uniqueness assertion and the claim about functoriality are clear.
∎

Remark 3.22
.

This proof shows that if
[f_{ab}]
is the transition matrix for
V|_{U}
corresponding to two weighted vector bundle coordinate systems, then
f_{ab}\in C^{\infty}(U)_{(v_{b}-v_{a})}
and the transition matrix for
\nu_{\mathcal{W}}(V|_{U})
is given by
[f_{ab}^{[v_{b}-v_{a}]}]\in C^{\infty}(\nu_{\mathcal{W}}(U,U\cap N),\mathrm{GL}(\mathbb{R}^{k}))
.

Examples 3.23
.

(a)

Suppose that
W\to N
is a subbundle of
V\to M
. If
V
is given the trivial weighting along
W
, then

\nu_{\mathcal{W}}(V)=\nu(V,W),

as a vector bundle over
\nu(M,N)
.

(b)

If
V
is linearly weighted by a filtration of wide subbundles

V=V_{(-r)}\supseteq V_{(-r+1)}\supseteq\cdots\supseteq V_{(q)}\supseteq 0,

as in
3.3
(a), then
\nu_{\mathcal{W}}(V)=\mathrm{gr}(V)\to M
. In particular, if
V=V_{(-r)}\oplus\cdots\oplus V_{(q)}
is a graded vector bundle, then
\nu_{\mathcal{W}}(V)=V
.

(c)

If
(M,N)
is a weighted pair and
TM
is linearly weighted as in
3.3
(b), then

\nu_{\mathcal{W}}(TM)=T\nu_{\mathcal{W}}(M,N).

Remark 3.24
.

The weighted normal bundle is invariant under shifting the weighting: for any
k\in\mathbb{Z}
one has

\nu_{\mathcal{W}}(V[k])=\nu_{\mathcal{W}}(V).

In particular, shifting the weighting of
V
so that it is concentrated in negative degrees, we can see that the weighted normal bundle as a bundle of homogeneous spaces of nilpotent Lie groups (cf.
[
LM23
, Proposition 7.7]
.)

11.2
Action of
\mathbb{R}^{\times}

Let
\mathbb{R}^{\times}
act on
\mathrm{gr}(C^{\infty}_{pol}(V))_{i}
by multiplication by
t^{i}
. This defines a smooth action
\alpha:\mathbb{R}^{\times}\times\nu_{\mathcal{W}}(V)\to\nu_{\mathcal{W}}(V)
on the weighted normal bundle which commutes with the vector bundle multiplication and extends the action of
\mathbb{R}^{\times}
on
\nu_{\mathcal{W}}(M,N)
described in
Section 6.2
; note in particular that since we are allowing the fibre coordinates to have negative weights multiplication by zero is
not
defined, so
\nu_{\mathcal{W}}(V)
fails to be a graded bundle in general. As in
Section 6.2
, given any
f\in C^{\infty}_{pol}(V)_{(i)}
the function
f^{[i]}\in C^{\infty}(\nu_{\mathcal{W}}(V))
is homogeneous of degree
i
. Letting
C^{\infty}_{[i,j]}(\nu_{\mathcal{W}}(V))
denote the functions with are homogeneous of degree
i
with respect to the vector bundle multiplication and homogeneous of degree
j
with respect to the
\mathbb{R}^{\times}
-action, we have that

C^{\infty}_{[i,j]}(\nu_{\mathcal{W}}(V))=\mathrm{gr}(C^{\infty}_{[i]}(V))_{j}.

Example 3.25
.

Suppose that
V=V_{(-r)}\oplus\cdots\oplus V_{(q)}\to M
is a graded vector bundle over
M
.With respect to the identification
\nu_{\mathcal{W}}(V)=V
explained in
3.23
(b), we have that

\alpha_{\lambda}(v_{-r},\dots,v_{q})=(\lambda^{r}v_{-r},\dots,\lambda^{-q}v_{q}).

11.3
Sections of the weighted normal bundle

Recall from
Section 6.2
that

\mathrm{gr}(C^{\infty}(M))=C^{\infty}_{pol}(\nu_{\mathcal{W}}(M,N)).

In this section, we are going to define a canonical identification

\mathrm{gr}(\Gamma(V))\to\Gamma_{pol}(\nu_{\mathcal{W}}(V))

of graded
\mathrm{gr}(C^{\infty}(M))
-modules, where

\mathrm{gr}(\Gamma(V))=\bigoplus_{i\in\mathbb{Z}}\Gamma(V)_{(i)}/\Gamma(V)_{(i+1)}

and
\Gamma_{pol}(\nu_{\mathcal{W}}(V))
are the
polynomial
sections of
\nu_{\mathcal{W}}(V)
, defined as follows. For
i\in\mathbb{Z}
, let
\Gamma_{[i]}(\nu_{\mathcal{W}}(V))
denote the sections of
\nu_{\mathcal{W}}(V)
which are homogeneous of degree
i
, i.e. sections
\sigma\in\Gamma(\nu_{\mathcal{W}}(V))
such that

\alpha_{\lambda,*}(\sigma)=\alpha_{\lambda}\circ\sigma\circ\alpha_{\lambda^{-1}}=\lambda^{i}\sigma.

Then

\Gamma_{pol}(\nu_{\mathcal{W}}(V))=\bigoplus_{i\in\mathbb{Z}}\Gamma_{[i]}(\nu_{\mathcal{W}}(V)).

Before we begin, recall that

\nu_{\mathcal{W}}(M,N)=\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathrm{alg}}(\mathrm{gr}(C^{\infty}(M)),\mathbb{R})

and that for any
\sigma\in\Gamma(V)_{(i)}
and
f\in C^{\infty}_{[n]}(V)_{(j)}
one has
f\circ\sigma\in C^{\infty}(M)_{(j+ni)}
. This motivates the following definition.

Definition 3.26
.

Given
\sigma\in\Gamma(V)_{(i)}
, the
i
-th homogeneous approximation
is the map
\sigma^{[i]}:\nu_{\mathcal{W}}(M,N)\to\nu_{\mathcal{W}}(V)
defined by

(\sigma^{[i]}(\varphi))(f^{[j]})=\varphi((f\circ\sigma)^{[j+ni]}),

(29)

where
\varphi\in\nu_{\mathcal{W}}(M,N)
and
f\in C^{\infty}_{[n]}(V)_{(j)}
.

Example 3.27
.

Suppose that
V=V_{(-r)}\oplus\cdots\oplus V_{(q)}\to M
is a graded vector bundle over
M
. A section
\sigma\in\Gamma(V)
has filtration degree
-i
if and only if it can be written as a sum

\sigma=\sigma_{-i}+\sigma_{-i+1}+\cdots+\sigma_{q},\quad\text{where}\quad\sigma_{-j}\in\Gamma(V_{(-j)}).

Then
\nu_{\mathcal{W}}(V)=V
and
\sigma^{[-i]}=\sigma_{-i}
with respect to this identification. Note that
\sigma^{[-i]}\in\Gamma_{[i]}(\nu_{\mathcal{W}}(V))
, by
Example 3.25
.

Lemma 3.28
.

For
\sigma\in\Gamma(V)_{(i)}
, the
i
-th homogeneous approximation is a smooth section of
\nu_{\mathcal{W}}(V)
which is homogeneous of degree
-i
and which depends only on the class of
\sigma
in
\Gamma(V)_{(i)}/\Gamma(V)_{(i+1)}
. Moreover, for any
f\in C^{\infty}_{[n]}(V)_{(j)}
and
g\in C^{\infty}(M)_{(k)}
one has

\displaystyle\begin{split}f^{[j]}\circ\sigma^{[i]}&=(f\circ\sigma)^{[j+ni]}\in C^{\infty}(\nu_{\mathcal{W}}(M,N))\quad\text{and}\\
g^{[k]}\sigma^{[i]}&=(g\sigma)^{[i+k]}\in\Gamma(\nu_{\mathcal{W}}(V)).\end{split}

(30)

Proof.

See
Appendix 10.A
.
∎

This establishes a map
\mathrm{gr}(\Gamma(V))\to\Gamma_{pol}(\nu_{\mathcal{W}}(V))
. By abusing notation, given
\sigma\in\Gamma(V)_{(i)}
we denote both its class in
\Gamma(V)_{(i)}/\Gamma(V)_{(i+1)}
and its
i
-homogeneous approximation by
\sigma^{[i]}
.

Theorem 3.29
.

If
\sigma_{b}
is a weighted frame for
V|_{U}
, then the homogeneous approximations
\sigma_{b}^{[v_{b}]}
define a frame for
\nu_{\mathcal{W}}(V|_{U})=\nu_{\mathcal{W}}(V)|_{\nu_{\mathcal{W}}(U,U\cap N)}
. In particular, the map

\displaystyle\mathrm{gr}(\Gamma(V))\to\Gamma_{pol}(\nu_{\mathcal{W}}(V))

(31)

is an isomorphism of graded
\mathrm{gr}(C^{\infty}(M))
-modules and

\Gamma(\nu_{\mathcal{W}}(V))=C^{\infty}(\nu_{\mathcal{W}}(M,N))\otimes_{\mathrm{gr}(C^{\infty}(M))}\mathrm{gr}(\Gamma(V)).

Proof.

Let
p_{b}\in C^{\infty}_{[1]}(V|_{U})_{(-v_{b})}
be the linear vector bundle coordinates defined by the frame
\sigma_{a}
and note that for any non-zero
\varphi_{0}\in\nu_{\mathcal{W}}(U,U\cap N)
one has

\displaystyle\begin{split}\sigma_{b}^{[v_{b}]}(\varphi_{0})\left(p_{a}^{[-v_{a}]}\right)=\varphi_{0}\left((p_{a}\circ\sigma_{b}))^{[v_{b}-v_{a}]}\right)=\left\{\begin{array}[]{cc}1&\text{if $a=b$}\\
0&\text{else,}\end{array}\right.\end{split}

which shows that
\sigma_{a}^{[v_{a}]}
form a linearly independent set. In particular, this shows that the map (
31
) is injective.

Since
\mathrm{gr}(C^{\infty}_{pol}(V|_{U}))
is generated as an algebra over
\mathrm{gr}(C^{\infty}(U))
by the elements
p_{b}^{[-v_{b}]}
, any
\varphi\in\nu_{\mathcal{W}}(V|_{U})
is determined by its value on
\mathrm{gr}(C^{\infty}(U))
and
p_{b}^{[-v_{b}]}
,
b=1,\dots,k
. In particular, if
\nu_{\mathcal{W}}(\pi)(\varphi)=\varphi_{0}
then

\varphi=\sum_{b}\varphi\left(p_{b}^{[-v_{b}]}\right)\sigma_{b}^{[v_{b}]}(\varphi_{0}),

so
\sigma_{b}^{[v_{b}]}
spans as well.

To see that (
31
) is an isomorphism, it suffices to show that it is surjective as we have already established injectivity. Suppose that
\sigma\in\Gamma(\nu_{\mathcal{W}}(V|_{U}))
can be written as
\sigma=\sum_{b}f_{b}\sigma_{b}^{[v_{b}]}
. The pairing

\Gamma(\nu_{\mathcal{W}}(V))\otimes\Gamma(\nu_{\mathcal{W}}(V^{*}))\to C^{\infty}(\nu_{\mathcal{W}}(M,N))

respects homogeneity, hence

\sigma\in\Gamma_{[i]}(\nu_{\mathcal{W}}(V|_{U}))\iff\quad f_{b}\in C^{\infty}_{[i-v_{b}]}(\nu_{\mathcal{W}}(U,N\cap U))

for
b=1,\dots,k
. This implies that
\mathrm{gr}(\Gamma(V))\to\Gamma_{pol}(\nu_{\mathcal{W}}(V))
is surjective, which completes the proof.
∎

11.4
Weighted Subbundles

Recall that a subbundle
W\to R
of a linearly weighted vector bundle
V\to M
is called a weighted subbundle if there exist a weighted atlas of subbundle coordinates for
W
. In this case, it is explained in
Section 10.3
that
W
inherits a linear weighting.

Proposition 3.30
.

If
W\to R
is a weighted subbundle of
V\to M
, then
\nu_{\mathcal{W}}(W)
is a subbundle of
\nu_{\mathcal{W}}(V)
.

Proof.

If
x_{a},p_{b}
are weighted subbundle coordinates for
W|_{R\cap U}
, then the homogeneous approximations
x_{a}^{[w_{a}]},p_{b}^{[-v_{b}]}\in C^{\infty}(\nu_{\mathcal{W}}(V|_{U}))
define subbundle coordinates for
\nu_{\mathcal{W}}(W|_{R\cap U})
, as in
2.42
.
∎

Let
(V|_{N})_{(i)}\to N
be the filtration of
V|_{N}
defined by the linear weighting (see
3.4
).
3.30
allows us to describe the weighted normal bundle of
V
in terms of the associated graded bundle

\mathrm{gr}(V|_{N})=\bigoplus_{i}(V|_{N})_{(i)}/(V|_{N})_{(i+1)}.

Corollary 3.31
.

Suppose that
V\to M
is linearly weighted. If
\pi:\nu_{\mathcal{W}}(M,N)\to N
denotes the graded bundle projection, then one has a (non-canonical) identification

\nu_{\mathcal{W}}(V)\cong\pi^{*}\mathrm{gr}(V|_{N}).

Proof.

Recall that
V|_{N}
is a weighted subbundle of
V
and that the induced weighting of
V|_{N}
is given by the filtration
(V|_{N})_{(i)}
. As in
3.23
(b), we have

\nu_{\mathcal{W}}(V|_{N})=\mathrm{gr}(V|_{N}).

On the other hand
\nu_{\mathcal{W}}(V|_{N})
is a subbundle of
\nu_{\mathcal{W}}(V)|_{N}
of equal rank, and so
\nu_{\mathcal{W}}(V|_{N})=\nu_{\mathcal{W}}(V)|_{N}
. Since
\pi:\nu_{\mathcal{W}}(M,N)\to N
is a smooth homotopy inverse of the inclusion
\iota:N\hookrightarrow\nu_{\mathcal{W}}(M,N)
it follows that

\nu_{\mathcal{W}}(V)\cong\pi^{*}\iota^{*}\nu_{\mathcal{W}}(V)=\pi^{*}(\nu_{\mathcal{W}}(V)|_{N})=\pi^{*}\mathrm{gr}(V|_{N}),

as claimed.
∎

Example 3.32
.

If
V\to M
is given the trivial weighting along the subbundle
W\to R
, then this proposition amounts to the identification

\nu(V,W)\cong\pi^{*}(V|_{N}/W\oplus W)

as vector bundles over
\pi:\nu(M,N)\to N
.

12
The Weighted Deformation Bundle

Let
V\to M
be a linearly weighted vector bundle. As explained above, the weighting of
M
determines a weighted deformation space
\delta_{\mathcal{W}}(M,N)
which admits a set-theoretic decomposition

\delta_{\mathcal{W}}(M,N)=\nu_{\mathcal{W}}(M,N)\sqcup(M\times\mathbb{R}^{\times}).

The purpose of this section is to explain how this construction carries over to the context of linearly weighted vector bundles, yielding a
weighted deformation bundle

\delta_{\mathcal{W}}(V)
which is itself a vector bundle over
\delta_{\mathcal{W}}(M,N)
. Since many of the proofs in this section are very similar to those in the previous section we will omit them for brevity.

12.1
Definitions

We use the same definition for the weighted deformation bundle as in
Section 7
.

Definition 3.33
.

The
weighted deformation bundle
of the linearly weighted vector bundle
V\to(M,N)
is the character spectrum

\delta_{\mathcal{W}}(V)=\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathrm{alg}}(\mathrm{Rees}(C^{\infty}_{pol}(V)),\mathbb{R})

Given
f\in C^{\infty}_{pol}(V)_{(i)}
, let
\widetilde{f}^{[i]}:\delta_{\mathcal{W}}(V)\to\mathbb{R}
be as in
Section 7
and
\pi_{\delta}=\widetilde{1}^{[-1]}:\delta_{\mathcal{W}}(V)\to\mathbb{R}
. If
\pi:V\to M
and
\kappa_{\lambda}:V\to V
denote the vector bundle projection and scalar multiplication by
\lambda\in\mathbb{R}
, respectively, it follows that we get maps

\delta_{\mathcal{W}}(\pi):\delta_{\mathcal{W}}(V)\to\delta_{\mathcal{W}}(M,N)\quad\text{and}\quad\delta_{\mathcal{W}}(\kappa_{\lambda}):\delta_{\mathcal{W}}(V)\to\delta_{\mathcal{W}}(V).

As before, these give
\delta_{\mathcal{W}}(V)
the structure of a family of vector spaces over
\delta_{\mathcal{W}}(M,N)
. The following can be proved by the same argument as
[
LM23
, Theorem 5.1]
(cf.
Theorem 2.40
and
[
LM23
, Theorem 4.2]
).

Theorem 3.34
.

Let
V\to M
be a weighted vector bundle.

(a)

The weighted deformation bundle has the unique structure of a
C^{\infty}
-vector bundle over
\delta_{\mathcal{W}}(M,N)
of rank equal to that of
V
over
\delta_{\mathcal{W}}(M,N)
such that
\pi_{\delta}:\delta_{\mathcal{W}}(V)\to\mathbb{R}
is a surjective submersion and that for all
n\geq 0

\mathrm{Rees}(C^{\infty}_{[n]}(V))\subseteq C^{\infty}_{[n]}(\delta_{\mathcal{W}}(V)).

(b)

Given weighted vector bundle coordinates
x_{a}
and
p_{b}
on
V|_{U}
, the functions
\widetilde{x_{a}}^{[w_{a}]}
,
\widetilde{p_{b}}^{[-v_{b}]}
serve as vector bundle coordinates on
\delta_{\mathcal{W}}(V|_{U})=\delta_{\mathcal{W}}(V)|_{\delta_{\mathcal{W}}(U,U\cap N)}
.

(c)

This construction is functorial: any weighted vector bundle morphism
\varphi:V\to W
between linearly weighted vector bundles defines a vector bundle morphism
\delta_{\mathcal{W}}(\varphi):\delta_{\mathcal{W}}(V)\to\delta_{\mathcal{W}}(W)
.

As before, the surjective submersion
\pi_{\delta}:\delta_{\mathcal{W}}(V)\to\mathbb{R}
defines a decomposition
\delta_{\mathcal{W}}(V)=\nu_{\mathcal{W}}(V)\sqcup(V\times\mathbb{R}^{\times})
. The following diagram commutes

\textstyle{\delta_{\mathcal{W}}(V)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\delta_{\mathcal{W}}(\pi)}
\scriptstyle{\pi_{\delta}}
\textstyle{\delta_{\mathcal{W}}(M,N)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\pi_{\delta}}
\textstyle{\mathbb{R}}

so we can think of
\delta_{\mathcal{W}}(V)
as a family of vector bundles over
\delta_{\mathcal{W}}(M,N)
, with

\delta_{\mathcal{W}}(V)|_{\pi_{\delta}^{-1}(t)}=\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}V&t\neq 0,\\
\nu_{\mathcal{W}}(V)&t=0.\end{array}\right.

(32)

Examples 3.35
.

(a)

If
V=V_{(-r)}\oplus\cdots\oplus V_{(q)}
is a graded vector bundle, then there is a canonical diffeomorphism
\delta_{\mathcal{W}}(V)\to V\times\mathbb{R}
given by the family of maps

\delta_{\mathcal{W}}(V)|_{\pi_{\delta}^{-1}(t)}\to V,\quad\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}(v_{-r},\dots,v_{q})\mapsto(t^{-r}v_{-r},\dots,t^{q}v_{q}),&t\neq 0\\
(v_{-r},\dots,v_{q})\mapsto(v_{-r},\dots,v_{q}),&t=0,\end{array}\right.

where we are identifying
\nu_{\mathcal{W}}(V)
with
V
for
t=0
.

(b)

If
(M,N)
is a weighted pair and
TM
is linearly weighted as in
3.3
(b), then

\delta_{\mathcal{W}}(TM)=\bigcup_{t\in\mathbb{R}}T\delta_{\mathcal{W}}(M,N)|_{\pi_{\delta}^{-1}(t)}.

12.2
Zoom action of
\mathbb{R}^{\times}

The action of
\mathbb{R}^{\times}
on
\nu_{\mathcal{W}}(V)
extends smoothly to a linear action of
\mathbb{R}^{\times}
on
\delta_{\mathcal{W}}(V)
such that for any
f\in C^{\infty}_{pol}(V)_{(i)}
the homogeneous interpolation
\widetilde{f}^{[i]}\in C^{\infty}(\delta_{\mathcal{W}}(V))
is homogeneous of degree
i
. On the open dense set
V\times\mathbb{R}^{\times}\subseteq\delta_{\mathcal{W}}(V)
, this action is given by

\alpha_{\lambda}(v,t)=(v,\lambda^{-1}t)

Example 3.36
.

If
V=V_{(-r)}\oplus\cdots\oplus V_{(-1)}
is a graded vector bundle then, with respect to the identification
\delta_{\mathcal{W}}(V)\to V\times\mathbb{R}
described in
3.35
, the zoom action is given by

\alpha_{\lambda}(v_{-r},\dots,v_{q},t)=(\lambda^{r}v_{-r},\dots,\lambda^{-q}v_{q},\lambda^{-1}t).

12.3
Sections of the weighted deformation bundle

Analogous to
Section 11.3
, let
\Gamma_{[i]}(\delta_{\mathcal{W}}(V))
denote the sections of
\delta_{\mathcal{W}}(V)
which are homogeneous of degree
i
with respect to the zoom action of
\mathbb{R}^{\times}
, and let

\Gamma_{pol}(\delta_{\mathcal{W}}(V))=\bigoplus_{i\in\mathbb{Z}}\Gamma_{[i]}(\delta_{\mathcal{W}}(V)).

We are going to define an inclusion

\mathrm{Rees}(\Gamma(V))\to\Gamma_{pol}(\delta_{\mathcal{W}}(V))

of
\mathrm{Rees}(C^{\infty}(M))
-modules, where

\mathrm{Rees}(\Gamma(V))=\left\{\sum_{i\in\mathbb{Z}}\sigma_{i}z^{-i}:\sigma_{i}\in\Gamma(V)_{(i)}\right\}\subseteq\Gamma(V)[z,z^{-1}].

Definition 3.37
.

Given
\sigma\in\Gamma(V)_{(i)}
, the
i
-th homogeneous interpolation
is the map
\widetilde{\sigma}^{[i]}:\delta_{\mathcal{W}}(M,N)\to\delta_{\mathcal{W}}(V)
defined by

(\widetilde{\sigma}^{[i]}(\varphi))(fz^{-j})=\varphi((f\circ\sigma)z^{-j-ni}),

(33)

where
\varphi\in\delta_{\mathcal{W}}(M,N)
and
f\in C^{\infty}_{[n]}(V)_{(j)}
.

Lemma 3.38
.

For
\sigma\in\Gamma(V)_{(i)}
, the
i
-th homogeneous interpolation is a smooth section of
\delta_{\mathcal{W}}(V)
of homogeneity
i
such that, for any
f\in C^{\infty}_{[n]}(V)_{(j)}
and
g\in C^{\infty}(M)_{(k)}
, one has

\displaystyle\begin{split}\widetilde{f}^{[j]}\circ\widetilde{\sigma}^{[i]}&=\widetilde{f\circ\sigma}^{[j+ni]}\in C^{\infty}(\delta_{\mathcal{W}}(M,N))\quad\text{and}\\
\widetilde{g}^{[k]}\widetilde{\sigma}^{[i]}&=\widetilde{g\sigma}^{[i+k]}\in\Gamma(\delta_{\mathcal{W}}(V)).\end{split}

(34)

As was the case for the weighted normal bundle, this yields a map

\mathrm{Rees}(\Gamma(V))\to\Gamma_{pol}(\delta_{\mathcal{W}}(V)),\quad\sum_{i}\sigma_{i}z^{-i}\mapsto\sum_{i}\widetilde{\sigma}_{i}^{[i]}

In terms of the decomposition (
32
) one finds, using the relations (
34
), that if
\sigma\in\Gamma(V)_{(i)}
then

\widetilde{\sigma}^{[i]}|_{\pi_{\delta}^{-1}(t)}=\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}t^{-i}\sigma&t\neq 0\\
\sigma^{[i]}&t=0.\end{array}\right.

Example 3.39
.

Suppose that
V=V_{(-r)}\oplus\cdots\oplus V_{(q)}
is a graded vector bundle, and let

\sigma=\sigma_{-i}+\sigma_{-i+1}+\cdots+\sigma_{q}\in\Gamma(V)_{(-i)}.

With respect to the identification
\delta_{\mathcal{W}}(V)\to V\times\mathbb{R}
described in
3.35
(a), we have that

\widetilde{\sigma}^{[-i]}=\sigma_{-i}+t\sigma_{-i+1}+\cdots+t^{i+q}\sigma_{q},

which clearly extends to
\sigma_{-i}=\sigma^{[-i]}
as
t\to 0
.

Theorem 3.40
.

If
\sigma_{b}
is a weighted frame for
V|_{U}
, then the homogeneous interpolations
\widetilde{\sigma}_{b}^{[v_{b}]}
define a frame for
\delta_{\mathcal{W}}(V|_{U})=\delta_{\mathcal{W}}(V)|_{\delta_{\mathcal{W}}(U,U\cap N)}
. In particular, the map

\mathrm{Rees}(\Gamma(V))\to\Gamma_{pol}(\delta_{\mathcal{W}}(V))

is an inclusion of
\mathrm{Rees}(C^{\infty}(M))
-modules and

\Gamma(\delta_{\mathcal{W}}(V))=C^{\infty}(\delta_{\mathcal{W}}(M,N))\otimes_{\mathrm{Rees}(C^{\infty}(M))}\mathrm{Rees}(\Gamma(V)).

Remark 3.41
.

In general, the map
\mathrm{Rees}(\Gamma(V))\to\Gamma_{pol}(\delta_{\mathcal{W}}(V))
fails to be surjective because the map
\mathrm{Rees}(C^{\infty}(M))\to C^{\infty}_{pol}(\delta_{\mathcal{W}}(M,N))
need not be surjective.

For completeness, we record the following.

Proposition 3.42
.

If
W\to R
is a weighted subbundle of
V\to M
, then
\delta_{\mathcal{W}}(W)
is a subbundle of
\delta_{\mathcal{W}}(V)
.

13
The Rescaled Spinor Bundle and Ševera’s Algebroid

We now take a some time to explain how our constructions put two separate constructions in the literature, namely the
rescaled spinor bundle
of Higson and Yi introduced in
[
HY19
]
and the "associative algebroid" introduced by Ševera in
[
Šev17
]
, under the same umbrella. The results of this section are the result of discussions with Gabriel Beiner, Yiannis Loizides, and Eckhard Meinrenken. See
[
Bei22
]
for another account and more information on the connections to index theory.

13.1
The rescaled spinor bundle

Let
M
be an even dimensional spin Riemannian manifold with spinor bundle
S\to M
and Clifford connection
\nabla
on
S
. Higson and Yi define a filtration of differential operators acting on sections of
S
by declaring that Clifford multiplication and covariant differentiation have order
-1
. Thus,
D\in\mathrm{DO}(S)_{(-q)}
if and only if it can be locally expressed as a sum of terms of the form

fD_{1}\cdots D_{q}

where
f\in C^{\infty}(M)
and each
D_{i}
is either a covariant derivative
\nabla_{X}
, Clifford multiplication
c(X)
, or the identity operator (cf.
[
HY19
, Definition 3.3.1]
). Higson and Yi say that
D
has
Getzler order

-q
if
D\in\mathrm{DO}(S)_{(-q)}
.

The Getzler filtration of
\mathrm{DO}(S)
determines a filtration of
\Gamma(S\boxtimes S^{*})
as follows. Recall that
\mathbb{C}\mathrm{l}(TM)
is naturally filtered

\mathbb{C}\mathrm{l}(TM)=\mathbb{C}\mathrm{l}_{-\dim(TM)}(TM)\supseteq\cdots\supseteq\mathbb{C}\mathrm{l}_{0}(TM)=\mathbb{C},

(35)

hence
S\boxtimes S^{*}|_{M}=S\otimes S^{*}\cong\mathbb{C}\mathrm{l}(TM)
is naturally filtered. Define

\displaystyle\begin{split}\Gamma(S\boxtimes S^{*})_{(i)}=\{\sigma\in\Gamma(S\boxtimes S^{*}&):D\in\mathrm{DO}(S)_{(-q)}\\
&\implies D\sigma\in\Gamma(S\boxtimes S^{*},\mathbb{C}\mathrm{l}_{i-q}(TM))\},\end{split}

(36)

where we are using the identification
\mathbb{C}\mathrm{l}(TM)=S\boxtimes S^{*}|_{M}
.

Theorem 3.43

(
[
HY19
, Lemma 3.4.10]
)
.

.
If
M\times M
is given the trivial weighting along the diagonal then the filtration (
36
) defines a linear weighting of
S\boxtimes S^{*}
such that the induced filtration of
S\boxtimes S^{*}|_{M}
is given by (
35
).

Applying the weighted deformation bundle to
S\boxtimes S^{*}
with this linear weighting yields the rescaled spinor bundle.

Definition 3.44
.

(cf.
[
HY19
, Section 3.4]
).
The
rescaled spinor bundle
is the weighted deformation bundle

\mathbb{S}=\delta_{\mathcal{W}}(S\boxtimes S^{*})\to\mathbb{T}M.

13.2
Ševera’s algebroid

Let
V\to M
be a rank
k
vector bundle with inner product and assume that the principal
\mathrm{SO}(k)
-frame bundle admits a lift to a principal
\mathrm{Spin}(k)
-bundle
P
. Let
\mathbb{C}\mathrm{l}(\mathbb{R}^{k})
be the complexified Clifford algebra, and consider the action of
\mathrm{Pair}(\mathrm{Spin}(k))
on
\mathrm{Pair}(P)\times\mathbb{C}\mathrm{l}(\mathbb{R}^{k})
given by

(g_{1},g_{2}).(f_{1},f_{2},v)=(f_{1}.g_{1},f_{2}.g_{2},g_{1}vg_{2}^{-1}).

Definition 3.45

(cf.
[
Šev17
]
)
.

If
V
is a rank
k
vector bundle equipped with inner product and spin structure, the
Clifford algebroid
is the associated bundle

\mathscr{C}l(V)=\mathrm{Pair}(P)\times_{\mathrm{Pair}(\mathrm{Spin}(k))}\mathbb{C}\mathrm{l}(\mathbb{R}^{k})\to\mathrm{Pair}(M)

We will show that there is a canonical linear weighting of
\mathscr{C}l(V)
, starting with a technical lemma. The proof of this lemma uses weighted paths so in order to keep our exposition short, we postpone the proof to the appendix.

Lemma 3.46
.

If
\mathrm{Pair}(P)
,
\mathrm{Pair}(\mathrm{Spin}(k))
are given the doubled trivial weighting along the diagonal, and
\mathbb{C}\mathrm{l}(\mathbb{R}^{k})
is given the linear weighting defined by its filtration by subspaces, then the group action

\mathrm{Pair}(\mathrm{Spin}(k))\times\mathrm{Pair}(P)\times\mathbb{C}\mathrm{l}(\mathbb{R}^{k})\to\mathrm{Pair}(P)\times\mathbb{C}\mathrm{l}(\mathbb{R}^{k})

(37)

is a weighted morphism.

Proof.

See
10.B
(cf.
[
Bei22
]
).
∎

A consequence of this lemma is the following theorem.

Theorem 3.47
.

Let
\pi:\mathrm{Pair}(P)\times\mathbb{C}\mathrm{l}(\mathbb{R}^{k})\to\mathscr{C}l(V)
be the quotient map. Then

C^{\infty}_{pol}(\mathscr{C}l(V))_{(i)}=\{f\in C^{\infty}_{pol}(\mathscr{C}l(V)):\pi^{*}f\in C^{\infty}_{pol}(\mathrm{Pair}(P)\times\mathbb{C}\mathrm{l}(\mathbb{R}^{k}))_{(i)}\}

defines a linear weighting of
\mathscr{C}l(V)
, where
\mathrm{Pair}(P)\times\mathbb{C}\mathrm{l}(\mathbb{R}^{k})
is given the product weighting.

Proof.

We have to find weighted vector bundle coordinates. Any point
p\in\mathrm{Pair}(M)
is contained in an open neighbourhood
U\subseteq\mathrm{Pair}(M)
such that
\mathrm{Pair}(P)|_{U}
is isomorphic to
U\times\mathrm{Pair}(\mathrm{Spin}(k))
as weighted manifolds. Choosing
U
small enough, we may assume that there exist weighted coordinates
x_{a}\in C^{\infty}(U)
. If
p_{b}
are linear weighted coordinates on
\mathbb{C}\mathrm{l}(\mathbb{R}^{k})
then since the action map (
78
) is weighted and the following diagram commutes

\textstyle{\mathrm{Pair}(P)|_{U}\times\mathbb{C}\mathrm{l}(\mathbb{R}^{k})\cong U\times\mathrm{Pair}(\mathrm{Spin}(k))\times\mathbb{C}\mathrm{l}(\mathbb{R}^{k})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\pi}
\textstyle{\mathscr{C}l(V)|_{U}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\cong}
\textstyle{U\times\mathbb{C}\mathrm{l}(\mathbb{R}^{k}),}

(38)

it follows that we can take
x_{a},p_{b}
to be our weighted vector bundle coordinates on
\mathscr{C}l(V)|_{U}
.
∎

Definition 3.48
.

The
Ševera algebroid
is the weighted deformation bundle

{}^{\tau}\mathscr{C}l(V)=\delta_{\mathcal{W}}(V).

†
†
margin:

4

Multiplicative Weightings

In this chapter we will develop the theory of multiplicative weightings for Lie groupoids
G\rightrightarrows M
. We will show that if
G\rightrightarrows M
is multiplicatively weighted along
H\rightrightarrows N
, then one has Lie groupoids

\nu_{\mathcal{W}}(G,H)\rightrightarrows\nu_{\mathcal{W}}(M,N)\quad\text{and}\quad\delta_{\mathcal{W}}(G,H)\rightrightarrows\delta_{\mathcal{W}}(M,N).

We also discuss the appropriate notion of equivalence of weighted Lie groupoids.

14
Preliminaries on Lie groupoids

14.1
Definition and basic examples

A Lie groupoid
G\rightrightarrows M
is a smooth manifold
G
with a partially defined associative binary operation together with two surjective submersions
t,s:G\to M
onto a smooth submanifold
M\subseteq G
. Elements of
G
are called
arrows
, elements of
M
are called
units
, and the maps
t,s
are called the
target
and
source
, respectively. The space of
k
-arrows
is the
k
-fold fibre product

G^{(k)}=\{(g_{1},\dots,g_{k})\in G^{k}:s(g_{i})=t(g_{i+1}),\ i=1,\dots,k-1\};

we refer to
G^{(2)}
as the space of
composable arrows
. The partially defined multiplication is a map

\mathrm{mult}_{G}:G^{(2)}\to G,\ (g_{1},g_{2})\mapsto g_{1}\circ g_{2}

defined on composable arrows, satisfying

(a)

g_{1}\circ(g_{2}\circ g_{3})=(g_{1}\circ g_{2})\circ g_{3}
,

(b)

t(g)\circ g=g\circ s(g)=g
.

Moreover, we assume that for every
g\in G
there exists some
h\in G
such that
(g,h)\in G^{(2)}
,
(h,g)\in G^{(2)}
and that both
g\circ h
and
h\circ g
are units. It is automatic that for a given
g
, the corresponding element
h
is unique and denoted
g^{-1}
; the map corresponding map
g\mapsto g^{-1}
is denoted
\mathrm{inv}_{G}
.

A smooth map
F:H\to G
between Lie groupoids
H\rightrightarrows N
and
G\rightrightarrows M
is called a
morphism
of Lie groupoids if

F(g_{0}\circ g_{1})=F(g_{0})\circ F(g_{1}).

If
F
is the inclusion of a submanifold then
H\rightrightarrows N
is called a Lie subgroupoid. We will often denote a Lie groupoid
G\rightrightarrows M
together with a Lie subgroupoid
H\rightrightarrows N
as a pair
(G,H)\rightrightarrows(M,N)
. If
(G,H)\rightrightarrows(M,N)
is a Lie groupoid pair with
N=M
then we call
H
a
wide
subgroupoid.

Remark 4.1
.

In general it is not required that Lie groupoids be Hausdorff, asking only that the source fibres and units be Hausdorff. However, for simplicity, we will assume that all groupoids
are
Hausdorff.

Examples 4.2
.

(a)

A Lie group is a Lie groupoid whose object space is a single point.

(b)

Any smooth manifold
M
defines a trivial Lie groupoid with
s=t=\mathrm{id}_{M}
.

(c)

The
pair groupoid
of a manifold
M
is the groupoid

\mathrm{Pair}(M)=M\times M\rightrightarrows M

where
s(m,m^{\prime})=m^{\prime}
and
t(m,m^{\prime})=m
. Composition is defined as

(m,m^{\prime})\circ(m^{\prime},m^{\prime\prime})=(m,m^{\prime\prime}).

(d)

Let
G
be a Lie group,
M
be a smooth manifold
M
, and

\alpha:G\times M\to M,\quad(g,m)\mapsto g\cdot m

be a smooth action. The
action groupoid
is the defined to be the manifold
G\times M
with source map
s(g,m)=m
, target
t(g,m)=g\cdot m
, and composition law

(g,m)=(g^{\prime},m^{\prime})\circ(g^{\prime\prime},m^{\prime\prime})\iff m=m^{\prime\prime},\ m^{\prime}=g^{\prime\prime}\cdot m^{\prime\prime},\text{ and }g=g^{\prime}g^{\prime\prime}.

By identifying
G\times M
with the graph of the group action, the action groupoid can be realized as a Lie subgroupoid of
G\times\mathrm{Pair}(M)

(e)

A Lie groupoid
G\rightrightarrows M
such that
s=t
is called a
family of Lie groups
over
M
. Note that this differs slightly from a Lie group bundle over
M
, since the Lie group structure may differ from fibre to fibre. In particular, vector bundles are examples of Lie groupoids.

An important observation is that the groupoid structure of
G
is completely determined by the graph of multiplication,

\Gamma(\mathrm{mult}_{G})=\{(g,g_{0},g_{1})\in G^{3}:g=g_{0}\circ g_{1}\}\subseteq G^{3}.

Indeed,

(a)

the objects of
G
are exactly the elements
m\in G
with the property that
(m,m,m)\in\Gamma(\mathrm{mult}_{G})

(b)

given
g\in G
,
s(g)
is the unique element of
G
such that
(g,g,s(g))\in\Gamma(\mathrm{mult}_{G})
;
t(g)
is described similarly,

(c)

g^{-1}
is the unique element for which
(s(g),g^{-1},g)\in\Gamma(\mathrm{mult}_{G})
and
(t(g),g,g^{-1})\in\Gamma(\mathrm{mult}_{G})
.

14.2
The group of bisections

One consequence of the partially defined multiplication operation is that there is no map

G\to\mathrm{Diff}(G)

generalizing left or right translation on a Lie group. Instead, one needs to consider certain submanifolds
S\subseteq G
called
bisections
.

Definition 4.3
.

A
bisection
of a Lie groupoid
G\rightrightarrows M
is a submanifold
S\subseteq G
such that both
t
and
s
restrict to diffeomorphisms
s|_{S},t|_{S}:S\to M
. The set of bisections of the groupoid
G
is denoted
\Gamma(G)
.

The set of bisections is naturally a group, with multiplication given by

S_{1}\circ S_{2}=\{g_{1}\circ g_{2}:(g_{1},g_{2})\in(S_{1}\times S_{2})\cap G^{(2)}\}.

The group
\Gamma(G)
acts on
G
in three ways:

(a)

the
left action

\mathcal{A}^{L}:\Gamma(G)\times G\to G,\quad(S,g)\mapsto\mathcal{A}^{L}_{S}(g)=h\circ g,

where
h\in S
is the unique element such that
s(h)=t(g)
;

(b)

the
right action

\mathcal{A}^{R}:\Gamma(G)\times G\to G,\quad(S,g)\mapsto\mathcal{A}^{R}_{S}(g)=g\circ h^{-1},

where
h\in S
is the unique element so that
s(h)=s(g)
;

(c)

the
adjoint action
,
\mathrm{Ad}_{S}=\mathcal{A}^{R}_{S}\mathcal{A}^{L}_{S}
.

For a general Lie groupoid, a global bisection through an element
g\in G
might not exist, unless the groupoid has connected source fibres (
[
ZCL09
, Theorem 3.1]
). On the other hand, there always exists a
local
bisection through any
g\in G
.

14.3
VB-groupoids

A useful tool for the problem of differentiating weighted groupoids is the notion of a so-called "
vector bundle groupoid
", or VB-groupoid. Using the Grabowski-Rotkiewicz characterization of vector bundles in terms of their scalar multiplication (cf.
[
GR09
]
), Bursztyn, Cabrera, and del Hoyo showed that VB-groupoids can be defined rather simply as groupoids with a vector bundle scalar multiplication by groupoid morphisms
[
BCdH16
, Theorem 3.2.3]
. Following their lead, we define
graded bundle groupoids
, or GB-groupoids.

Definition 4.4

(cf.
[
BCdH16
, Theorem 3.2.3]
)
.

A Lie groupoid
V\rightrightarrows E
is called a
GB-groupoid
if it is also a graded bundle such that the monoid action
\kappa:\mathbb{R}\times V\to V
is by groupoid morphisms. If
V
is a vector bundle then it is called a
VB-groupoid
. A
GB-subgroupoid
is a Lie subgroupoid which is closed under scalar multiplication.

If
V\rightrightarrows E
is a VB-groupoid, then the zero section
G\subseteq V
inherits a groupoid structure over the zero section
M\subseteq E
. We depict a VB-groupoid as a diagram

\textstyle{V\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{E\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{G\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{M}

(39)

If
V\rightrightarrows E
is any VB-groupoid, then the dual bundle
V^{*}
is canonically a VB-groupoid with units
\mathrm{ann}(E)
(cf.
[
Mei17b
, Theorem 4.8]
; see also
[
Mac05
, Section 11.2]
and
[
Pra88
]
). The groupoid structure on
V^{*}
is uniquely characterized by the requirement that
\mu=\mu_{1}\circ\mu_{2}
if and only if

\langle\mu,v\rangle=\langle\mu_{1},v_{1}\rangle+\langle\mu_{2},v_{2}\rangle

whenever
v=v_{1}\circ v_{2}
. The graph of multiplication of
V^{*}
is

\Gamma(\mathrm{mult}_{V^{*}})=\mathrm{ann}^{\sharp}(\Gamma(\mathrm{mult}_{V})),

where
\mathrm{ann}^{\sharp}(\Gamma(\mathrm{mult}_{V}))
is the subbundle defined by

(\mu_{2},\mu_{1})\in\mathrm{ann}^{\sharp}(\Gamma(\mathrm{mult}_{V}))\iff\langle\mu_{2},v_{2}\rangle=\langle\mu_{1},v_{1}\rangle\ \ \ \forall(v_{2},v_{1})\in\Gamma(\mathrm{mult}_{V}).

Example 4.5
.

The main example of a VB-groupoid is given by applying the tangent functor to a Lie groupoid
G\rightrightarrows M
. This yields the
tangent groupoid
,

\textstyle{TG\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{TM\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{G\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{M.}

An explicit formula for multiplication on the tangent groupoid can be given using bisections. Indeed, let
X_{g_{0}}\in T_{g_{0}}G
and
Y_{g_{1}}\in T_{g_{1}}G
be composable. If
S_{0}
is a bisection through
g_{0}
and
S_{1}
is a bisection through
g_{1}
, then

X_{g_{0}}\circ Y_{g_{1}}=(T_{g_{0}}\mathcal{A}^{R}_{S_{1}})(X_{g_{0}})+(T_{g_{1}}\mathcal{A}^{L}_{S_{0}})(Y_{g_{1}})-(T_{g_{1}}(\mathcal{A}^{L}_{S_{0}}\mathcal{A}^{R}_{S_{1}}t))(Y_{g_{1}});

(40)

see
[
MX97
, Proposition 3.1]
.

We now record two lemmas that we will need later on.

Lemma 4.6

(
[
LBM14
, Corollary C.4]
)
.

Let
V\rightrightarrows E
be a VB-groupoid. If
W
is a VB-subgroupoid, then
\mathrm{ann}(W)\subseteq V^{*}
is a VB-subgroupoid with objects
\mathrm{ann}(W)\cap\mathrm{ann}(E)
.

Suppose that
V\rightrightarrows W
and
W\rightrightarrows F
are VB-groupoids with base groupoid
G\rightrightarrows M
. Their direct sum is the VB-groupoid

\textstyle{V\oplus W\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{E\oplus F\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{G\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{M}

with the obvious source and target maps, and multiplication defined by

(v_{0},w_{0})\circ(v_{1},w_{1})=(v_{0}\circ v_{1},w_{0}\circ w_{1})

for
((v_{0},w_{0}),(v_{1},w_{1}))\in V^{(2)}\oplus W^{(2)}=(V\oplus W)^{(2)}
.

Lemma 4.7
.

If

\textstyle{V\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{E\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{G\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{M}

is a VB-groupoid, then
TV|_{G}
is the direct sum VB-groupoid
TG\oplus V\rightrightarrows TM\oplus E
.

Proof.

The map

TG\oplus V\to TV|_{G},\quad(\xi,v)\mapsto\xi+v,

where we are identifying
V
with the vertical bundle in
TV|_{G}
