, is an isomorphism of vector bundles, so it remains to show that it is a groupoid morphism. But this follows since
\mathrm{mult}_{V}:V^{(2)}\to V
is a linear map with base map
\mathrm{mult}_{G}
, so

T\mathrm{mult}_{V}|_{G}=T\mathrm{mult}_{G}\oplus\mathrm{mult}_{V}.\qed

15
Definition of Multiplicative Weightings

We are now move on to
multiplicative weightings
for Lie groupoids
G\rightrightarrows M
. The obvious definition is given by inserting the adjective "weighted" in the standard definition. However, we recall from
Section 4
and
Section 5
that it is
not
obvious how to do this!

Definition 4.8
.

A weighting of
G
along
H\subseteq G
is said to be
multiplicative
if

(a)

The units
M\subseteq G
are a weighted submanifold,

(b)

the source map is a weighted submersion,

(c)

multiplication
m:G^{(2)}\to G
is a weighted morphism, and

(d)

inversion is a weighted morphism.

A groupoid with a multiplicative weighting is called a
weighted Lie groupoid
.

Remarks 4.9
.

(a)

Since inversion is its own inverse, it is automatically a weighted
diffeomorphism
.

(b)

Since
t=s\circ\mathrm{inv}
, it follows that the target map is also a weighted submersion.

(c)

Since the source and target are weighted submersions, if follows from
Theorem 2.27
that the space of
k
-arrows is a weighted submanifold of
G^{k}
. In particular, the space of composable arrows is naturally a weighted manifold, and therefore
Definition 4.8
(c) makes sense.

(d)

All of the face maps
\partial_{i}:G^{(k)}\to G^{(k-1)}
,

\partial_{i}(g_{1},\dots,g_{k})=\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}(g_{2},\dots,g_{k})&i=0,\\
(g_{1},\dots,g_{i}\circ g_{i+1},\dots,g_{k})&0<i<k,\\
(g_{1},\dots,g_{k-1})&i=k,\end{array}\right.

and all the degeneracy maps

\epsilon_{i}:G^{(k)}\to G^{(k+1)}\quad(g_{1},\dots,g_{k})\mapsto(g_{1},\dots,g_{i},s(g_{i}),g_{i+1},\dots,g_{k})

are automatically weighted.

Note that in the definition we did
not
assume that
H\subseteq G
was a Lie subgroupoid. It turns out that this is automatic.

Proposition 4.10
.

Suppose that a weighting of
G\rightrightarrows M
along
H
is multiplicative. Then
H
is a Lie subgroupoid of
G
with units
N=H\cap M
.

Proof.

Since
M\subseteq G
is a weighted submanifold, it follows by
2.9
that
H
intersects
M
cleanly; in particular,
N=H\cap M
is a submanifold. We have furthermore that
N\neq\emptyset
so long as
H\neq\emptyset
because
s:(G,H)\to(M,N)
is a map of pairs (it is a weighted morphism).

Similarly, both

\mathrm{mult}_{G}:(G^{(2)},H^{(2)})\to(G,H)\quad\text{and}\quad\mathrm{inv}_{G}:(G,H)\to(G,H)

are maps of pairs, hence
H
is also closed under multiplication and inversion. Finally, it follows from the normal form for weighted submersions (
Theorem 2.22
) that the restrictions of
s
and
t
to
H
are submersions.
∎

Lemma 4.11
.

Let
V\to M
and
W\to N
be smooth vector bundles and let
\varphi:V\to W
be a vector bundle morphism. Then
\varphi
is a submersion if and only if it is fibrewise surjective and the base map
\varphi_{M}:M\to N
is a submersion.

Proof.

Suppose that
\varphi
is a submersion, and let
p\in M
. Since
\varphi
is a vector bundle morphism, the tangent map
T_{p}\varphi
splits as
T_{p}\varphi=T_{p}\varphi_{M}\oplus\varphi
with respect to the splitting
T_{p}V=T_{p}M\oplus V_{p}
. Thus
\varphi_{M}
is a submersion and
\varphi
is fibrewise surjective.

Conversely, if
\varphi
is fibrewise surjective and the base map
\varphi_{M}:M\to N
is a submersion then by the previous paragraph it is a submersion near
M\subseteq V
. Since it is equivariant with respect to the
\mathbb{R}
-action, it follows that it is a submersion everywhere.
∎

In practice it can be difficult to verify that all the structure maps are indeed weighted in the appropriate sense. As an application of
Theorem 2.32
we have the following equivalent characterization of multiplicative weightings.

Theorem 4.12
.

A weighting of
G\rightrightarrows M
along
H\subseteq G
is multiplicative if and only if

(a)

M
is a weighted submanifold of
G
,

(b)

the graph of multiplication is a weighted submanifold of
G^{3}
, and

(c)

the filtration of
TG|_{H}
is by subgroupoids

(TG|_{H})_{(i)}\rightrightarrows(TM|_{N})_{(i)}.

Proof.

Suppose that
G
is multiplicatively weighted along
H
. Since multiplication is a weighted morphism, its graph is a weighted submanifold, establishing
(b)
. To establish (c), note that since
s
,
t
,
\mathrm{mult}_{G}
, and
\mathrm{inv}_{G}
are all weighted morphisms their tangent maps are filtration preserving and so each

(TG|_{H})_{(i)}\rightrightarrows(TM|_{N})_{(i)},\quad i=-r,-r+1,\dots,0,

are set-theoretic subgroupoids. To show that they are
Lie
subgroupoids, it remains to show that
Ts
and
Tt
are submersions. To see this, note that since
s
is a weighted submersion its tangent map
Ts:(TG|_{H})_{(i)}\rightrightarrows(TM|_{N})_{(i)}
is a fibrewise surjection whose base map is a submersion. Therefore, it follows by
Lemma 4.11
that
Ts:(TG|_{H})_{(i)}\rightrightarrows(TM|_{N})_{(i)}
is a submersion. Similarly,
Tt:(TG|_{H})_{(i)}\rightrightarrows(TM|_{N})_{(i)}
is a submersion, establishing (c).

For the converse, suppose that (a), (b), and (c) hold and let
\pi_{1}:G^{(2)}\to G
be projection onto the first factor. It is sufficient to show that
G^{(2)}
is a weighted submanifold of
G^{2}
and that
\mathrm{mult}_{G},\pi_{1}:G^{(2)}\to G
are weighted submersions for the following reason. Since the filtration of
TG|_{H}
is by subgroupoids

(TG|_{H})_{(i)}\rightrightarrows(TM|_{N})_{(i)}

it follows that
T\mathrm{inv}_{G}
is filtration preserving and
Ts
is filtration preserving and fibrewise surjective in each filtration degree. Therefore, to establish conditions (b) and (d) in
Definition 4.8
it suffices by
Theorem 2.32
to show that their graphs are weighted submanifolds. This will follow because the graphs of
\mathrm{inv}_{G}
and
s
are the inverse images of
M\subseteq G
under the weighted submersions
\mathrm{mult}_{G}:G^{(2)}\to G
and
\pi_{1}:G^{(2)}\to G
.

Let
f:\Gamma(\mathrm{mult}_{G})\to G^{2}
be the restriction of the map
G^{3}\to G^{2}
given by projection to the last two factors. In order to show that
G^{(2)}
is a weighted submanifold, we will show that
f
is a weighted embedding. Since this map is an embedding as well as a weighted morphism, it suffices, by
Theorem 2.16
, to check that the maps

(T\Gamma(\mathrm{mult}_{G})|_{\mathrm{mult}_{H}})_{(i)}\to(TG^{2}|_{TH^{2}})_{(i)}

are injective, with range equal to the intersection with
\mathrm{ran}(Tf)
. This is clear since
(TG|_{H})_{(i)}
is a subgroupoid: the graph of its multiplication is a subbundle of

T\Gamma(\mathrm{mult}_{G})=\Gamma(\mathrm{mult}_{TG}).

This establishes the claim that
G^{(2)}
is a weighted subbundle and that
f:\Gamma(\mathrm{mult}_{G})\to G^{(2)}
is a weighted diffeomorphism. The maps
\mathrm{mult}_{G}
and
\pi_{1}
are weighted morphisms since they factor as compositions

G^{(2)}\to\Gamma(\mathrm{mult}_{G})\hookrightarrow G^{3}\to G.

To see that they are weighted submersions we only need to check that the maps

((TG|_{H})_{(i)})^{(2)}=((TG^{(2)}|_{H^{(2)}})_{(i)}\to(TM|_{N})_{(i)}

are fibrewise surjective bundle maps, which follows since the
(TG|_{H})_{(i)}
are subgroupoids.
∎

Corollary 4.13
.

Suppose that
G\rightrightarrows M
is a weighted Lie groupoid and
K\rightrightarrows P
is a Lie subgroupoid. If
K
is a weighted submanifold of
G
then the induced weighting is multiplicative.

Proof.

It is clear that inversion
\mathrm{inv}_{K}:K\to K
is a weighted morphism because it is the restriction of a weighted morphism to a weighted submanifold. It remains to show that
P
is a weighted submanifold of
K
, that
s:K\to P
is a weighted submersion, and that multiplication on
K
is a weighted morphism.

Consider the map
K\to K
defined as the restriction of the composition

G\overset{s}{\longrightarrow}M\hookrightarrow G

to
K
. This is a weighted projection of
K
whose image is
P
, hence
P
is a weighted submanifold of
K
by
2.11
.

We now show that
s|_{K}:K\to P
is a weighted submersion. Since
K
is a weighted submanifold, it follows by considering weighted submanifold coordinates that
TK
intersects
(TG|_{H})_{(i)}
cleanly. By
Theorem 4.12
, each
(TG|_{H})_{(i)}
is a Lie subgroupoid of
TG|_{H}
, so it follows by
[
Mei17b
, Theorem 4.20]
that the intersections
TK\cap(TG|_{H})_{(i)}=(TK|_{K\cap H})_{(i)}
are Lie subgroupoids with objects
TP\cap(TM|_{N})_{(i)}=(TP|_{P\cap N})_{(i)}
. This, in particular, implies that

Ts|_{K\cap H}:(TK|_{K\cap H})_{(i)}\to(TP|_{P\cap N})_{(i)}

is fibrewise surjective, hence
s|_{K}:K\to P
is a weighted submersion.

Finally, we show that multiplication is weighted. Both
s|_{K}
and
t|_{k}
are weighted submersions, and so
K^{(2)}
is a weighted submanifold of
G^{(2)}
. Since multiplication for
K
is the restriction of multiplication for
G
, it is a weighted morphism.
∎

Remark 4.14
.

In particular,
Corollary 4.13
shows that "weighted subgroupoids" are simply the Lie subgroupoids which are weighted submanifolds.

Examples 4.15
.

(a)

If
H\rightrightarrows N
is a Lie subgroupoid of
G\rightrightarrows M
, then the trivial weighting of
G
along
H
is multiplicative.

(b)

If
(M,N)
is a weighted pair, then
M\times M=\mathrm{Pair}(M)
is a weighted groupoid with respect to the product weighting along
\mathrm{Pair}(N)
. Indeed, the units are a weighted submanifold by
Example 2.33
, the graph of multiplication is a weighted submanifold as it is the image of the weighted embedding

M^{3}\to M^{6}\quad(m_{1},m_{2},m_{3})\mapsto(m_{1},m_{3},m_{1},m_{2},m_{2},m_{3}),

and the filtration of
T\mathrm{Pair}(M)|_{\mathrm{Pair}(N)}
is given by the subgroupoids

(T\mathrm{Pair}(M)|_{\mathrm{Pair}(N)})_{(i)}=\mathrm{Pair}((TM|_{N})_{(i)}).

(c)

If
\pi:M\to N
is a weighted submersion, then the submersion groupoid
M\times_{\pi}M
is a weighted Lie groupoid. Indeed, this follows by
Corollary 4.13
as it is a weighted submanifold of the pair groupoid
\mathrm{Pair}(M)
.

(d)

A weighted Lie groupoid
G\rightrightarrows M
with
M
equal to a point will be called a weighted Lie group. In this case, the first condition in
Definition 4.8
is vacuous, and the second just says that
H
contains the group unit. Hence, weighting of a Lie group along a submanifold
H
is multiplicative if and only if
H
contains the group unit and both multiplication and inversion are weighted morphisms.

(e)

An action of a weighted Lie group
G
on a weighted manifold
M
will be called
weighted
if the action map
G\times M\to M
is a weighted morphism. In this case, the action groupoid
G\times M\rightrightarrows M
is a weighted Lie groupoid. Indeed, the action groupoid can be identified with the graph of the group action, a subgroupoid of
G\times\mathrm{Pair}(M)
. Since the group action is weighted, the graph is a weighted submanifold, and the corresponding weighting on the action groupoid is weighted by
Corollary 4.13
.

(f)

If
V\to M
is a vector bundle over
M
thought of as a Lie groupoid, then a weighting is multiplicative if and only if it is linear and concentrated in non-positive degrees.

(g)

Let
(G,H)
be a weighted Lie group pair and
P\to B
a principal
G
-bundle with a principal weighting along
Q\subseteq P
(see
8.4
(b)). By
8.10
,
Q
is a principal
H
-bundle. Using an argument analogous to to proof of
Theorem 8.8
one finds that the product weighting on
\mathrm{Pair}(P)
descends to a weighting of the Atiyah groupoid
\mathrm{At}(P)=(P\times P)/G
along
\mathrm{At}(Q)=(Q\times Q)/H
and that this weighting is multiplicative.

Proposition 4.16
.

Let
G\rightrightarrows M
be a weighted Lie groupoid. For all
g\in G
there is a local bisection through
g
which is given by a weighted submanifold.

Proof.

We may assume that
g\in H
as otherwise the statement is obvious. The tangent space
T_{g}G
is a weighted vector space (cf.
Remark 3.5
) with both
\ker(T_{g}t)
and
\ker(T_{g}s)
as weighted subspaces. We may choose a subspace of
T_{g}G
which is a weighted complement to both. This weighted subspace is realized as the tangent space to a weighted submanifold
S
. Taking
S
smaller if needed, this is the desired local bisection.
∎

15.1
Weighted VB-groupoids

Using the conclusion of
Theorem 4.12
, we can combine linear weightings and multiplicative weightings to give rise to a notion of weighted VB-groupoids. These will be used for the differentiation procedure discussed in
Section 21
.

Definition 4.17
.

A
weighted VB-groupoid
is a VB-groupoid

\textstyle{V\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{E\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{G\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{M}

together with a linear weighting such that

(a)

E
is a weighted subbundle of
V
,

(b)

the graph of multiplication is a weighted subbundle of
V^{3}
, and

(c)

the filtrations of
V|_{H}
and
TG|_{H}
are by subgroupoids

(V|_{H})_{(i)}\rightrightarrows(E|_{N})_{(i)}\quad\text{and}\quad(TG|_{H})_{(i)}\rightrightarrows(TM|_{N})_{(i)},

respectively.

Note that this definition ensures that the weighting of
G
along
H
is also multiplicative.

Example 4.18
.

If
G\rightrightarrows M
is a weighted groupoid, then
TG\rightrightarrows TM
is a weighted VB-groupoid. Indeed, since
M
is a weighted submanifold of
G
it follows that
TM
is a weighted subbundle of
TG
. Similarly, we have that

\Gamma(\mathrm{mult}_{TG}))=T\Gamma(\mathrm{mult}_{G})\subseteq TG^{3},

so it is a weighted subbundle. Finally, the filtration of

T(TG)|_{H}=TG|_{H}\oplus TG|_{H}

is by subgroupoids because the weighting of
G
is multiplicative.

Lemma 4.19
.

Let
V\to M
be a weighted vector bundle and
W\subseteq V
a weighted subbundle. Then
\mathrm{ann}(W)
is a weighted subbundle of
V^{*}
.

Proof.

See
Appendix 10.C
.
∎

Proposition 4.20
.

Let
V\rightrightarrows E
be a weighted VB-groupoid. Then the dual VB-groupoid
V^{*}\rightrightarrows\mathrm{ann}(E)
, equipped with the dual weighting (
Section 10.2
), is a weighted VB-groupoid.

Proof.

Since
E
is a weighted subbundle of
\mathcal{V}
, its annihilator is a weighted subbundle of
\mathcal{V}^{*}
by
Lemma 4.19
. Similarly,
\mathrm{ann}^{\sharp}(\mathrm{Gr}(\mathrm{mult}_{\mathcal{V}}))=\mathrm{Gr}(\mathrm{mult}_{\mathcal{V}^{*}})
is a weighted subbundle of
(\mathcal{V}^{*})^{3}
(see
Remark 10.6
). To conclude, we recall that

(\mathcal{V}^{*}|_{H})_{(i)}=\mathrm{ann}((\mathcal{V}|_{H})_{(-i+1)})

whence the filtration of
(T\mathcal{V}^{*})|_{H}
is by subgroupoids, since the annihilator of a VB-subgroupoid is itself a VB-subgroupoid by
Lemma 4.6
.
∎

16
The Weighted Normal and Deformation Groupoids

Let
G\rightrightarrows M
be a Lie groupoid. The most naive definition of a multiplicative weighting would be one with the property that the groupoid structure
G\times\mathbb{R}^{\times}\rightrightarrows M\times\mathbb{R}^{\times}
extends to a groupoid structure
\delta_{\mathcal{W}}(G,H)\rightrightarrows\delta_{\mathcal{W}}(M,N)
. In this section we will show that this is
equivalent
to our definition of multiplicative weighting.

Lemma 4.21
.

Suppose that
(M,N)
and
(M^{\prime},N^{\prime})
are weighted pairs and
F:M\to M^{\prime}
is a smooth map. Then
F
is a weighted morphism if and only if the map

\widetilde{F}:M\times\mathbb{R}^{\times}\to M^{\prime}\times\mathbb{R}^{\times},\quad(p,t)\mapsto(F(p),t)

extends to a smooth map
\delta_{\mathcal{W}}(M,N)\to\delta_{\mathcal{W}}(M^{\prime},N^{\prime})
.

Proof.

Given
f\in C^{\infty}(M)_{(i)}
we will show that the function

\widetilde{f}^{[j]}:M\times\mathbb{R}^{\times}\to\mathbb{R}\quad p\mapsto t^{-i}f(p)

extends to
\delta_{\mathcal{W}}(M,N)
if and only if
i\geq j
. Working locally we may assume that
x_{a}
is a global weighted coordinate system on
M
. Let

y_{a}=\widetilde{x}_{a}^{[w_{a}]}=\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}t^{-w_{a}}x_{a}&t\neq 0\\
x_{a}^{[w_{a}]}&t=0\end{array}\right.

be the corresponding (global) coordinates on
\delta_{\mathcal{W}}(M,N)
. Using that
f\in C^{\infty}(M)_{(i)}
we may write

f(x_{1},\dots,x_{m})=\chi(x_{1},\dots,x_{m})x^{s}=\chi(x_{1},\dots,x_{m})x_{1}^{s_{1}}x_{2}^{s_{2}}\cdots x_{m}^{s_{m}}

where
\chi\in C^{\infty}(M)
and
s\cdot w=\sum_{a}s_{a}w_{a}=i
. Then, for
t\neq 0
, we have

\widetilde{f}^{[j]}(y,t)=t^{-j}f(x)=t^{-j}\chi(x)x^{s}=t^{s\cdot w-j}\chi(t^{w_{1}}y_{1},\dots,t^{w_{m}}y_{m})y^{s}

which extends smoothly to
t=0
if and only if
i-j=s\cdot w-j\geq 0
.

To see how this implies the lemma,
y_{b}\in C^{\infty}(M)_{(w^{\prime}_{b})}
be weighted coordinates on
M^{\prime}
. Then
F
extends to
\delta_{\mathcal{W}}(M,N)
if and only if each of the component functions
f_{b}=F^{*}y_{b}
extend to
\delta_{\mathcal{W}}(M,N)
. By the previous argument, this happens if and only if each
f_{b}
has weight
w^{\prime}_{b}
, which is equivalent to
F
being a weighted morphism.
∎

We now state the main theorem of the chapter.

Theorem 4.22
.

Let
G\rightrightarrows M
be a Lie groupoid which is weighted along the Lie subgroupoid
H\rightrightarrows N
. If the weighting of
G
along
H
is multiplicative, then the Lie groupoid
G\times\mathbb{R}^{\times}\to M\times\mathbb{R}^{\times}
extends uniquely to

\delta_{\mathcal{W}}(G,H)\rightrightarrows\delta_{\mathcal{W}}(M,N).

Conversely, if
(M,N)
is a weighted pair and the groupoid structure on
G\times\mathbb{R}^{\times}\rightrightarrows M\times\mathbb{R}^{\times}
extends to
\delta_{\mathcal{W}}(G,H)\rightrightarrows\delta_{\mathcal{W}}(M,N)
, then
(G,H)
is a weighted Lie groupoid pair.

Proof.

Suppose that the weighting of
G
along
H
is multiplicative. Since
M
is a weighted submanifold of
G
, it follows from the discussion in
Section 7.2
(b) that
\delta_{\mathcal{W}}(M,N)
is a submanifold of
\delta_{\mathcal{W}}(G,H)
. Since both
s,t:G\to M
are weighted submersions, the maps
\delta_{\mathcal{W}}(s_{g}),\delta_{\mathcal{W}}(t_{g}):\delta_{\mathcal{W}}(G,H)\to\delta_{\mathcal{W}}(M,N)
are submersions by
Theorem 2.49
. By
Section 7.2
(c) we have that
\delta_{\mathcal{W}}(G,H)^{(2)}=\delta_{\mathcal{W}}(G^{(2)},H^{(2)})
, and therefore define groupoid multiplication by

\mathrm{mult}_{\delta_{\mathcal{W}}(G,H)}=\delta_{\mathcal{W}}(\mathrm{mult}_{G}).

Associativity of multiplication, that
\delta_{\mathcal{W}}(M,N)
are the units, and existence of inverses all follow from functoriality. For example, the fact that
\mathrm{mult}_{\delta_{\mathcal{W}}(G,H)}(\varphi,s_{\delta_{\mathcal{W}}(G,H)}(\varphi))=\varphi
for any
\varphi\in\delta_{\mathcal{W}}(G,H)
follows since the composition

\textstyle{G\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{(\mathrm{id},s)}
\textstyle{G^{(2)}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\mathrm{mult}_{G}}
\textstyle{G}

is the identity. Uniqueness of this extension is clear since
G\times\mathbb{R}^{\times}
is a dense open set in
\delta_{\mathcal{W}}(G,H)
.

Conversely, suppose that the Lie groupoid structure of
G\times\mathbb{R}^{\times}\rightrightarrows M\times\mathbb{R}^{\times}
extends to
\delta_{\mathcal{W}}(G,H)\to\delta_{\mathcal{W}}(M,N)
. By
Lemma 4.21
, it follows that inversion is a weighted morphism and, using
Theorem 2.49
in addition, the source and target maps are weighted submersions. Similarly,
M
is a weighted submanifold of
G
. Since
s
and
t
are weighted submersions
G^{(2)}
is a weighted submanifold of
G^{2}
, and
\delta_{\mathcal{W}}(G,H)^{(2)}=\delta_{\mathcal{W}}(G^{(2)},H^{(2)})
because this holds on the dense open set
G^{2}\times\mathbb{R}\subseteq\delta_{\mathcal{W}}(G^{2},H^{2})
. It follows from
Lemma 4.21
that
\mathrm{mult}_{G}
is a weighted morphism as well.
∎

Remark 4.23
.

If
G\rightrightarrows M
is multiplicatively weighted along a
wide
subgroupoid
H\rightrightarrows M
then
\delta_{\mathcal{W}}(M,M)=M\times\mathbb{R}
and we may think of

\delta_{\mathcal{W}}(G,H)\rightrightarrows M\times\mathbb{R}

as a family of Lie groupoids over
M
.

Recall (
Definition 4.4
) that a GB-groupoid is a Lie groupoid together with a monoid action of
\mathbb{R}
by groupoid morphisms. Applying the weighted normal functor to a weighted Lie groupoid gives an example of such an object.

Corollary 4.24
.

If
(G,H)\rightrightarrows(M,N)
is a weighted Lie groupoid pair, then

\nu_{\mathcal{W}}(G,H)\rightrightarrows\nu_{\mathcal{W}}(M,N)

is a GB-groupoid with

(a)

s_{\nu_{\mathcal{W}}(G,H)}=\nu_{\mathcal{W}}(s)
and
t_{\nu_{\mathcal{W}}(G,H)}=\nu_{\mathcal{W}}(t)
,

(b)

\nu_{\mathcal{W}}(G,H)^{(2)}=\nu_{\mathcal{W}}(G^{(2)},H^{(2)})
,

(c)

\mathrm{mult}_{\nu_{\mathcal{W}}(G,H)}=\nu_{\mathcal{W}}(\mathrm{mult}_{G})

(d)

\mathrm{inv}_{\nu_{\mathcal{W}}(G,H)}=\nu_{\mathcal{W}}(\mathrm{inv}_{G})

Examples 4.25
.

(a)

If
G\rightrightarrows M
is trivially weighted along
H\rightrightarrows N
, then
\nu_{\mathcal{W}}(G,H)
is just
\nu(G,H)\rightrightarrows\nu(M,N)
with its usual Lie groupoid structure.

(b)

If
(M,N)
is weighted pair, then the product weighting of
\mathrm{Pair}(M)
along
\mathrm{Pair}(N)
is multiplicative and

\delta_{\mathcal{W}}(\mathrm{Pair}(M),\mathrm{Pair}(N))=\mathrm{Pair}(\delta_{\mathcal{W}}(M,N))

by the discussion in
Section 7.2
(c).

(c)

If
(M,N)
and
M^{\prime},N^{\prime})
are weighted pairs, and
q:M\to M^{\prime}
is a weighted submersion, then submersion groupoid
M\times_{M^{\prime}}M
is multiplicatively weighted along
N\times_{N^{\prime}}N
. In this case

\delta_{\mathcal{W}}(M\times_{M^{\prime}}M,N\times_{N^{\prime}}N)=\delta_{\mathcal{W}}(M,N)\times_{\delta_{\mathcal{W}}(M^{\prime},N^{\prime})}\delta_{\mathcal{W}}(M,N),

see
Section 7.2
(c) again.

(d)

Suppose that
(G,H)
is a weighted Lie group pair and
(M,N)
is a weighted
G
-space, then
\delta_{\mathcal{W}}(G,H)
acts on
\delta_{\mathcal{W}}(M,N)
and applying the weighted deformation functor to the action groupoid gives that action groupoid for the action of
\delta_{\mathcal{W}}(G,H)
on
\delta_{\mathcal{W}}(M,N)
.

(e)

Let
G
be a weighted Lie group and
P\to B
a weighted principal
G
-bundle. Then
\delta_{\mathcal{W}}(P,Q)\to\delta_{\mathcal{W}}(B,C)
is a weighted principal
\delta_{\mathcal{W}}(G,H)
-bundle and

\mathrm{At}(\delta_{\mathcal{W}}(P,Q))=\delta_{\mathcal{W}}(\mathrm{At}(P),\mathrm{At}(Q)).

(f)

(
[
VEY17
,
SH18
]
) Recall that a filtered manifold is a manifold
M
together with a filtration

TM=F_{-r}\supseteq F_{-r+1}\supseteq\cdots\supseteq F_{-1}\supseteq 0

by subbundles satisfying
[\Gamma(F)_{(-i)},\Gamma(F_{-j})]\subseteq\Gamma(F_{-i-j})
. By
Theorem 2.54
, the product filtration

\mathrm{Pair}(TM)=\mathrm{Pair}(F_{-r})\supseteq\mathrm{Pair}(F_{-r+1})\supseteq\cdots\supseteq\mathrm{Pair}(F_{-1})\supseteq 0

defines a weighting of
\mathrm{Pair}(M)
along
M
. Since the weighting is along
M
, it follows in particular that it is a weighted submanifold and it is shown in
[
VEY17
]
that the groupoid structure

\mathrm{Pair}(M)\times\mathbb{R}^{\times}\rightrightarrows M\times\mathbb{R}^{\times}

extends to
\delta_{\mathcal{W}}(\mathrm{Pair}(M),M)\rightrightarrows M\times\mathbb{R}^{\times}
. By
Theorem 4.22
it follows that this weighting is multiplicative. In the next chapter we will give a direct proof of this fact (
Theorem 5.23
). In this example,
\nu_{\mathcal{W}}(\mathrm{Pair}(M),M)\rightrightarrows M
is the family of simply connected Lie groups integrating the family of nilpotent Lie algebras

\mathfrak{t}_{H}M=\bigoplus H_{-i}/H_{-i+1}\to M.

†
†
margin:

5

Infinitesimally Multiplicative Weightings

The infinitesimal counterpart a Lie groupoid is a
Lie algebroid
. In this chapter we will define
infinitesimally multiplicative
weightings (
Definition 5.11
), which are the infinitesimal analogue of multiplicative weightings. We then explain two equivalent characterizations in terms of linear Poisson manifolds and the de Rham complex of the Lie algebroid (
Theorem 5.15
). We will then discuss differentiation of multiplicative weightings (
Theorem 5.20
) and integration of infinitesimally multiplicative weightings along wide subalgebroids (
Theorem 5.23
), and use this to show that Lie groupoids with a multiplicative weighting along their units are in 1-1 correspondence with filtered groupoids in the sense of van Erp and Yuncken (cf.
[
VEY19
, Definition 67]
).

17
Preliminaries on Lie Algebroids

Lie algebroids were introduced by Pradines in
[
Pra67
]
.

Definition 5.1
.

A
Lie algebroid
over a smooth manifold
M
is a vector bundle
A\to M
together with a Lie bracket
[\cdot,\cdot]:\Gamma(A)\times\Gamma(A)\to\Gamma(A)
and a vector bundle morphism
a:A\to TM
, called the
anchor
, such that

[\sigma,f\tau]=f[\sigma,\tau]+\mathcal{L}_{a(\sigma)}f\cdot\tau

for all
\sigma,\tau\in\Gamma(A)
and
f\in C^{\infty}(M)
.

We use the notation
A\Rightarrow M
to imply that the vector bundle
A\to M
is a Lie algebroid.

Examples 5.2
.

(a)

The tangent bundle
TM
of any smooth manifold
M
is a Lie algebroid with anchor map given by the identity.

(b)

A Lie algebroid over a point is a Lie algebra.

(c)

The tangent bundle
T_{\mathcal{F}}M
of a foliation
\mathcal{F}
on
M
is a Lie algebroid with anchor map given by the identity.

(d)

If
\kappa:P\to M
is a principal
G
-bundle, then
\mathrm{At}(P)=TP/G\to M
is a Lie algebroid with Lie bracket induced by the Lie bracket on
G
-invariant vector fields on
P
and anchor map induced by
T\kappa:TP\to TM
.

In the special case the
P
is the frame bundle of a vector bundle
V\to M
, we may identify
\Gamma(\mathrm{At}(P))
with the
linear vector fields
on
V
, i.e. the vector fields
X\in\mathfrak{X}(V)
with the property that

\mathcal{L}_{X}:C^{\infty}_{[n]}(V)\to C^{\infty}_{[n]}(V)

for all
n\geq 0
, where
C^{\infty}_{[n]}(V)
denotes the functions on
V
which are fibrewise homogeneous of degree
n
(see
Section 6.1
).

(e)

A
family of Lie algebras
is a vector bundle
A\to M
with a fibre-wise Lie bracket. A family of Lie algebras is a Lie algebroid over
M
by taking the anchor map to be zero. Conversely, any Lie algebroid with zero anchor map is given in this way.

(f)

Let
\omega\in\Omega^{2}(M)
be a 2-form on a manifold
M
and consider the vector bundle
A=TM\times\mathbb{R}\to M
. Define a bracket on
\Gamma(A)=\mathfrak{X}(M)\oplus C^{\infty}(M)
by

[X+f,Y+g]=[X,Y]+\mathcal{L}_{X}g-\mathcal{L}_{Y}f+\omega(X,Y)

and let
a:TM\times\mathbb{R}\to TM
be the projection onto the first factor. Then
(A,[\cdot,\cdot],a)
is a Lie algebroid if and only if
\omega
is closed.

For our work on weighted Lie algebroids, we will need two equivalent characterizations of Lie algebroids.

17.1
Lie algebroids as linear Poisson manifolds

Recall that a Poisson structure on a manifold
M
is a is skew-symmetric bilinear map
\{\cdot,\cdot\}:C^{\infty}(M)\times C^{\infty}(M)\to C^{\infty}(M)
such that, for all
f,g,h\in C^{\infty}(M)
,

(i)

the derivation property:
\{f,gh\}=\{f,g\}h+g\{f,h\}
, and

(ii)

the Jacobi identity:
\{f,\{g,h\}\}=\{\{f,g\},h\}+\{g,\{f,h\}\}
.

A Poisson structure on
M
defines a bivector field
\pi\in\mathfrak{X}^{2}(M)=\Gamma(\wedge^{2}TM)
by the equation

\pi(\mathrm{d}f,\mathrm{d}g)=\{f,g\}.

(41)

Conversely, a bivector field
\pi\in\mathfrak{X}^{2}(M)
defines a skew-symmetric bracket on
C^{\infty}(M)
satisfying the derivation property by (
41
); it is called a
Poisson bivector field
if the induced bracket on
C^{\infty}(M)
satisfies the Jacobi identity. If
V
is a vector bundle, then a Poisson structure on
V
is
linear
if
C^{\infty}_{[n]}(V)
is a subalgebra of
C^{\infty}(V)
for any
n\geq 0
. Equivalently,
\pi\in\mathfrak{X}^{2}_{[-1]}(V)
.

Example 5.3
.

Let
\omega\in\Omega^{2}(M)
be a symplectic form on
M
. Since
\omega
is non-degenerate it defines an isomorphism

\omega^{\flat}:TM\to T^{*}M,\quad X\mapsto\iota_{X}\omega.

Let
\pi^{\sharp}:T^{*}M\to TM
be the inverse map and, given
f\in C^{\infty}(M)
, let
X_{f}\in\mathfrak{X}(M)
denote the Hamiltonian vector field

X_{f}=-\pi^{\sharp}(\mathrm{d}f).

Then

\{f,g\}=\omega(X_{f},X_{g})

defines a Poisson structure on
M
. In this way, every symplectic manifold is canonically a Poisson manifold. If
x_{1},\dots,x_{m}
,
y_{1},\dots,y_{m}
are local Darboux coordinates for
M
so that
\omega=\sum_{i}\mathrm{d}x_{i}\wedge\mathrm{d}y_{i}
, then the corresponding Poisson bivector field is given by

\sum_{i}\frac{\partial}{\partial x_{i}}\wedge\frac{\partial}{\partial y_{i}}.

(42)

In particular, the cotangent bundle
T^{*}M
of
any
manifold
M
is canonically a Poisson manifold. Moreover, from (
42
) we deduce that it is a
linear
Poisson manifold.

Given a section
\sigma\in\Gamma(V)
let
f_{\sigma}\in C^{\infty}_{[1]}(V^{*})
be the corresponding linear function. The following theorem explains how linear Poisson structures are related to Lie algebroid structures.

Theorem 5.4
.

[
Cou90
, Theorem 2.1.4]

For any Lie algebroid
A\Rightarrow M
, the total space of the dual bundle
p:A^{*}\to M
has a unique Poisson structure such that for all sections
\sigma,\tau\in\Gamma(A)
,

\{f_{\sigma},f_{\tau}\}=f_{[\sigma,\tau]}.

The anchor map is described in terms of the Poisson bracket as

p^{*}(a(\sigma)f)=\{f_{\sigma},p^{*}f\},

for
f\in C^{\infty}(M)
and
\sigma\in\Gamma(A)
, while
\{p^{*}f,p^{*}g\}=0
for all functions
f
,
g
. The Poisson structure on
A^{*}
is linear; conversely, every linear Poisson structure on a vector bundle
V\to M
arises in this way from a unique Lie algebroid structure on the dual bundle
V^{*}
.

Example 5.5
.

Recall from
5.2
(a) that the tangent bundle
TM
of any manifold
M
is a Lie algebroid. The Poisson structure on
T^{*}M
described in
Theorem 5.4
is the one corresponding to the canonical symplectic structure, as described in
Example 5.3
.

17.2
Super-geometric characterization of Lie algebroids

A Lie algebroid structure on a vector bundle
A\to M
induces a differential graded algebra structure on
\Gamma(\wedge A^{*})
by setting

\displaystyle\begin{split}(\mathrm{d}_{A}\omega)(\sigma_{1},\dots,\sigma_{k})=\sum_{i<j}&(-1)^{i+j}\omega([\sigma_{i},\sigma_{j}],\sigma_{1},\ldots,\widehat{\sigma_{i}},\ldots,\widehat{\sigma_{j}},\ldots,\sigma_{k})\\
&+\sum_{i=1}^{k}(-1)^{i+1}\mathcal{L}_{a(\sigma_{i})}\omega(\sigma_{1},\ldots,\widehat{\sigma_{i}},\ldots,\sigma_{k}),\end{split}

(43)

where
\omega\in\Gamma(\wedge^{k}A^{*})
and where the hat denotes omission.

Vaintrob
[
Vai97
]
observed that the converse is also true. If
A\to M
is a vector bundle and
\mathrm{d}_{A}:\Gamma(\wedge A^{*})\to\Gamma(\wedge A^{*})
is a degree
+1
derivation satisfying
\mathrm{d}_{A}\circ\mathrm{d}_{A}=0
, then we can define a Lie algebroid structure on
A
as follows. For
\sigma\in\Gamma(A)
, let
\iota_{\sigma}:\Gamma(\wedge A^{*})\to\Gamma(\wedge A^{*})
be contraction by
\sigma
and let
\mathcal{L}_{\sigma}=\mathrm{d}_{A}\circ\iota_{\sigma}+\iota_{\sigma}\circ\mathrm{d}_{A}
. Given
\sigma_{1},\sigma_{2}\in\Gamma(A)
, define
[\sigma_{1},\sigma_{2}]\in\Gamma(A)
by the formula

\iota_{[\sigma_{1},\sigma_{2}]}=[\mathcal{L}_{\sigma_{1}},\iota_{\sigma_{2}}].

Finally, define the map
a:A\to TM
by the equation

\mathcal{L}_{a(\sigma)}f=\mathcal{L}_{\sigma}f,

where on the right hand side we are identifying
\Gamma(\wedge^{0}A^{*})
with
C^{\infty}(M)
. Then
(A,[\cdot,\cdot],a)
is a Lie algebroid over
M
. Moreover, these two constructions are inverse to one another. Summarizing, we have:

Theorem 5.6

(
[
Vai97
]
)
.

Let
A\to M
be a vector bundle, and let
\mathrm{d}_{A}
be a differential on
\Gamma(\wedge A^{*})
, that is,
\mathrm{d}_{A}
is a derivation of degree 1 with
\mathrm{d}_{A}\circ\mathrm{d}_{A}=0
. Then
\mathrm{d}_{A}
determines a unique Lie algebroid structure on
A
for which
\mathrm{d}_{A}
is the de Rham differential.

This approach has the benefit that Lie algebroid morphisms are readily defined.

Definition 5.7
.

Let
A\Rightarrow M
and
B\Rightarrow N
be Lie algebroids. A vector bundle morphism
\varphi:B\to A
is a
Lie algebroid
morphism if the pullback map
\Gamma(\wedge A^{*})\to\Gamma(\wedge B^{*})
is a cochain map.

See
[
Mei17b
, Section 12]
for more details.

Remark 5.8
.

In super-geometric terms, the graded commutative algebra
\Gamma(\wedge A^{*})
is understood as the smooth functions on a "super-manifold" denoted
1
1

1

Not to be confused with the shifted weighting, as described in
Section 10.2
(c).

A[1]
. The differential
\mathrm{d}_{A}
is a derivation of this graded commutative algebra, hence can be thought of as a vector field
H
on
A[1]
. The condition that
\mathrm{d}_{A}\circ\mathrm{d}_{A}=0
is equivalent to
[H,H]=2H^{2}=0
. Such a vector field is called a
homological vector field
. One may therefore restate
Theorem 5.6
as saying that a Lie algebroid is a vector bundle
A\to M
together with a homological vector field
H
.

17.3
The Lie functor

We now explain how to associate a Lie algebroid to a Lie groupoid
G\rightrightarrows M
; our approach is somewhat non-standard, so for more details see
[
Mei17a
, Section 11]
. As a vector bundle, let
\mathrm{Lie}(G)=\nu(G,M)\to M
be the normal bundle of the units. Recall that the tangent bundle
TG
is a VB-groupoid, hence dually we obtain the cotangent groupoid

\textstyle{T^{*}G\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\mathrm{Lie}(G)^{*}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{G\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{M}

The canonical Poisson structure on
T^{*}G
defines a Poisson structure on
\mathrm{Lie}(G)^{*}
by the formula

\{s_{T^{*}G}^{*}f,s_{T^{*}G}^{*}g\}_{T^{*}G}=s_{T^{*}G}^{*}\{f,g\}_{\mathrm{Lie}(G)^{*}},

(44)

which is linear since the Poisson structure on
T^{*}G
is linear. By
Theorem 5.4
, this gives
\mathrm{Lie}(G)
the structure of a Lie algebroid.

We can describe this Lie algebroid structure more concretely as follows. Since the source and target maps
s_{G},t_{G}:G\to M
agree on
M
, it follows that
Ts-Tt:TG\to TM
vanishes on
TM
and therefore descends to a map
a:\mathrm{Lie}(G)\to TM
; we take this to be the anchor map. To describe the bracket on sections, let
\sigma\in\Gamma(\mathrm{Lie}(G))
and let
f_{\sigma}\in C^{\infty}_{[1]}(\mathrm{Lie}(G)^{*})
be the corresponding linear function. Since
s_{T^{*}G}:T^{*}G\to\mathrm{Lie}(G)^{*}
is a vector bundle morphism, the pullback
s_{T^{*}G}^{*}f_{\sigma}\in C^{\infty}_{[1]}(T^{*}G)
is a linear function and therefore corresponds to a vector field
\sigma^{L}\in\mathfrak{X}(G)
; it is called the
left-invariant extension
of
\sigma\in\Gamma(\mathrm{Lie}(G))
. The Lie bracket on
\Gamma(\mathrm{Lie}(G))
is the determined by the equation

[\sigma^{L},\tau^{L}]=[\sigma,\tau]^{L}.

Similarly, we define the
right-invariant
extension of
\sigma\in\Gamma(\mathrm{Lie}(G))
to be the vector field
\sigma^{R}\in\mathfrak{X}(G)
corresponding to
-t_{T^{*}G}^{*}f_{\sigma}\in C^{\infty}_{[1]}(T^{*}G)
.

Example 5.9

(
[
Mac05
, Example 11.3.1]
)
.

Let
G=\mathrm{Pair}(M)\rightrightarrows M
. In this case, we identify
\mathrm{Lie}(\mathrm{Pair}(TM))
with
TM
using the map

TM\oplus TM\to TM,\quad(X,Y)\mapsto Y-X.

The cotangent groupoid of
\mathrm{Pair}(M)
is the VB-groupoid

\textstyle{T^{*}\mathrm{Pair}(M)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{T^{*}M\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\mathrm{Pair}(M)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{M,}

where

s_{T^{*}\mathrm{Pair}(M)}(\xi,\tau)=\tau\quad\text{and}\quad t_{T^{*}\mathrm{Pair}(M)}(\xi,\tau)=-\xi.

Given a vector field
X\in\mathfrak{X}(M)
, we have that

s^{*}_{T^{*}\mathrm{Pair}(M)}f_{X}=f_{(0,X)}\quad\text{and}\quad t^{*}_{T^{*}\mathrm{Pair}(M)}f_{X}=f_{(-X,0)},

hence
X^{L}=(0,X)\in\mathfrak{X}(\mathrm{Pair}(M))
and
X^{R}=(X,0)\in\mathfrak{X}(\mathrm{Pair}(M))
.

For reference, we record the following fact.

Lemma 5.10
.

Let
G\rightrightarrows M
be a Lie groupoid with Lie algebroid
\mathrm{Lie}(G)\Rightarrow M
. Then for all
\sigma,\tau\in\Gamma(\mathrm{Lie}(G))
:

(a)

a(\sigma)\sim_{i}\sigma^{L}-\sigma^{R}
, where
i:M\to G
is the inclusion, and

(b)

[\sigma^{L},\tau^{R}]=0
and
[\sigma^{R},\tau^{R}]=-[\sigma,\tau]^{R}
.

18
Definition of Infinitesimally Multiplicative Weightings

Let
A\Rightarrow M
be a Lie algebroid.

Definition 5.11
.

An
infinitesimally multiplicative weighting
of
A\Rightarrow M
is a linear weighting of
A
with the additional properties that

(a)

a(\Gamma(A)_{(i)})\subseteq\mathfrak{X}(M)_{(i)}
for all
i
, and

(b)

for all
\sigma\in\Gamma(A)_{(i)}
and
\tau\in\Gamma(A)_{(j)}
, we have

[\sigma,\tau]\in\Gamma(A)_{(i+j)}.

A Lie algebroid endowed with an infinitesimally multiplicative weighting is called a
weighted Lie algebroid
.

Examples 5.12
.

(a)

If
B\Rightarrow N
is a Lie subalgebroid of
A\Rightarrow M
, then the trivial weighting of
A
along
B
is infinitesimally multiplicative.

(b)

If
(M,N)
is a weighted pair, then
TM
with its induced weighting is a weighted Lie algebroid.

(c)

If
\pi:M\to N
is a weighted submersion, then
\ker(T\pi)
is infinitesimally weighted via the filtration

\Gamma(\ker(T\pi))_{(i)}=\{X\in\mathfrak{X}(M)_{(i)}:X\sim_{\pi}0\}.

(d)

Recall from
Remark 3.5
that a linear weighting of a vector space is simply a filtration by subspaces. Hence, an infinitesimally multiplicative weighting of a Lie algebra
\mathfrak{g}
is given by a filtration of
\mathfrak{g}
by subspaces
\mathfrak{g}_{i}
with the property that

[\mathfrak{g}_{i},\mathfrak{g}_{j}]\subseteq\mathfrak{g}_{i+j}.

(45)

If
(G,H)
is a weighted Lie group pair then the filtration of
TG|_{H}
restricts to a filtration of
\mathfrak{g}
satisfying (
45
). Hence the Lie algebra of a weighted Lie group inherits an infinitesimally multiplicative weighting.

(e)

An action of a weighted Lie algebra
\mathfrak{g}
on a weighted manifold
M
is called
weighted
if the action map

\mathfrak{g}\times M\to TM,\quad(\xi,p)\mapsto\xi_{M}(p)

is a morphism of weighted vector bundles. In this case, the
action algebroid

\mathfrak{g}\times M\Rightarrow M

is a weighted Lie algebroid.

(f)

Any linearly weighted vector bundle can be thought of as a weighted Lie algebroid by taking both the anchor and bracket to be zero.

(g)

If
G
is a weighted Lie group and
P\to M
is a principal
G
-bundle with a principal weighting along
Q\to N
, then the Atiyah algebroid
\mathrm{At}(P)\Rightarrow M
has a canonical Lie algebroid weighting along
\mathrm{At}(Q)\Rightarrow N
. The filtration of the sections is defined by the identification with
G
-invariant vector fields on
P
. Similarly, the Lie algebroid
\mathfrak{gau}(P)=(P\times\mathfrak{g})/G\Rightarrow M
has a canonical infinitesimally multiplicative weighting along
\mathfrak{gau}(Q)\Rightarrow N
. Moreover,

0\overset{}{\longrightarrow}\mathfrak{gau}(P)\overset{}{\longrightarrow}\mathrm{At}(P)\overset{}{\longrightarrow}TM\overset{}{\longrightarrow}0

is an exact sequence of weighted vector bundles in the sense that one can find a splitting
TM\to\mathrm{At}(P)
which is a weighted vector bundle morphism.

(h)

An infinitesimally multiplicative weighting of a Lie algebroid
A\Rightarrow M
along the zero subalgebroid is given by a filtration

A=F_{-r}\supseteq F_{-r+1}\supseteq\cdots\supseteq 0

such that
[\Gamma(F_{-i}),\Gamma(F_{-j})]\subseteq\Gamma(F_{-i-j})
. If
A=\mathrm{Lie}(G)
for some Lie groupoid
G
, then this is a
Lie filtration
of
G
, in the sense of van Erp and Yuncken (cf.
[
VEY17
, Definition 17]
). In particular, a Lie filtration of
M
is a infinitesimally multiplicative weighting of
TM
along the zero section.

(i)

Let
(M,N)
be a weighted pair and let
\omega\in\Omega^{2}(M)
be closed. Let
A=TM\times\mathbb{R}\Rightarrow M
be the Lie algebroid defined in
5.2
(f), with

[X+f,Y+g]=[X,Y]+\mathcal{L}_{X}f-\mathcal{L}_{Y}g+\omega(X,Y)

Then
\Gamma(A)_{(i)}=\mathfrak{X}(TM)_{(i)}\oplus C^{\infty}(M)_{(i)}
is an infinitesimally multiplicative weighting if and only if
\omega\in\Omega^{2}(M)_{(0)}
.

Let
A\Rightarrow M
be a weighted Lie algebroid. Given a frame weighted
\sigma_{a}
for
A|_{U}
let
\Gamma_{ab}^{c}\in C^{\infty}(U)
be the corresponding structure functions for the Lie algebroid, defined by

[\sigma_{a},\sigma_{b}]=\sum_{c}\Gamma_{ab}^{c}\sigma_{c}.

Since
\sum_{c}\Gamma_{ab}^{c}\sigma_{c}\in\Gamma(V|_{U})_{(v_{a}+v_{b})}
if and only if
\Gamma_{ab}^{c}\in C^{\infty}(U)_{(v_{a}+v_{b}-v_{c})}
, we have the following lemma.

Lemma 5.13
.

A linear weighting of a Lie algebroid
A\Rightarrow M
is infinitesimally multiplicative if and only if the anchor map is a weighted vector bundle morphism and for any choice of weighted frame
\sigma_{a}\in\Gamma(A|_{U})_{(v_{a})}
the associated structure functions satisfy
\Gamma_{ab}^{c}\in C^{\infty}(U)_{(v_{a}+v_{b}-v_{c})}
.

Remark 5.14
.

For a weighted Lie algebra
\mathfrak{g}
with structure constants
\Gamma_{ab}^{c}
, this says that
\Gamma_{ab}^{c}=0
whenever
v_{a}+v_{b}>v_{c}
.

19
Alternative Characterizations of Lie Algebroid Weightings

We now explain the weighted analogue of the characterizations of Lie algebroids described in
Section 17.1
and
Section 17.2
. The latter perspective allows for quick definition of weighted Lie algebroid morphisms, whereas the former will be used in our proof of
Theorem 5.20
, which says that the Lie algebroid of a weighted Lie groupoid has a canonical infinitesimally multiplicative weighting.

Before we begin, we remind the reader of
Section 10.2
and
Theorem 3.8
, where we explain how a linear weighting of
V\to M
defines a linear weighting of
V^{*}\to M
and how this can be described in terms of a filtration of
C^{\infty}_{pol}(V^{*})
.

Theorem 5.15
.

Let
A\Rightarrow M
be a Lie algebroid endowed with a linear weighting. Then the following are equivalent.

(a)

A
is a weighted Lie algebroid,

(b)

the Poisson bivector field
\pi\in\mathfrak{X}^{2}(A^{*})
has filtration degree zero,

(c)

the differential
\mathrm{d}_{A}:\Gamma(\wedge A^{*})\to\Gamma(\wedge A^{*})
is filtration preserving.

Proof that (a) and (b) are equivalent.

Let
\{\cdot,\cdot\}_{\pi}
be the Poisson bracket defined by
\pi
. By considering weighted vector bundle coordinates we see that
\pi\in\mathfrak{X}^{2}_{[-1]}(A^{*})
has filtration degree zero if and only if

\{\cdot,\cdot\}_{\pi}:C^{\infty}_{[n]}(A^{*})_{(i)}\times C^{\infty}_{[n]}(A^{*})_{(j)}\to C^{\infty}_{[n]}(A^{*})_{(i+j)}\quad

(46)

for n=0,1.

We start with
n=1
. Given
\sigma\in\Gamma(A)_{(i)}
and
\tau\in\Gamma(A)_{(j)}
, let
f_{\sigma}\in C^{\infty}_{[1]}(V^{*})_{(i)}
and
f_{\tau}\in C^{\infty}_{[1]}(V^{*})_{(j)}
be the corresponding linear functions. The equation

\{f_{\sigma},f_{\tau}\}_{\pi}=f_{[\sigma,\tau]}

shows that
[\sigma,\tau]\in\Gamma(V)_{(i+j)}
if and only if
\{f_{\sigma},f_{\tau}\}_{\pi}\in C^{\infty}_{[1]}(V^{*})_{(i+j)}
, i.e. (
46
) holds for
n=1
.

Now let
f\in C^{\infty}(M)_{(j)}
. Since the bundle projection
p:A^{*}\to M
is a weighted morphism and
M
is a weighted submanifold of
A^{*}
, the equation

p^{*}(\mathcal{L}_{a(\sigma)}f)=\{f_{\sigma},p^{*}f\}_{\pi}

shows that
\{f_{\sigma},p^{*}f\}_{\pi}\in C^{\infty}_{[1]}(V^{*})_{(i+j)}
if and only if
\mathcal{L}_{a(\sigma)}f\in C^{\infty}(M)_{(i+j)}
. Since
f\in C^{\infty}(M)_{(j)}
was arbitrary, the latter holds if and only if
a(\sigma)\in\mathfrak{X}(M)_{(i)}
. Thus (
46
) holds for
n=0
if and only if
a:\Gamma(V)_{(i)}\to\mathfrak{X}(M)_{(i)}
, which completes the proof.
∎

Proof that (a) and (c) are equivalent.

Suppose that
A
is a weighted Lie algebroid and let
\sigma_{j}\in\Gamma(V)_{(i_{j})}
and
\omega\in\Gamma(\wedge^{k}A^{*})_{(i)}
. Since
[\sigma_{\ell},\sigma_{j}]\in\Gamma(V)_{(i_{\ell}+i_{j})}
and
a(\sigma_{j})\in\mathfrak{X}(M)_{(i_{j})}
it follows that both

\displaystyle\omega([\sigma_{\ell},\sigma_{j}],\sigma_{1},\dots,\hat{\sigma}_{\ell},\dots,\hat{\sigma}_{j},\dots,\sigma_{k})\quad\text{and}\quad\mathcal{L}_{a(\sigma_{\ell})}\omega(\sigma_{1},\dots,\hat{\sigma}_{\ell},\dots,\sigma_{k+1})

are in
C^{\infty}(M)_{(i+i_{1}+\cdots+i_{k+1})}
. It follows by
Equation 43
that

(\mathrm{d}_{A}\omega)(\sigma_{1},\dots,\sigma_{k+1})\in C^{\infty}(M)_{(i+i_{1}+\cdots+i_{k+1})},

hence
\mathrm{d}_{A}
is filtration preserving.

For the converse, recall that the weighting of
\wedge A^{*}
is defined so that
\iota_{\sigma}:\Gamma(\wedge A^{*})_{(j)}\to\Gamma(\wedge A^{*})_{(i+j)}
if and only if
\sigma\in\Gamma(A)_{(i)}
. In particular, if
\mathrm{d}_{A}
is filtration preserving then

\mathcal{L}_{\sigma}=\mathrm{d}_{A}\circ\iota_{\sigma}+\iota_{\sigma}\circ\mathrm{d}_{A}:\Gamma(\wedge A^{*})_{(j)}\to\Gamma(\wedge A^{*})_{(i+j)}

whenever
\sigma\in\Gamma(A)_{(i)}
. Thus, given
\sigma_{1}\in\Gamma(A)_{(i)}
and
\sigma_{2}\in\Gamma(A)_{(j)}
, the formula
\iota_{[\sigma_{1},\sigma_{2}]}=[\mathcal{L}_{\sigma_{1}},\iota_{\sigma_{2}}]
implies that
[\sigma_{1},\sigma_{2}]\in\Gamma(A)_{(i+j)}
. Similarly, given
\sigma\in\Gamma(A)_{(i)}
, the formula
\mathcal{L}_{a(\sigma)}f=\mathcal{L}_{\sigma}f
implies that
a(\sigma)\in\mathfrak{X}(M)_{(i)}
.
∎

As promised, we give the following definition.

Definition 5.16
.

Let
A\Rightarrow M
and
B\Rightarrow N
be weighted Lie algebroids. A weighted Lie algebroid morphism
\varphi:B\to A
is a vector bundle morphism such that pullback map
\Gamma(\wedge A^{*})\to\Gamma(\wedge B^{*})
is a filtration preserving cochain map.

20
The Weighted Normal and Weighted Deformation Algebroids

We now explain how the weighted normal bundle and weighted deformation bundles of a weighted Lie algebroid
A\Rightarrow M
are naturally Lie algebroids.

Theorem 5.17
.

Let
A\Rightarrow M
be a weighted Lie algebroid. Then both
\nu_{\mathcal{W}}(A)\to\nu_{\mathcal{W}}(M,N)
and
\delta_{\mathcal{W}}(A)\to\delta_{\mathcal{W}}(M,N)
are naturally Lie algebroids and the isomorphism

\delta_{\mathcal{W}}(A)|_{\pi_{\delta}^{-1}(t)}\to\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}A&t\neq 0\\
\nu_{\mathcal{W}}(A)&t=0\end{array}\right.

is an isomorphism of Lie algebroids.

Proof.

Recall from
3.23
(c) that
\nu_{\mathcal{W}}(TM)=T\nu_{\mathcal{W}}(M,N)
. Since
a:A\to TM
is a weighted morphism,
\nu_{\mathcal{W}}(a):\nu_{\mathcal{W}}(A)\to\nu_{\mathcal{W}}(TM)
is defined and we take this to be our anchor for
\nu_{\mathcal{W}}(A)
. We define the Lie bracket as follows. Given
\sigma\in\Gamma(A)_{(i)}
and
\tau\in\Gamma(V)_{(j)}
define

[\sigma^{[i]},\tau^{[j]}]=[\sigma,\tau]^{[i+j]};

(47)

this defines a Lie bracket
\mathrm{gr}(\Gamma(A))\times\mathrm{gr}(\Gamma(A))\to\mathrm{gr}(\Gamma(A))
.
Given
f\in C^{\infty}(M)_{(k)}
, we compute

\displaystyle[\sigma^{[i]},f^{[k]}\tau^{[j]}]

\displaystyle=[\sigma^{[i]},(f\tau)^{[j+k]}]

\displaystyle=[\sigma,f\tau]^{[i+j+k]}

\displaystyle=(f[\sigma,\tau]+\mathcal{L}_{a(\sigma)}f\cdot\tau)^{[i+j+k]}

\displaystyle=f^{[k]}[\sigma,\tau]^{[i+j]}+\mathcal{L}_{a(\sigma)^{[i]}}f^{[k]}\cdot\tau^{[j]}

\displaystyle=f^{[k]}[\sigma,\tau]^{[i+j]}+\mathcal{L}_{\nu_{\mathcal{W}}(a)(\sigma^{[i]})}f^{[k]}\cdot\tau^{[j]},

hence (
47
) satisfies the Leibniz rule for any
f\in C^{\infty}_{pol}(\nu_{\mathcal{W}}(M,N))=\mathrm{gr}(C^{\infty}(M))
. Recall from
Theorem 3.29
that

\Gamma(\nu_{\mathcal{W}}(A))=C^{\infty}(\nu_{\mathcal{W}}(M,N))\otimes_{\mathrm{gr}(C^{\infty}(M))}\mathrm{gr}(\Gamma(A)),

hence we may extend (
47
) to
\Gamma(\nu_{\mathcal{W}}(A))
using the Leibniz rule, which gives the Lie algebroid structure for
\nu_{\mathcal{W}}(A)
. An analogous construction gives the Lie algebroid structure for
\delta_{\mathcal{W}}(A)
. The last statement is clear.
∎

Remarks 5.18
.

(a)

If
\Gamma_{ij}^{k}\in C^{\infty}(U)_{(v_{i}+v_{j}-v_{k})}
are the local structure functions for
A|_{U}
corresponding to a local weighted frame, then

(\Gamma_{ij}^{k})^{[v_{i}+v_{j}-v_{k}]}\quad\text{and}\quad\widetilde{(\Gamma_{ij}^{k})}^{[v_{i}+v_{j}-v_{k}]}

are the local structure functions for
\nu_{\mathcal{W}}(A)
and
\delta_{\mathcal{W}}(A)
, respectively.

(b)

If
\pi\in\mathfrak{X}^{2}(A^{*})_{(0)}
is the Poisson bivector field then
\pi^{[0]}\in\mathfrak{X}^{2}(\nu_{\mathcal{W}}(A)^{*})
and
\widetilde{\pi}^{[0]}\in\mathfrak{X}^{2}(\delta_{\mathcal{W}}(A)^{*})
are the Poisson bivector fields for
\nu_{\mathcal{W}}(A)^{*}
and
\delta_{\mathcal{W}}(A)^{*}
, respectively. .

(c)

The de Rham differentials for
\nu_{\mathcal{W}}(A)
and
\delta_{\mathcal{W}}(A)
are defined by the equations

\mathrm{d}_{\nu_{\mathcal{W}}(A)}\omega^{[i]}=(\mathrm{d}_{A}\omega)^{[i]}\quad\text{and}\quad\mathrm{d}_{\delta_{\mathcal{W}}(A)}\widetilde{\omega}^{[i]}=\widetilde{(\mathrm{d}_{A}\omega)}^{[i]},

respectively.

(d)

By the last remark, we see that this construction is functorial for weighted Lie algebroid morphisms.

Examples 5.19
.

(a)

Let
A=A_{-r}\oplus\cdots\oplus A_{0}\Rightarrow M
be a graded Lie algebroid, i.e.
[A_{i},A_{j}]\subseteq A_{i+j}
. If
M
is weighted along itself then the filtration

\Gamma(A)_{(-i)}=\Gamma(A_{-i})\oplus\cdots\oplus\Gamma(A_{0})

defines an infinitesimally multiplicative weighting.

In particular, we can view any Lie algebroid
A\Rightarrow M
as a graded Lie algebroid by setting
A=A_{-r}
. The corresponding bracket on
\delta_{\mathcal{W}}(A)
is given by

[\widetilde{\sigma}^{[-r]},\widetilde{\tau}^{[-r]}]=\widetilde{[\sigma,\tau]}^{[-2r]}=t^{r}[\sigma,\tau].

(b)

Let
A\Rightarrow M
be a Lie algebroid with Lie filtration

A=A_{-r}\supseteq A_{-r+1}\supseteq\cdots\supseteq A_{-1}\supseteq 0.

This defines an infinitesimally multiplicative weighted of
A
and the weighted normal algebroid is given by
\mathrm{gr}(A)=\bigoplus_{i=1}^{r}A_{-i}/A_{-i+1}
. Since
\mathfrak{X}(M)=\mathfrak{X}(M)_{(0)}
, then anchor map for
\nu_{\mathcal{W}}(A)
is zero and
\nu_{\mathcal{W}}(A)
is a family of nilpotent Lie algebras.

(c)

If
(M,N)
is a weighted pair,
\omega\in\Omega(M)_{(0)}
, and
TM\times\mathbb{R}\Rightarrow M
is the Lie algebroid defined in
5.12
, then
\nu_{\mathcal{W}}(TM\times\mathbb{R})=T\nu_{\mathcal{W}}(M,N)\times\mathbb{R}\Rightarrow\nu_{\mathcal{W}}(M,N)
is the Lie algebroid defined analogously, using
\omega^{[0]}\in\Omega^{2}(\nu_{\mathcal{W}}(M,N))
.

21
Differentiation of Multiplicative Weightings

Let
G\rightrightarrows M
be a weighted groupoid with Lie algebroid
A\Rightarrow M
. In this section we will show that the weighting of
G
differentiates to an infinitesimally multiplicative weighting of
A
, and the the Lie functor commutes with both a weighted normal and weighted deformation functors. More specifically, we prove the following theorem.

Theorem 5.20
.

Let
G\rightrightarrows M
be a weighted Lie groupoid with Lie algebroid
A=\mathrm{Lie}(G)\Rightarrow M
. Then

\Gamma(A|_{U})_{(i)}=\{\sigma\in\Gamma(A|_{U}):\sigma^{L}\in\mathfrak{X}^{L}(G|_{U})_{(i)}\}

(48)

defines an infinitesimally multiplicative weighting of
A
such that

\mathrm{Lie}(\nu_{\mathcal{W}}(G,H))=\nu_{\mathcal{W}}(A)\quad\text{and}\quad\mathrm{Lie}(\delta_{\mathcal{W}}(G,H))=\delta_{\mathcal{W}}(A).

The idea of the proof is as follows. Since
G\rightrightarrows M
is a weighted Lie groupoid, the cotangent groupoid
T^{*}G\rightrightarrows A^{*}
is a weighted VB-groupoid, as per
4.20
. In particular,
A^{*}
is a weighted subbundle of
T^{*}G
and is therefore weighted in its own right. We then argue that the Poisson structure on
A^{*}
induced by the canonical one on
T^{*}G
has filtration degree zero. By
Theorem 5.15
, this implies the dual weighting on
A
is infinitesimally multiplicative. We then verify that (
48
) agrees with the dual weighting.

To begin, note that for any weighted manifold
M
the canonical Poisson structure on
T^{*}M
has filtration degree zero. Indeed, the follows from
Theorem 5.15
since the weighting of
TM
is infinitesimally multiplicative. In particular, for a weighted Lie groupoid
G
the canonical Poisson structure on
T^{*}G
has filtration degree zero. Recall that the Poisson structure on
A^{*}
is defined by the equation

\{s_{T^{*}G}^{*}f,s_{T^{*}G}^{*}g\}_{T^{*}G}=s_{T^{*}G}^{*}\{f,g\}_{A^{*}},

(49)

where
s_{T^{*}G}:T^{*}G\to AG^{*}
is the source map for
T^{*}G
.

Lemma 5.21
.

The Poisson bivector field on
A^{*}
defined by (
49
) has filtration degree zero.

Proof.

We have to show that

f\in C^{\infty}_{pol}(A^{*})_{(i)},\ g\in C^{\infty}_{pol}(A^{*})_{(j)}\implies\{f,g\}_{A^{*}}\in C^{\infty}_{pol}(A^{*})_{(i+j)}.

We claim that this happens if and only if
s^{*}\{f,g\}_{A^{*}}\in C^{\infty}_{pol}(T^{*}G)_{(i+j)}
. Indeed, since
s:T^{*}G\to A^{*}
is a weighted morphism we have that

\{f,g\}_{A^{*}}\in C^{\infty}_{pol}(A^{*})_{(i+j)}\implies s^{*}\{f,g\}_{A^{*}}\in C^{\infty}_{pol}(T^{*}G)_{(i+j)}.

On the other hand, since
AG^{*}
is a weighted subbundle of
T^{*}G
, the inclusion
i:A^{*}\hookrightarrow T^{*}G
is a weighted morphism. Hence

\displaystyle s^{*}\{f,g\}_{A^{*}}\in C^{\infty}_{pol}(T^{*}G)_{(i+j)}\implies\{f,g\}_{A^{*}}=i^{*}s^{*}\{f,g\}_{A^{*}}\in C^{\infty}_{pol}(A^{*})_{(i+j)}

which proves the claim. Combining this with the fact that the canonical Poisson structure on
T^{*}G
is filtration preserving and using (
49
) completes the proof.
∎

Proof of
Theorem 5.20
.

By
Lemma 5.21
and
Theorem 5.15
the weighting of
A
defined as the dual of
A^{*}
is infinitesimally multiplicative. It remains to establish that this weighting is given by (
48
).

Given
\sigma\in\Gamma(A|_{U})
, let
f_{\sigma}\in C^{\infty}_{[1]}(A|_{U}^{*})
be the corresponding linear function. Recall that
\sigma^{L}\in\mathfrak{X}(G|_{U})
is the vector field corresponding to the linear function
s^{*}_{T^{*}G}f_{\sigma}\in C^{\infty}_{[1]}(T^{*}G)
. Using this and the fact that
s_{T^{*}G}:T^{*}G\to A^{*}
is a weighted submersion we have

\displaystyle\sigma\in\Gamma(A|_{U})_{(i)}

\displaystyle\iff f_{\sigma}\in C^{\infty}_{[1]}(A|_{U}^{*})_{(i)}

\displaystyle\iff s^{*}_{T^{*}G}f_{\sigma}\in C^{\infty}_{[1]}(T^{*}G|_{U})_{(i)}

\displaystyle\iff\sigma^{L}\in\mathfrak{X}(G|_{U})_{(i)},

as claimed. The identification
\mathrm{Lie}(\nu_{\mathcal{W}}(G,H))=\nu_{\mathcal{W}}(A)
is given at the level of sections by the map

\mathrm{gr}(\Gamma(A))\to\mathfrak{X}^{L}(\nu_{\mathcal{W}}(G,H)),\quad\sigma^{[i]}\mapsto(\sigma^{L})^{[i]}.

Similarly, the identification
\mathrm{Lie}(\delta_{\mathcal{W}}(G,H))=\delta_{\mathcal{W}}(A)
is given by

\mathrm{Rees}(\Gamma(A))\to\mathfrak{X}^{L}(\delta_{\mathcal{W}}(G,H)),\quad\widetilde{\sigma}^{[i]}\mapsto\widetilde{(\sigma^{L})}^{[i]}.\qed

Remark 5.22
.

Since
\mathrm{inv}_{G}
is a weighted diffeomorphism and
\sigma^{L}\sim_{\mathrm{inv}_{G}}-\sigma^{R}
, it follows that

\sigma\in\Gamma(A)_{(i)}\iff\sigma^{R}\in\mathfrak{X}(G)_{(i)}.

22
Integration of Lie Algebroid Weightings - the Wide Case

In this section we give a partial converse to
Theorem 5.20
. Notice from the previous work that any infinitesimally multiplicative weighting defined by differentiating a multiplicative weighting is necessarily concentrated in non-positive degrees.

Theorem 5.23
.

Suppose that
G\rightrightarrows M
is a Lie groupoid and

A=A_{-r}\supseteq A_{-r+1}\supseteq\cdots A_{-1}\supseteq 0

(50)

is a Lie filtration of
A=\mathrm{Lie}(G)
. Suppose that
H\subseteq G
is an
s
-connected Lie subgroupoid. If
B=\mathrm{Lie}(H)
is such that

(a)

[\Gamma(B),\Gamma(A_{-i})]\subseteq\Gamma(A_{-i})
for all
i

(b)

the assignment
m\mapsto\dim(B_{m}+A_{-i}|_{m})
is constant as a function on
M

then (
50
) defines a multiplicative weighting of
G
along
H
such that the induced weighting of
A
defined by
Theorem 5.20
is given by the filtration

A=A_{-r}+B\supseteq A_{-r+1}+B\supseteq\cdots A_{-1}+B\supseteq B.

(51)

The idea of the proof is to use the filtration (
50
) to define a singular Lie filtration of
G
with respect to which
H
is clean. By
Theorem 2.54
, this defines a weighting of
G
along
H
which we then show that this weighting is multiplicative.

22.1
Definition of the singular Lie filtration

Let
F_{-i}^{L}\subseteq TG
be the subbundle spanned by the left-invariant vector fields
\sigma^{L}\in\mathfrak{X}(G)
for
\sigma\in\Gamma(A_{-i})
, and let
F_{-i}^{R}
be defined analogously using right-invariant extensions. Motivated by Equation (
48
) and
Remark 5.22
, we define

\mathcal{F}_{(-i)}=\Gamma(F_{-i}^{L})+\Gamma(F_{-i}^{R})\subseteq\mathfrak{X}(G),\quad i=1,\dots,r-1

(52)

and
\mathcal{F}_{(-r)}=\mathfrak{X}(G)
.

Lemma 5.24
.

\mathcal{F}_{\bullet}
is a singular Lie filtration.

Proof.

Since
F_{-i}^{L}
and
F_{-i}^{R}
are vector bundles, it follows that each
\mathcal{F}_{(-i)}
is locally finitely generated. To see that the bracket condition is satisfied, let
\sigma\in\Gamma(A_{-i})
,
\tau\in\Gamma(A_{-j})
, and
f\in C^{\infty}(G)
. Since (
50
) is a Lie filtration, we have

[\sigma^{L},f\tau^{L}]=f[\sigma,\tau]^{L}+\mathcal{L}_{\sigma^{L}}f\cdot\tau^{L}\in\mathcal{F}_{(-i-j)},

because
\mathcal{L}_{\sigma^{L}}f\cdot\tau^{L}\in\mathcal{F}_{(-j)}\subseteq\mathcal{F}_{(-i-j)}
as
-i-j\leq-j
; the same computation works for right invariant vector fields. Finally, since left and right invariant vector fields commute, we have

[\sigma^{L},f\tau^{R}]=\mathcal{L}_{\sigma^{L}}f\cdot\tau^{R}\in\mathcal{F}_{(-i-j)},

as needed.
∎

22.2
Showing that
H
is
\mathcal{F}_{\bullet}
-clean.

In order to show that (
52
) defines a weighting of
G
along
H
, we need to show that
H
is
\mathcal{F}_{\bullet}
-clean. Recall (cf.
Definition 2.52
) that this means that the function

H\to\mathbb{N},\quad h\mapsto\mathrm{dim}(T_{h}H+\mathcal{F}_{(-i)}|_{h})

is constant for each
i=1,\dots,r
.

Before proceeding, we fix some notation. Let
\sigma\in\Gamma_{c}(B)
be a compactly supported section of
B
. Since
\sigma
is compactly support, the corresponding left-invariant extension
\sigma^{L}\in\mathfrak{X}(H)
is complete (cf.
[
ZCL09
]
), and so
\sigma
defines a bisection
\exp(\sigma)\in\Gamma(H)
by the formula

\exp(\sigma):M\to H,\quad m\mapsto\phi^{1}(m)

where
\phi^{1}
denotes the time 1 flow of the left-invariant vector field
\sigma^{L}
.

Lemma 5.25
.

H
is
\mathcal{F}_{\bullet}
-clean.

Proof.

To begin we claim that, for any
m\in M
, one has

T_{m}H+\mathcal{F}_{(-i)}|_{m}=T_{m}H+(A_{-i})_{m}.

Indeed, let
X_{m}+\sigma_{m}^{L}+\tau_{m}^{R}\in T_{m}H+\mathcal{F}_{(-i)}|_{m}
and observe that

\displaystyle X_{m}+\sigma_{m}^{L}+\tau_{m}^{R}

\displaystyle=X_{m}+\sigma_{m}^{L}-\sigma_{m}^{R}+\sigma_{m}^{R}+\tau_{m}^{R}

\displaystyle=(X_{m}+a(\sigma_{m}))+(\sigma_{m}+\tau_{m})^{R}\in T_{m}H+(A_{-i})_{m},

where was have used
Lemma 5.10
, which says that the restriction of
\sigma^{L}-\sigma^{R}
to
M
is given by
a(\sigma)
.

Next, let
h\in H
. Since
H
is
s
-connected, we may choose compactly supported sections
\sigma_{1},\dots,\sigma_{k}\in\Gamma_{c}(B)
such that the bisection

S=\exp(\sigma_{1})\cdots\exp(\sigma_{k})\in\Gamma(H)

passes through
h^{-1}
. Let
X_{h}+\sigma_{h}^{L}+\tau_{h}^{R}\in T_{h}H+\mathcal{F}_{-i}|_{h}
. Since
\exp(\mathrm{ad}_{\sigma})=\mathrm{Ad}(\exp(\sigma))
as operators on
\Gamma(A)
, it follows that
\mathrm{ad}_{\Gamma(B)}(\Gamma(A_{-i}))\subseteq\Gamma(A_{-i})
implies
\mathrm{Ad}_{\exp(\sigma)}(A_{-i})\subseteq A_{-i}
. Therefore,

T_{h}\mathcal{A}^{L}_{S}(X_{h}+\sigma_{h}^{L}+\tau_{h}^{R})=T_{h}\mathcal{A}^{L}_{S}(X_{h})+\sigma_{s(h)}^{L}+(\mathrm{Ad}_{S}\tau)^{R}_{s(h)}\in T_{s(h)}H+\mathcal{F}_{-i}|_{s(h)}.

This shows that

\displaystyle\dim(T_{h}H+\mathcal{F}_{-i}|_{h})

\displaystyle=\dim(T_{s(h)}H+\mathcal{F}_{-i}|_{s(h)})

\displaystyle=\dim(T_{s(h)}H+(A_{-i})_{s(h)})

\displaystyle=\dim(T_{s(h)}M)+\dim(B_{s(h)}+A_{-i}|_{s(h)})

which is constant as a function of
h\in H
by assumption.
∎

Therefore, by
[
LM23
, Theorem 4.1]
, the Lie filtration
\mathcal{F}_{\bullet}
defines a weighting of
G
along
H
such that
(TG|_{H})_{(-i)}=TH+(F_{-i}^{L}+F_{-i}^{R})|_{H}
.

22.3
Verification the weighting is multiplicative

We conclude by showing that the weighting of
G
along
H
is multiplicative. Since
H
is a wide subgroupoid, it follows from
Corollary 2.18
that
M
is a weighted submanifold of
G
and the induced weighting of
M
is along itself. It remains to show two things:

(a)

the bundles

(TG|_{H})_{(-i)}=TH+(F_{-i}^{L}+F_{-i}^{R})|_{H}

are wide VB-subgroupoids of
TG|_{H}\rightrightarrows TM
and

(b)

the graph of multiplication is a weighted submanifold of
G^{3}
.

To see why (b) holds, recall from
Example 4.5
that the composition rule for
TG
is given by

X_{g_{0}}\circ Y_{g_{1}}=T_{g_{0}}\mathcal{A}^{R}_{S_{1}}(X_{g_{0}})+T_{g_{1}}\mathcal{A}^{L}_{S_{0}}(Y_{g_{1}})-T_{g_{1}}(\mathcal{A}^{L}_{S_{0}}\mathcal{A}^{R}_{S_{1}}t)(Y_{g_{1}}),

(53)

where
X_{g_{0}}\in T_{g_{0}}G
and
Y_{g_{1}}\in T_{g_{1}}G
are composable,
S_{0}
is a bisection through
g_{0}
and
S_{1}
is a bisection through
g_{1}
.

Lemma 5.26
.

Each
(TG|_{H})_{(-i)}\rightrightarrows TM
is a VB-subgroupoid.

Proof.

First of all, note that both
Ts,Tt:(TG|_{H})_{(-i)}\rightrightarrows TM
are submersions because
TH\subseteq(TG|_{H})_{(-i)}
and
H
is a wide subgroupoid. Similarly,
TM\subseteq(TG|_{H})_{(-i)}
. We now show that
(TG|_{H})_{(-i)}
is closed under composition and inversion.

Let
X+\sigma_{0}^{L}+\tau_{0}^{R}\in(T_{h_{0}}G)_{(-i)}
and
Y+\sigma_{1}^{L}+\tau_{1}^{R}\in(T_{h_{1}}G)_{(-i)}
be composable. We have

\displaystyle T_{h_{0}}\mathcal{A}^{R}_{S_{1}}(X+\sigma_{0}^{L}+\tau_{0}^{R})

\displaystyle=T_{h_{0}}\mathcal{A}^{R}_{S_{1}}X+(\mathrm{Ad}_{S_{1}}\sigma_{0})^{L}+\tau_{0}^{R}\in(T_{h_{0}\circ h_{1}}G)_{(-i)},

\displaystyle T_{h_{1}}\mathcal{A}^{L}_{S_{0}}(Y+\sigma_{1}^{L}+\tau_{1}^{R})

\displaystyle=T_{h_{1}}\mathcal{A}^{L}_{S_{0}}Y+\sigma_{1}^{L}+(\mathrm{Ad}_{S_{0}}\tau_{1})^{R}\in(T_{h_{0}\circ h_{1}}G)_{(-i)}.

Moreover, since
H
is assumed to be wide and
S_{0}
and
S_{1}
are bisections in
H
,

T_{h_{1}}(\mathcal{A}^{L}_{S_{0}}\mathcal{A}^{R}_{S_{1}}t)(Y+\sigma_{1}^{L}+\tau_{1}^{R})\in T_{h_{0}\circ h_{1}}H\subseteq(T_{h_{0}\circ h_{1}}G)_{(-i)},

where we have used that
\sigma_{1}^{L}\sim_{t}0
and
\tau_{1}^{R}\sim_{t}a(\tau_{1})
. Since the composition
(X+\sigma_{0}^{L}+\tau_{0}^{R})\circ(Y+\sigma_{1}^{L}+\tau_{1}^{R})
is the sum of these three terms
Equation 53
, it follows that
(TG|_{H})_{(-i)}
is closed under multiplication. Similarly, using that
\sigma^{L}\sim_{\mathrm{inv}}-\sigma^{R}
, it follows that
(TG|_{H})_{(-i)}
is closed under inversion hence hence is a VB-groupoid.
∎

We conclude the proof with the following lemma.

Lemma 5.27
.

The graph of multiplication is a weighted submanifold.

Proof.

Recall (cf.
[
Mei17b
, Section 9.6]
) that
\Gamma(\mathrm{mult}_{G})\subseteq G^{3}
is the flow-out of
\{(m,m,m):m\in M\}\subseteq G^{3}
by the vector fields

X_{\sigma}=(-\sigma^{R},-\sigma^{R},0)\quad\text{and}\quad Y_{\tau}=(0,\tau^{L},-\tau^{R}),

with
\sigma,\tau\in\Gamma(A)
. Therefore, analogously to the work above, we can define a singular Lie filtration of
\Gamma(\mathrm{mult}_{G})
with respect to which
\Gamma(\mathrm{mult}_{H})
is clean. This defines a weighting of
\Gamma(\mathrm{mult}_{G})
along
\Gamma(\mathrm{mult}_{H})
. One has, by construction, that

(T\Gamma(\mathrm{mult}_{G})|_{\mathrm{mult}_{H}})_{(-i)}=T\Gamma(\mathrm{mult}_{G})\cap(TG^{3}|_{H^{3}})_{(-i)},

since
G^{3}
is weighted along
H^{3}
using the product Lie filtration. Furthermore,
2.55
implies that the inclusion
\Gamma(\mathrm{mult}_{G})\hookrightarrow G^{3}
is a weighted morphism.
Corollary 2.18
implies that
\Gamma(\mathrm{mult}_{G})
is a weighted submanifold.
∎

This concludes the proof of
Theorem 5.23
. As an immediate application, we have the following classification theorem, which says that
any
multiplicative weighting along an
s
-connected wide subgroupoid arises in this way. In particular, a Lie groupoid with a multiplicative weighting along its objects is a filtered Lie groupoid in the sense of van Erp and Yuncken (cf.
[
VEY17
, Definition 17]
), and conversely.

Theorem 5.28
.

The differentiation procedure of
Theorem 5.20
and integration procedure of
Theorem 5.23
define a 1-1 correspondence between multiplicative weightings along
s
-connected, wide Lie subgroupoids and infinitesimally multiplicative weightings along wide Lie subalgebroids.

Proof.

Let
G\rightrightarrows M
be a Lie groupoid and
H\rightrightarrows M
be an
s
-connected, wide Lie subgroupoid. An infinitesimally multiplicative weighting of
\mathrm{Lie}(G)
along
\mathrm{Lie}(H)
is a Lie filtration

\mathrm{Lie}(G)=F_{-r}\supseteq\cdots\supseteq F_{-1}\supseteq\mathrm{Lie}(H)

which satisfies the assumptions of
Theorem 5.23
. Thus, it defines a multiplicative weighting of
G
along
H
and
Equation 51
says that the differentiated weighting of
\mathrm{Lie}(G)
along
\mathrm{Lie}(H)
is the one we started with.

Now, suppose that
G
is multiplicatively weighted along
H
. By
Theorem 5.20
, the induced weighting of
\mathrm{Lie}(G)
along
\mathrm{Lie}(H)
is given by

\mathrm{Lie}(G)=F_{-r}\supseteq\cdots\supseteq F_{-1}\supseteq\mathrm{Lie}(H),

where

F_{-i}=(TG|_{M})_{(-i)}/TM.

Let
\widetilde{G}
denote
G
but with the weighting along
H
defined by
Theorem 5.23
. By
2.55
, the identity map

\widetilde{G}\to G

is a weighted morphism. We claim that it is a weighted diffeomorphism. By the normal form for weighted submersions (
Theorem 2.22
), it is enough to show that
T_{h}\widetilde{G}_{(-i)}=T_{h}G_{(-i)}
for all
h\in H
. But this clearly holds for all
h\in M
and since left multiplication by any bisection in
H
is a weighted diffeomorphism
2
2

2

This can be seen as a consequence of
Lemma 4.21
. Indeed, since
S\subseteq H
it is automatically a weighted submanifold of
G
. Therefore,
\delta_{\mathcal{W}}(S,S)=S\times\mathbb{R}
is a bisection of
\delta_{\mathcal{W}}(G,H)
and one has that
\delta_{\mathcal{W}}(\mathcal{A}^{L}_{S})=\mathcal{A}^{L}_{\delta_{\mathcal{W}}(S,S)}
it therefore holds for all
h\in H
.
∎

Examples 5.29
.

(a)

Suppose that
G=\mathrm{Pair}(M)
is the pair groupoid and
H=M
is the units. An infinitesimally multiplicative weighting of
\mathrm{Lie}(\mathrm{Pair}(M))=TM
is a filtered structure on
M
,

TM=F_{-r}\supseteq F_{-r+1}\supseteq\cdots\supseteq F_{-1}\supseteq 0.

The singular Lie filtration of
\mathrm{Pair}(M)
defined by (
52
) is regular, and is given by

T\mathrm{Pair}(M)_{-i}=F_{-i}\times F_{-i}.

(b)

Let
G
be a Lie be a Lie group,
H\subseteq G
a connected, closed subgroup, and let

\mathfrak{g}=\mathfrak{g}_{-r}\supseteq\mathfrak{g}_{-r+1}\supseteq\cdots\mathfrak{g}_{-1}\supseteq\mathfrak{h}=\mathrm{Lie}(H),

be a Lie filtration. The weighting defined by
Theorem 5.23
is the same as the weighting defined by the (right-invariant) Lie filtration

TG=G\times\mathfrak{g}_{-r}\supseteq G\times\mathfrak{g}_{-r+1}\supseteq\cdots G\times\mathfrak{g}_{-1}\supseteq G\times\mathfrak{h},

given by left-translation. Indeed, this follows by a similar argument as in
Theorem 5.28
.
