†
margin:

6

Weightings and Higher Tangent Bundles

One of the main goals in
[
LM23
]
is to give a “coordinate-free" definition of a weighting. They accomplish this by showing that a weighting of
M
along
N
is completely described by a graded subbundle (cf.
Section 6.1
) of the
r
-th order tangent bundle
T_{r}M
. This perspective can be used to give an alternative characterization of multiplicative and infinitesimally multiplicative weightings. In this chapter we will explain this perspective and use it to extend
Theorem 5.23
to arbitrary
s
-connected Lie subgroupoids (
Theorem 6.23
).

23
Higher Tangent Bundles

23.1
Definition

We begin be reviewing the definition of
T_{r}M
, following the algebraic perspective presented in
[
KMS13
, Chapter VIII]
. Consider the truncated polynomial algebra
\mathbb{A}_{r}:=\mathbb{R}[\epsilon]/\langle\epsilon^{r+1}\rangle
.

Definition 6.1
.

The
r
-th order tangent bundle
of
M
is the character spectrum

T_{r}M:=\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathrm{alg}}(C^{\infty}(M),\mathbb{A}_{r}).

Example 6.2
.

Any
\varphi\in T_{r}M=\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathrm{alg}}(C^{\infty}(M),\mathbb{A}_{r})
can be written as

\varphi=\sum_{i=0}^{r}u_{i}\epsilon^{i}

for linear maps
u_{i}:C^{\infty}(M)\to\mathbb{R}
. We see that
u_{0}
is simply an algebra morphism and therefore is given by evaluation at a point
p\in M
. Hence
T_{0}M=M
. Similarly,
u_{1}
is a derivation with respect to
u_{0}
, and is therefore a tangent vector based at
p\in M
. Thus,
T_{1}M=TM
.

The assignment
M\mapsto T_{r}M
is functorial: for a smooth map
F:M\to M^{\prime}
its
r
-th order tangent lift is defined by

T_{r}F(u)=\sum(u_{i}\circ F^{*})\epsilon^{i}.

23.2
Lifts of functions and vector fields

Any function
f\in C^{\infty}(M)
admits
r
tangent lifts
f^{(i)}\in C^{\infty}(T_{r}M)
, for
i=0,1,\dots,r
, defined by

f^{(i)}:\sum_{i=0}^{r}u_{i}\epsilon^{i}\mapsto u_{i}(f).

These lifts satisfy the product rule

(fg)^{(i)}=\sum_{j=0}^{i}f^{(j)}g^{(i-j)}.

(54)

Furthermore, if
F:M\to M^{\prime}
and
g\in C^{\infty}(M^{\prime})
then

(T_{r}F)^{*}g^{(i)}=(F^{*}g)^{(i)}.

If
x_{a}
,
a=1,\dots,m
, are coordinates for
M
defined on
U
, then the lifts
x^{(i)}_{a}
,
a=1,\dots,m
and
i=0,\dots,r
, define coordinates on
T_{r}U\subseteq T_{r}M
.

Example 6.3
.

Given
f\in C^{\infty}(M)
, the zeroth lift
f^{(0)}\in C^{\infty}(TM)
is just the pullback of
f
to
TM
, while
f^{(1)}\in C^{\infty}(TM)
is
\mathrm{d}f
thought of as a function on
TM
.

Morimoto observed in
[
Mor70
]
that any vector field
X\in\mathfrak{X}(M)
also admits lifts
X^{(-i)}\in\mathfrak{X}(T_{r}M)
for
i=0,1,\dots,r
. These lifts are uniquely determined by their action on lifts of functions
f\in C^{\infty}(M)
, which is given by

X^{(-i)}f^{(j)}=(Xf)^{(j-i)}.

These lifts satisfy the product rule and are compatible with Lie brackets:

(fX)^{(-i)}=\sum_{k=i}^{r}f^{(k-i)}X^{(-k)}\quad\text{and}\quad[X^{(-i)},Y^{(-j)}]=[X,Y]^{(-i-j)},

(55)

where
[X,Y]^{(-i-j)}=0
if
i+j>r
.

23.3
Iterating the higher tangent functor

Given non-negative integers
r_{1},\dots,r_{k}
one can, more generally, define

T_{r_{1},\dots,r_{k}}(M)=\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathrm{alg}}(C^{\infty}(M),\mathbb{A}_{r_{1}}\otimes\cdots\otimes\mathbb{A}_{r_{k}}).

This can be identified with the iterated tangent bundles
T_{r_{1}}T_{r_{2}}\cdots T_{r_{k}}(M)
. In particular, for any permutation
\sigma\in S_{r}
the corresponding algebra isomorphism
\mathbb{A}_{r_{1}}\otimes\cdots\mathbb{A}_{r_{k}}\to\mathbb{A}_{r_{\sigma(1)}}\otimes\cdots\mathbb{A}_{r_{\sigma(k)}}
induces an isomorphism

\delta_{\sigma}:T_{r_{1}}T_{r_{2}}\cdots T_{r_{k}}(M)\overset{\cong}{\longrightarrow}T_{r_{\sigma(1)}}T_{r_{\sigma(2)}}\cdots T_{r_{\sigma(k)}}(M).

(56)

In the case
k=2
, the isomorphism
T_{r}T_{s}M\cong T_{s,r}M
is given as follows. Let
\varphi\in T_{r}T_{s}M=\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathrm{alg}}(C^{\infty}(T_{s}M),\mathbb{A}_{r})
and define
\tilde{\varphi}\in T_{r,s}M
by

\tilde{\varphi}(f)=\sum_{i=0}^{s}\varphi(f^{(i)})\tau^{i}.

To see that the map

\displaystyle T_{r}T_{s}M\to T_{s,r}M,\quad\varphi\mapsto\tilde{\varphi}

is an isomorphism we write down its inverse. Let
\Psi\in T_{r,s}M
, and write
\Psi=\sum_{i=0}^{s}\psi_{i}\tau^{i}
. Define
\widehat{\Psi}\in T_{r}T_{s}(M)
by

(\widehat{\Psi})(f^{(i)})=\psi_{i}(f)\in\mathbb{A}_{r},\quad f\in C^{\infty}(M);

since
T_{s}M
is a graded bundle, after extending as needed to make
\widehat{\Psi}
an algebra morphism, this is sufficient to define
\widehat{\Psi}
. It is straightforward to check that these maps are inverse to one another.

Remark 6.4
.

With respect to the identification
T(T_{r}M)=T_{r}(TM)
we have

T_{r}X=X^{(0)}\quad\text{and}\quad T_{r}\phi_{X}^{t}=\phi_{X^{(0)}}^{t},

where
\phi_{X}^{t}
represents the time
t
flow of
X
; see
Lemma 9.10
.

23.4
Higher tangent bundles of vector bundles

If
V\to M
is a vector bundle with scalar multiplication given by
\kappa_{t}
, then
T_{r}V
is a vector bundle over
T_{r}M
with scalar multiplication given by
T_{r}\kappa_{t}
. In this section we will generalize the lifting procedure for vector fields to sections of an arbitrary vector bundle.

Let
E\in\mathfrak{X}(V)
be the Euler vector field on
V
, and let
\mathfrak{X}_{[n]}(V)
denote the vector fields
X\in\mathfrak{X}(V)
with the property that
[E,X]=nX
. Recall that any section
\sigma\in\Gamma(V)
defines a vector field
X_{\sigma}\in\mathfrak{X}_{[-1]}(V)
by the formula

X_{\sigma}(v)=\sigma(p)\quad v\in V_{p}

where we are identifying
V_{p}
with the vertical subspace of
T_{v}V
. The corresponding map

\Gamma(V)\to\mathfrak{X}_{[-1]}(V),\quad\sigma\mapsto X_{\sigma}

is an isomorphism of
C^{\infty}(M)
-modules. We will use this observation to define the tangent lifts of sections of
V
.

Lemma 6.5
.

If
E\in\mathfrak{X}(V)
is the Euler vector field for
V
, then
E^{(0)}\in\mathfrak{X}(T_{r}V)
is the Euler vector field for
T_{r}V
.

Proof.

Recall that the Euler vector field on
V
is the vector field whose flow is given by
\phi^{t}_{E}=\kappa_{e^{t}}
. Using
Remark 6.4
, we find that

\Phi^{t}_{E^{(0)}}=T_{r}\phi^{t}_{E}=T_{r}\kappa_{e^{t}}.

Since scalar multiplication on
T_{r}V
is given by
T_{r}\kappa
, the result follows.
∎

Proposition 6.6
.

Any section
\sigma\in\Gamma(V)
admits tangent lifts
\sigma^{(-i)}\in\Gamma(T_{r}V)
for
i=0,1,\dots,r
. These lifts are uniquely characterized by the conditions that

(a)

\sigma^{(0)}=T_{r}\sigma:T_{r}M\to T_{r}V
,

(b)

(f\sigma)^{(0)}=\sum_{k=0}^{r}f^{(k)}\sigma^{(-k)}
for all
f\in C^{\infty}(M)
, and

(c)

if
V
is a vector space then
T_{r}V=\oplus_{i=0}^{r}V
, and we demand that
\sigma^{(-i)}
be the copy if
\sigma
in the
i
-th term of this direct sum.

Proof.

Given
\sigma\in\Gamma(V)
, let
X_{\sigma}\in\mathfrak{X}_{[-1]}(V)
be the vector field on
V
it corresponds to under the identification
\Gamma(V)\cong\mathfrak{X}_{[-1]}(V)
. Using,
Remark 6.4
, and
Equation 55
we find that

[E^{(0)},X_{\sigma}^{(-i)}]=[E,X_{\sigma}]^{(-i)}=-X_{\sigma}^{(-i)}

hence, by
Lemma 6.5
, it follows that

X_{\sigma}^{(-i)}\in\mathfrak{X}_{[-1]}(T_{r}V)

for
i=0,1,\dots,r
. Using the isomorphism
\Gamma(T_{r}V)\cong\mathfrak{X}_{[-1]}(T_{r}V)
we thus define
\sigma^{(-i)}
by

X_{\sigma^{(-i)}}=X_{\sigma}^{(-i)}.

It follows that these lifts satisfy conditions (a), (b), and (c) because the lifts of vector fields do. Furthermore, by considering local vector bundle coordinates we see that these conditions uniquely characterize the lifts.
∎

Remark 6.7
.

We can also proceed as follows. First of all, note that applying the
r
-th order tangent functor to the non-degenerate bilinear pairing

V\times_{M}V^{*}\to\mathbb{R}

gives a map
T_{r}V\times_{T_{r}M}T_{r}V^{*}\to T_{r}\mathbb{R}
, and composing this with projection onto the last factor or
T_{r}\mathbb{R}=\oplus_{i=0}^{r}\mathbb{R}
gives a non-degenerate bilinear pairing between
T_{r}V
and
T_{r}V^{*}
. In particular, this shows that
T_{r}V^{*}=(T_{r}V)^{*}
.

Now, given
\sigma\in\Gamma(V)
, let
f_{\sigma}\in C^{\infty}_{[1]}(V^{*})
be the corresponding linear function. For each
i=0,1,\dotsm,r
, the lift
f_{\sigma}^{(i)}\in C^{\infty}(T_{r}V^{*})
is linear. Using the previous observation, we let
\sigma^{(-i)}\in\Gamma(T_{r}V)
be the section corresponding to the linear function
f_{\sigma}^{(r-i)}
. Conditions (a), (b), and (c) in
6.6
are all satisfied, hence this gives the same result as before.

23.5
Higher tangent lifts of Poisson and Lie algebroid structures

As an application of the previous section, we can describe how to define tangent lifts of Poisson and algebroid structures. There is (see
[
KW23
, Section 4.1]
) a vector bundle morphism

\epsilon_{M}^{r,q}:T_{r}(\Lambda^{q}TM)\to\Lambda^{q}T(T_{r}M)

and so any multi-vector field
X\in\mathfrak{X}^{q}(M)=\Gamma(\Lambda^{q}TM)
admits lifts
X^{(-i)}\in\mathfrak{X}^{q}(T_{r}M)
,
i=0,1,\dots,r
, defined as the composition

T_{r}M\overset{X^{(-i)}}{\longrightarrow}T_{r}(\Lambda^{q}TM)\overset{\epsilon_{M}^{r,q}}{\longrightarrow}\Lambda^{q}T(T_{r}M).

Proposition 6.8

(
[
KW23
, Theorems 4.2 and 5.1]
)
.

(a)

If
X\in\mathfrak{X}^{2}(M)
is a Poisson bivector field, then
X^{(0)}\in\mathfrak{X}^{2}(T_{r}M)
is a Poisson bivector field for
T_{r}M
. Furthermore, the corresponding Poisson bracket satisfies

\{f^{(r-i)},g^{(r-j)}\}=\{f,g\}^{(r-i-j)}\quad\forall f,g\in C^{\infty}(M).

(b)

If
A\Rightarrow M
is a Lie algebroid with anchor map
a:A\to TM
, then there is a unique bracket on
\Gamma(T_{r}A)
such that

[f^{(i)},g^{(j)}]=[f,g]^{(i+j)}

making
T_{r}A\Rightarrow T_{r}M
a Lie algebroid with anchor map given by the composition

T_{r}A\overset{T_{r}a}{\longrightarrow}T_{r}(TM)\overset{\cong}{\longrightarrow}T(T_{r}M).

Moreover, the corresponding dual Poisson structure on
T_{r}A^{*}
is the order
0
lift of the dual Poisson structure on
A^{*}
as specified in the previous point.

Example 6.9
.

Given any Lie groupoid
G\rightrightarrows M
, the
r
-order tangent bundle has the structure a Lie groupoid
T_{r}G\rightrightarrows T_{r}M
by applying the
r
-th order tangent functor to all the structure maps. If
A\Rightarrow M
is the Lie algebroid of
G\rightrightarrows M
, then
T_{r}A\Rightarrow T_{r}M
is the Lie algebroid of
T_{r}G\rightrightarrows T_{r}M
.

24
Weightings as Graded Subbundles of
T_{r}M

24.1
The graded subbundle defined by a weighting

We now explain how an order
r
weighting of
M
naturally gives rise to a graded subbundle
Q\subseteq T_{r}M
and how multiplicative and infinitesimally multiplicative weightings can be described in terms of this graded subbundle. Let
M
be weighted to order
r
along
N
and consider the set

Q:=\{q\in T_{r}M:\forall f\in C^{\infty}(M)_{(i)},\ j<i\leq r\implies f^{(j)}(q)=0\}.

(57)

We summarize the results of
[
LM23
, Sections 7 and 8]
in the following theorem.

Theorem 6.10

(
[
LM23
]
)
.

With
Q
as in (
57
), we have the following

(a)

Q
is a graded subbundle of
T_{r}M
with base
N
.

(b)

TQ|_{N}=TN\oplus(TM|_{N})_{(-1)}\oplus(TM|_{N})_{(-2)}\oplus\cdots\oplus(TM|_{N})_{(-r)}

as a graded subbundle of
T(T_{r}M)|_{N}=TM|_{N}^{\oplus(r+1)}
.

(c)

The weighting of
M
along
N
can be recovered from
Q
as

C^{\infty}(M)_{(i)}=\{f\in C^{\infty}(M):j<i\implies f^{(j)}|_{Q}=0\}.

(58)

(d)

X\in\mathfrak{X}(M)_{(-i)}
if and only if
X^{(-i)}
is tangent to
Q
. Moreover, vector fields of the form
X^{(-i)}
with
0\leq i\leq r
and
X\in\mathfrak{X}(M)_{(-i)}
span the tangent bundle of
Q
everywhere.

(e)

A smooth map
F:M\to M^{\prime}
is a weighted morphism if and only if
T_{r}F(Q_{M})\subseteq T_{r}F(Q_{M^{\prime}})
.

If
x_{a}\in C^{\infty}(U)
is a weighted coordinate system then the set
Q\cap T_{r}U
is given by

\displaystyle\begin{split}x^{(0)}_{a}=0&\quad\text{for }w_{a}>0\\
x^{(1)}_{a}=0&\quad\text{for }w_{a}>1\\
&\vdots\\
x^{(r-1)}_{a}=0&\quad\text{for }w_{a}>r-1.\end{split}

(59)

Conversely, if
Q\subseteq T_{r}M
is a graded subbundle over
N\subseteq M
with the property any
p\in N
is contained in an open neighbourhood
U\subseteq M
with coordinates
x_{a}\in C^{\infty}(U)
such that
Q\cap T_{r}U
is defined by (
59
), then
Q
defines a weighting of
M
along
N
and the
x_{a}
form a weighted coordinate system on
U
.

Proposition 6.11
.

Let
(M,N)
be a weighted pair. A submanifold
R\subseteq M
is a weighted submanifold if and only if
T_{r}R
intersects
Q
cleanly in
T_{r}M
. In this case, the induced weighting of
R
along
R\cap N
is given by
Q\cap T_{r}R
.

Proof.

Suppose that
R
is a weighted submanifold. If
x_{a},y_{b}\in C^{\infty}(U)
are weighted coordinates such that
R
is locally cut out by the
y_{b}
coordinates, then
T_{r}(R\cap U)
is cut out by the lifts
y_{b}^{(i)}
,
i=0,\dots,r
. Therefore, by (
59
), we see that the system of lifts
x_{a}^{(i)}
,
y_{b}^{(i)}
,
i=0,\dots,r
form submanifold coordinates for
T_{r}(R\cap U)
and
Q\cap T_{r}U
which implies that
T_{r}R
intersects
Q
cleanly.

Conversely, if
Q
intersects
T_{r}R
cleanly then the intersection
Q\cap T_{r}R\subseteq T_{r}R
defines an order
r
weighting of
R
along
R\cap N
such that the inclusion
R\hookrightarrow M
is a weighted morphism. By
Theorem 6.10
(b), we have that

(TQ|_{R\cap N}\cap T(T_{r}R)|_{R\cap N})_{(-i)}=(TM|_{R\cap N})_{(-i)}\cap TR|_{R\cap N}

Therefore, since
Q
and
T_{r}R
intersect cleanly it follows that

\displaystyle(TR|_{R\cap N})_{(-i)}=(T(Q\cap T_{r}R)|_{R\cap N})_{(-i)}=(TM|_{R\cap N})_{(-i)}\cap TR|_{R\cap N}

hence
R
is a weighted submanifold of
M
by
Corollary 2.18
.
∎

24.2
Multiplicative and infinitesimally multiplicative weightings in terms of
Q

Recall that if
G\rightrightarrows M
is a Lie groupoid, then
T_{r}G\rightrightarrows T_{r}M
is a Lie groupoid as well. We can use this observation to give another characterization of multiplicative weightings in terms of the graded subbundle
Q\subseteq T_{r}G
.

Theorem 6.12

(cf.
[
LM23
, Section 8.5]
)
.

Let
G\rightrightarrows M
be weighted along
H\subseteq G
. The weighting is multiplicative if and only if the graded subbundle
Q_{G}\subseteq T_{r}G
is a Lie subgroupoid
Q_{G}\rightrightarrows Q_{M}
of
T_{r}G\rightrightarrows T_{r}M
.

Proof.

Suppose that
G
is multiplicatively weighted along
H
, so that
M\subseteq G
is a weighted submanifold,
s,t:G\to M
are weighted submersions, and
\mathrm{mult}_{G}:G^{(2)}\to G
, and
\mathrm{inv}:G\to G
are weighted morphisms. By
6.11
, the intersection
T_{r}M\cap Q_{G}=Q_{M}
is clean and is the graded subbundle associated to the induced weighting of
M
. Using
Theorem 6.10
(e), we see that
Q_{G}
is closed under multiplication and inversion. Furthermore, by
Theorem 6.10
(b) and
2.9
, it follows that
T_{r}s|_{Q_{G}}:Q_{G}\to Q_{M}
and
T_{r}t|_{Q_{G}}:Q_{G}\to Q_{M}
are submersions near
H\subseteq Q_{G}
. By homogeneity they are therefore submersions on all of
Q_{G}
.

Conversely, suppose that
Q_{G}\subseteq T_{r}G
is a subgroupoid. It follows by reversing the above argument at inversion and multiplication are weighted morphisms, and that source and target are weighted submersions. Since
T_{r}M
are the objects of
T_{r}G
, it follows that
Q_{G}
intersects
T_{r}M
cleanly, hence
M
is a weighted submanifold of
G
by
6.11
.
∎

Example 6.13
.

Let
(M,N)
be a weighted pair. If the graded bundle corresponding to the weighting of
M
along
N
is
Q_{M}\subseteq T_{r}M
, then the graded bundle corresponding to the product weighting of
\mathrm{Pair}(M)
along
\mathrm{Pair}(N)
is
\mathrm{Pair}(Q_{N})
. In particular, gives another proof that the product weighting is multiplicative.

Carry on, let
G\rightrightarrows M
be a Lie groupoid with the property that the groupoid anchor
a_{G}=(s,t):G\to\mathrm{Pair}(M)
is transverse to
\mathrm{Pair}(N)
. In this case,
T_{r}a_{G}:T_{r}G\to\mathrm{Pair}(T_{r}M)
is transverse to
\mathrm{Pair}(Q_{M})
and

(T_{r}a_{G})^{-1}(\mathrm{Pair}(Q_{M}))\subseteq T_{r}G

is a graded subbundle which defines a multiplicative weighting of
G
along
G|_{N}
.

Corollary 6.14
.

Let
V\to M
be a vector bundle, thought of as a manifold. A weighting of
V
is linear (with weights concentrated in non-positive degree) if and only if the graded subbundle
Q_{V}\subseteq T_{r}V
is a vector subbundle. In this case, we have that
\sigma\in\Gamma(V)_{(i)}
if and only if
\sigma^{(i)}
restricts to a section of
Q_{V}
over
Q_{V}\cap T_{r}M
.

Proof.

Thinking of
V
as a Lie groupoid over
M
, the weighting of
V
is linear (with weights concentrated in non-positive degree) if and only if it is multiplicative. Hence, it is linear if and only if
Q_{V}\subseteq T_{r}V
is a subgroupoid, which in this case is a vector subbundle. The statement about lifts is just a reformulation of
Theorem 6.10
(d).
∎

Likewise, we have a similar characterization for Lie algebroid weightings which are concentrated in non-positive degree.

Theorem 6.15

(cf.
[
LM23
, Section 8.5]
)
.

Let
A\Rightarrow M
be a Lie algebroid with Lie subalgebroid
B\Rightarrow N
. A linear weighting of
A
along
B
concentrated in non-positive degree is infinitesimally multiplicative if and only if the corresponding graded subbundle
Q_{A}\subseteq T_{r}A
is a Lie subalgebroid
Q_{A}\Rightarrow Q_{M}
of
T_{r}A\Rightarrow T_{r}M
.

Proof.

Suppose that
A
is infinitesimally weighted along
B
, with corresponding graded bundle
Q_{A}\subseteq T_{r}A
, and let
Q_{M}=T_{r}M\cap Q
be the graded bundle associated to the induced weighting of
M
along
N
. To show that
Q_{A}
is a Lie subalgebroid of
T_{r}A
, we must show

(a)

a_{T_{r}A}(Q_{A})\subseteq TQ_{M}
, and

(b)

\Gamma(T_{r}A,Q_{A})\subseteq\Gamma(T_{r}A)
is a Lie subalgebra.

For (a), recall that the anchor map for
T_{r}A
is
T_{r}a
. Using
Corollary 6.14
and
Theorem 6.10
, it is enough to show that
T_{r}a(\sigma^{(i)})
is tangent to
Q_{M}
for any
\sigma\in\Gamma(A)_{(i)}
. But, as the weighting is infinitesimally multiplicative, if
\sigma\in\Gamma(A)_{(i)}
one has that
a(\sigma)\in\mathfrak{X}(M)_{(i)}
and since

T_{r}a(\sigma^{(i)})=(a(\sigma))^{(i)}

it follows from
Theorem 6.10
(d) that
T_{r}a(\sigma^{(i)})
is tangent to
Q_{M}
.

For (b), it suffices to show that if
f\sigma^{(i)}\in\Gamma(T_{r}A,Q_{A})
and
g\tau^{(j)}\in\Gamma(T_{r}A,Q_{A})
, then
[f\sigma^{(i)},g\tau^{(j)}]\in\Gamma(T_{r}A,Q_{A})
, where
\sigma,\tau\in\Gamma(V)
and
f,g\in C^{\infty}(T_{r}M)
. Using the Jacobi identity

\displaystyle[f\sigma^{(i)},g\tau^{(j)}]=fg[\sigma,\tau]^{(i+j)}+f\mathcal{L}_{T_{r}a(\sigma^{(i)})}g\cdot\tau^{(j)}-g\mathcal{L}_{T_{r}a(\tau^{(j)})}f\cdot\sigma^{(i)},

(60)

we see that there are three cases to consider:

•

If
\sigma^{(i)},\tau^{(j)}\in\Gamma(T_{r}A,Q_{A})
, then by
Corollary 6.14
it follows that
\sigma\in\Gamma(A)_{(i)}
and
\tau\in\Gamma(A)_{(J)}
. Since the weighting is infinitesimally multiplicative, we have
[\sigma,\tau]\in\Gamma(A)_{(i+j)}
, hence

[\sigma^{(i)},\tau^{(j)}]=[\sigma,\tau]^{(i+j)}\in\Gamma(T_{r}A,Q_{A}).

Therefore,
[f\sigma^{(i)},g\tau^{(j)}]\in\Gamma(T_{r}A,Q_{A})
in this case.

•

If
\sigma^{(i)},\tau^{(j)}\notin\Gamma(T_{r}A,Q_{A})
, then both
f
and
g
must vanish on
Q_{M}
. By the Jacobi identity,
[f\sigma^{(i)},g\tau^{(j)}]
also vanishes along
Q_{M}
, hence belongs to
\Gamma(T_{r}A,Q_{A})
in this case.

•

Finally, suppose that
\sigma^{(i)}\notin\Gamma(T_{r}A,Q_{A})
and
\tau^{(j)}\in\Gamma(T_{r}A,Q_{A})
. In this case, we must have that
f
vanishes along
Q_{M}
. Since
T_{r}a(\tau^{(j)})=(a(\tau))^{(j)}
is tangent to
Q_{M}
, it follows that
\mathcal{L}_{T_{r}a(\tau^{(j)})}f
vanishes on
Q_{M}
and therefore
[f\sigma^{(i)},g\tau^{(j)}]
vanishes along
Q_{M}
as well.

In each of these cases we see that
[f\sigma^{(i)},g\tau^{(j)}]\in\Gamma(T_{r}A,Q_{A})
, which shows that
\Gamma(T_{r}A,Q_{A})
is a Lie subalgebra of
\Gamma(T_{r}A)
.

Conversely, suppose that
Q_{A}\subseteq T_{r}A
is a Lie subalgebroid. In order to show that conditions (a) and (b) in
Definition 5.11
are satisfied, it is sufficient, since the weighting is concentrated in non-positive degree, to consider indices
-i,-j
with
0\leq i,j\leq r
. If
\sigma\in\Gamma(A)_{(-i)}
, and
\tau\in\Gamma(A)_{(-j)}
, then
\sigma^{(-i)}\in\Gamma(T_{r}A,Q_{A})
and
\tau^{(-j)}\in\Gamma(T_{r}A,Q_{A})
, whence
[\sigma^{(-i)},\tau^{(-j)}]=[\sigma,\tau]^{(-i-j)}\in\Gamma(T_{r}A,Q_{A})
as
Q_{A}
is a Lie subalgebroid. This shows that
[\sigma,\tau]\in\Gamma(A)_{(-i-j)}
. Similarly, let
\sigma\in\Gamma(A)_{(-i)}
. Then
\sigma^{(-i)}\in\Gamma(T_{r}A,Q_{A})
and
T_{r}a(\sigma^{(-i)})=(a(\sigma))^{(-i)}
is tangent to
Q_{N}
, which shows that
a(\sigma)\in\mathfrak{X}(M)_{(-i)}
, as needed.
∎

Proposition 6.16
.

Suppose that
G\rightrightarrows M
is multiplicatively weighted along
H
, with graded bundle
Q_{G}\subseteq T_{r}G
. Then the graded bundle for the weighting of
A=\mathrm{Lie}(G)
is given by
Q_{A}=\mathrm{Lie}(Q_{G})
.

Proof.

Recall that the weighting of
A
is given by
\sigma\in\Gamma(A)_{(i)}
if and only if
\sigma^{L}\in\mathfrak{X}(G)_{(i)}
. The result follows since
(\sigma^{(-i)})^{L}=(\sigma^{L})^{(-i)}
for
0\leq i\leq r
, which follows because they are both left invariant and agree along
T_{r}M
.
∎

25
Integration of Lie Algebroid Weightings Revisited

In this section we will return to the integration problem for infinitesimally multiplicative weightings. More specifically, suppose that
H\rightrightarrows N
is an
s
-connected Lie subgroupoid of
G\rightrightarrows M
, and suppose that
A=\mathrm{Lie}(G)
has an infinitesimally multiplicative weighting along
B=\mathrm{Lie}(H)
. We will show that if the graded bundle
Q_{A}\subseteq T_{r}A
corresponding to this weighting integrates to an
s
-connected Lie subgroupoid
Q_{G}\subseteq T_{r}G
then this subgroupoid defines a weighting of
G
along
H
. In order to do this, we will use a weighted Lie algebroid spray to define an exponential map which allows us to find weighted coordinates for
G
near the object space. Flowing these coordinates along the
s
-fibres using left invariant vector fields of filtration degree 0 gives weighted coordinates everywhere along
H
, proving that
Q_{G}
indeed defines a weighting.

25.1
The spray exponential for a weighted Lie algebroid

Let
A\Rightarrow M
be a weighted Lie algebroid and let
G\rightrightarrows M
be a Lie groupoid integrating
A
. We briefly review how to construct a partially defined exponential map for
A\dashrightarrow G
; for a complete discussion, see
Chapter 9
. The data to define an exponential map
A\to G
is a Lie algebroid spray:

Definition 6.17

(
[
CMS20
, Definition 3.1]
)
.

Let
A\Rightarrow M
be a Lie algebroid. A
Lie algebroid spray
on
A
is a vector field
V\in\mathfrak{X}(A)
such that

(a)

\kappa_{t}^{*}V=tV
for all
t\neq 0
, where
\kappa_{t}
denotes scalar multiplication by
t
, and

(b)

for all
\xi\in A
, one has
T\pi(V_{\xi})=a(\xi)
, where
\pi:A\to M
is the vector bundle projection and
a:A\to TM
is the anchor.

If
V\in\mathfrak{X}(A)
is a Lie algebroid spray, then there is an open neighbourhood
U_{V}\subseteq A
of the zero section for which the flow
\phi_{V}^{t}
is defined for
|t|\leq 1
. For each
u\in U_{V}
, the path
t\to u(t)=\phi_{V}^{t}(u)
is an
A
-path, and therefore integrates to a
G
-path
\tilde{u}(t)
. The
spray exponential
is the map

\exp_{V}:U_{V}\to G,\quad u\mapsto\tilde{u}(1).

Following the notation in
[
CMS20
]
we use the dotted arrow notation

\exp_{V}:A\dashrightarrow G

to denote that
\exp_{V}
is only defined on an open neighbourhood of the zero section.

Example 6.18
.

Let
G
be a Lie group and
\mathfrak{g}=\mathrm{Lie}(G)
. Then
0\in\mathfrak{X}(\mathfrak{g})
is a Lie algebroid spray for
\mathfrak{g}
and
\exp_{0}
is the standard exponential map
\exp:\mathfrak{g}\to G
.

If
A
is a weighted Lie algebroid, the the appropriate sprays to consider are ones having filtration degree zero.

Lemma 6.19
.

For any weighted Lie algebroid
A
, there exists a spray
V\in\mathfrak{X}(A)_{(0)}
.

Proof.

Note that any convex combination of sprays is again a spray. Therefore, by pulling back a partition of unity on
M
it suffices to work locally.

Let
w_{i}
and
v_{j}
be the base and vertical weights for
A
, respectively. If
\Gamma_{ij}^{k}\in C^{\infty}(M)
denote the structure functions for
A
corresponding to a local weighted frame then recall from
Lemma 5.13
that
\Gamma_{ij}^{k}\in C^{\infty}(M)_{(v_{i}+v_{j}-v_{k})}
. If
x_{a},p_{b}
are weighted vector bundle coordinates for
A
then

V=a_{ij}(x)p_{i}\frac{\partial}{\partial x_{j}}+\Gamma^{k}_{ij}(x)p_{i}p_{j}\frac{\partial}{\partial p_{k}}

defines a spray for
A
. Since
a:\Gamma(A)_{(-i)}\to\mathfrak{X}(M)_{(-i)}
, it follows that
a_{ij}\in C^{\infty}(M)_{(v_{i}+w_{j})}
, hence
V\in\mathfrak{X}(A)_{(0)}
.
∎

Let
G\rightrightarrows M
be a Lie groupoid with a multiplicative weighting along
H\rightrightarrows N
, let
A=\mathrm{Lie}(G)
and let
B=\mathrm{Lie}(H)
. Let
Q_{G}\subseteq T_{r}G
be the graded bundle corresponding to the weighting, and let
Q_{A}=\mathrm{Lie}(Q_{G})\subseteq T_{r}A
.

Lemma 6.20
.

Let
V\in\mathfrak{X}(A)_{(0)}
be a Lie algebroid spray on
A
. Then the spray exponential is a partially defined map of pairs

T_{r}\exp_{V}:(T_{r}A,Q_{A})\dashrightarrow(T_{r}G,Q_{G}).

It is a diffeomorphism of pairs in a sufficiently small open neighbourhood of
T_{r}M\subseteq T_{r}G
. In particular, if
\exp_{V}
is globally defined it is a weighted morphism.

Proof.

By
9.12
, we have that
T_{r}\exp_{V}=\exp_{V^{(0)}}
. Since
V\in\mathfrak{X}(V)_{(0)}
, it follows by
Theorem 6.10
(d) that
V^{(0)}
is tangent to
Q_{A}
, hence by
Lemma 9.9

\exp_{V^{(0)}}:(T_{r}A,Q_{A})\dashrightarrow(T_{r}G,Q_{G})

is a (partially defined) map of pairs.
∎

This immediately implies the following result.

Proposition 6.21
.

For any weighted Lie group
G
, the exponential map is a weighted morphism. It is a weighted diffeomorphism on any sufficiently small open neighbourhood of the origin in
\mathfrak{g}
.

Proof.

The zero vector field on
\mathfrak{g}
is a weighted Lie algebroid spray, and the corresponding spray exponential is the standard exponential map. Hence the result follows by
Lemma 6.20
.
∎

Remark 6.22
.

This can also be proved using weighted paths in
\mathfrak{g}
, but the proof is considerably longer.

25.2
Integration of Lie algebroid weightings revisited

Let
G\rightrightarrows M
be a Lie groupoid with Lie algebroid
A=\mathrm{Lie}(G)
. We now prove the main result of this section, which says that if the graded bundle corresponding to an infinitesimally multiplicative weighting of
A
integrates to a subgroupoid of
T_{r}G
, then this is automatically a graded subbundle which defines a multiplicative weighting of
G
.

Theorem 6.23
.

Suppose that
G\rightrightarrows M
is a
s
-connected Lie groupoid and
H\rightrightarrows N
is a
s
-connected Lie subgroupoid with Lie algebroids
A=\mathrm{Lie}(G)
and
B=\mathrm{Lie}(H)
, respectively, and suppose that
A
is infinitesimally multiplicatively weighted along
B
. Let
Q_{A}\Rightarrow Q_{M}
be the graded subbundle of
T_{r}A\Rightarrow T_{r}M
corresponding to the weighting of
A
along
B
. If
Q_{A}
integrates to an
s
-connected subgroupoid
Q_{G}\rightrightarrows Q_{M}
of
T_{r}G\rightrightarrows T_{r}M
, then
Q_{G}
is a graded subbundle of
T_{r}G\rightrightarrows T_{r}M
which defines a multiplicative weighting of
G
along
H
.

Proof.

Let
Q_{A}\subseteq T_{r}A
be the graded bundle associated to the weighting of
A
along
B
, and let
Q_{G}\subseteq T_{r}G
be the Lie subgroupoid integrating it. To show that
Q_{G}
is a GB-subgroupoid we just have to show that it is closed under scalar multiplication. To do this, let
V\in\mathfrak{X}(A)_{(0)}
be a Lie algebroid spray. Let
\kappa_{t}:T_{r}G\to T_{r}G
be scalar multiplication by
t\in\mathbb{R}
; abusing notation we let
\kappa_{t}
denote scalar multiplication on
T_{r}A
as well. Since
V^{(0)}=T_{r}V
, it follows that
V^{(0)}\sim_{\kappa_{t}}V^{(0)}
, hence the following diagram commutes

\textstyle{T_{r}A\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\kappa_{t}}
\scriptstyle{\exp_{V^{(0)}}}
\textstyle{T_{r}A\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\exp_{V^{(0)}}}
\textstyle{T_{r}G\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\kappa_{t}}
\textstyle{T_{r}G.}

Using this, the fact that both

\exp_{V^{(0)}}:(T_{r}A,Q_{A})\dashrightarrow(T_{r}G,Q_{G})\quad\text{and}\quad\kappa_{t}:(T_{r}A,Q_{A})\to(T_{r}A,Q_{A})

are maps of pairs, and the assumption that
Q_{G}
is
s
-connected implies that
Q_{G}
is a graded subbundle. Since

\mathrm{Lie}(\kappa_{0}(Q_{G}))=\kappa_{0}(\mathrm{Lie}(Q_{G}))=B

it follows that
H=\kappa_{0}(Q_{G})
, since
H
is the Lie subgroupoid of
G
integrating
B
.

It remains to show that
Q_{G}\subseteq T_{r}G
defines a weighting. To do this, we will show that near any point
p\in H
one can always find local coordinates
x_{a}
for
G
such that
Q_{G}
is locally defined by
Equation 59
. There are open neighbourhoods
U_{A}\subseteq A
and
U_{G}\subseteq G
containing the objects for which
\exp_{V}:U_{A}\to U_{G}
is a diffeomorphism. By the previous section, we have

T_{r}\exp_{V}(Q_{A}\cap T_{r}U_{A})=Q_{G}\cap T_{r}U_{G}

In particular, if
x_{a}
are weighted coordinates defined on
U\subseteq U_{A}
, then
Q_{G}\cap T_{r}U
is defined by the the equations

\displaystyle((\exp_{V})_{*}x_{a})^{(0)}=

\displaystyle(\exp_{V^{(0)}})_{*}x_{a}^{(0)}=0\quad\text{for }w_{a}>0,

\displaystyle((\exp_{V})_{*}x_{a})^{(1)}=

\displaystyle(\exp_{V^{(0)}})_{*}x_{a}^{(1)}=0\quad\text{for }w_{a}>1,

\displaystyle((\exp_{V})_{*}x_{a})^{(r-1)}=

\displaystyle(\exp_{V^{(0)}})_{*}x_{a}^{(r-1)}=0\quad\text{for }w_{a}>r-1.

This gives weighted coordinates for
G
near
M
.

We will now construct weighted coordinates away from
M
. Let
h\in H
and assume, for simplicity, that
h=\phi_{\sigma^{L}}^{t}(s(h))
for some
\sigma\in\Gamma(A)_{(0)}
. If
x_{a}
are weighted coordinates for
G
defined on
U\subseteq G
containing
s(h)
, let
\tilde{x}_{a}=(\phi_{\sigma^{L}}^{t})_{*}x_{a}
; these give coordinates on
\tilde{U}=\phi_{\sigma^{L}}^{-t}(U)
, and we claim that

Q_{G}\cap T_{r}\tilde{U}=\{\tilde{x}_{a}^{(i)}=0:w_{a}>i,\ i=0,\dots,r-1\}.

To see this, note that by
Lemma 9.10

\displaystyle\tilde{x}_{a}^{(i)}=((\phi_{\sigma^{L}}^{t})_{*}x_{a})^{(i)}=(T_{r}\phi_{\sigma^{L}}^{t})_{*}x_{a}^{(i)}=(\phi_{(\sigma^{L})^{(0)}}^{t})_{*}x_{a}^{(i)}.

Since
\sigma\in\Gamma(V)_{(0)}
, it follows that
(\sigma^{L})^{(0)}
is tangent to
Q_{G}
hence
Q_{G}
is invariant under its flow. This shows that

\displaystyle Q_{G}\cap T_{r}\tilde{U}

\displaystyle=\phi_{(\sigma^{L})^{(0)}}^{t}(Q_{G}\cap T_{r}U)

\displaystyle=\phi_{(\sigma^{L})^{(0)}}^{t}(\{x_{a}^{(i)}=0:w_{a}>i,\ i=0,\dots,r-1\})

\displaystyle=\{(\phi_{(\sigma^{L})^{(0)}}^{t})_{*}x_{a}^{(i)}=0:w_{a}>i,\ i=0,\dots,r-1\})

\displaystyle=\{\tilde{x}_{a}^{(i)}=0:w_{a}>i,\ i=0,\dots,r-1\},

as claimed. Using induction and the fact that
H
is
s
-connected, we see that any
h\in H
is contained in a neighbourhood on which weighted coordinates are defined, which completes the proof.
∎

Remark 6.24
.

The question of whether or not the subalgebroid
Q_{A}\Rightarrow Q_{M}
integrates to a subgroupoid of
T_{r}G\rightrightarrows T_{r}M
is a little subtle. At first one might think that such an integration always exists, since any subalgebroid of an integrable Lie algebroid is always integrable. However,
[
MM06
]
shows that this is
not
always the case. Taking into account the graded bundle structure, one might wonder if it is enough to assume that the base algebroid integrates to a subgroupoid. However this is also not sufficient, as explained in
[
CBO18
]
. We leave it as an open problem to give conditions on when such an integration is possible.

†
†
margin:

7

Outlook

We conclude this thesis by examining some possible directions this research can lead. The following is speculative and vague, yet our aim is to inspire readers to explore the topic more thoroughly.

26
More on Linear Weightings

26.1
Linear singular Lie filtrations

The first avenue we think is worth pursuing is an adaptation of
Section 8
to vector bundles. Let
V\to M
be a vector bundle. Recall that the
linear
vector fields on
V
is the
C^{\infty}(M)
-module

\mathfrak{X}_{[0]}(V)=\{X\in\mathfrak{X}(V):[E,X]=0\},

where
E\in\mathfrak{X}(V)
is the Euler vector field. The linear vector fields on
V
can be identified with first-order differential operators acting on
\Gamma(V)
having scalar principal symbol. We propose the following definition.

Definition 7.1
.

(a)

A
linear singular distribution
on a vector bundle
V\to M
is a sheaf of
C^{\infty}_{M}
-submodules
\mathcal{L}\subseteq\mathfrak{X}_{[0]}(V)
which is locally finitely generated in the sense that each
p\in M
has an open neighbourhood
U\subseteq M
such that
\mathcal{L}(V|_{U})
is finitely generated.

(b)

A
linear singular Lie filtration
of order
r
is a filtration of the sheaf of linear vector fields

\mathfrak{X}_{[0]}(V)=\mathcal{L}_{(-r)}\supseteq\mathcal{L}_{(-r+1)}\supseteq\cdots\supseteq\mathcal{L}_{(-1)}\supseteq 0

(61)

by linear singular distributions such that

[\mathcal{L}_{-i},\mathcal{L}_{-j}]\subseteq\mathcal{L}_{-i-j}

for all
i,j
.

The linear vector fields on
V
are the sections of a Lie algebroid
\mathrm{At}(V)\Rightarrow M
, called the Atiyah algebroid of
V
, which fits in to an exact sequence

0\overset{}{\longrightarrow}\mathrm{End}(V)\overset{}{\longrightarrow}\mathrm{At}(V)\overset{}{\longrightarrow}TM\overset{}{\longrightarrow}0.

(62)

In particular, we see that a linear singular Lie filtration on
V
determines a filtration of
\Gamma(\mathrm{End}(V))
by
C^{\infty}(M)
-submodules. The space of linear differential operators acting on sections of
V
can be identified with the quotient

\mathcal{U}(\mathrm{At}(V))/I

where
\mathcal{U}(\mathrm{At}(V))
is the universal enveloping algebra of the Lie algebroid
\mathrm{At}(V)
(cf.
[
MM10
]
) and
I\subseteq\mathcal{U}(\mathrm{At}(V))
is the ideal generated by elements of the form
\eta\otimes\phi-\eta\phi
, where
\eta,\phi\in\Gamma(\mathrm{End}(V))
. Therefore, a linear singular Lie filtration (
61
) determines an algebra filtration

\cdots\mathrm{DO}(V)_{(i)}\supseteq\mathrm{DO}(V)_{(i+1)}\supseteq\cdots

of
\mathrm{DO}(V)
, so long as the induced filtration of
\Gamma(\mathrm{End}(V))
(using the exact sequence (
62
)) is multiplicative.

Let
N\subseteq M
be a closed submanifold and suppose that

\cdots\supseteq(V|_{N})_{(i)}\supseteq(V|_{N})_{(i+1)}\supseteq

(63)

be a filtration of
V|_{N}
be subbundles
(V|_{N})_{(i)}\to N
. With the filtration of
\mathrm{DO}(V)
as above, let

\Gamma(V)_{(i)}=\{\sigma\in\Gamma(V):D\in\mathrm{DO}(V)_{(q)}\implies D\sigma\in\Gamma(V,(V|_{N})_{(i+q)}\}.

(64)

This defines a filtration of
\Gamma(V)
.

Problem 7.2
.

Find the correct compatibility conditions between the linear singular Lie filtration (
61
) and the filtration (
63
) so that the filtration (
64
) defines a linear weighting of
V
.

One possible approach to this problem would be to mimic the technique sketched in
Remark 8.12
and find conditions for which the bundle of filtered frames is clean. We intend to pursue this in a future work.

Remark 7.3
.

This problem appears to be closely related to the works of Higson and Yi
[
HY19
]
and Braverman and Haj
[
BS22
]
, where a filtration of
\mathrm{DO}(V)
is defined using a connection on
V
and a filtration of
\mathrm{End}(V)
.

26.2
Weightings for associative algebroids

Let
G
be a Lie groupoid. An
associative algebroid
(cf.
[
Šev17
, Letter 6]
) is a vector bundle
V\to G
with an associative (in the appropriate sense) fibrewise product

V_{g}\otimes V_{h}\to V_{g\circ h},

whenever
g,h\in G
are composable. There is an obvious notion of weightings for associative algebroids.

Problem 7.4
.

Explain whether or not the weighted deformation bundle of a weighted associative algebroid has a canonical associative algebroid structure.

If yes, then there is a well-defined convolution product on sections of the weighted deformation bundle.

Problem 7.5
.

Understand multipliers of the convolution algebra of sections of an associative algebroid as algebras of pseudodifferential operators.

Combining this with the the previous question, it would be interesting to try to define a rescaled spinor bundle on a manifold with boundary, i.e. a deformation bundle over the
b
-tangent groupoid, and try to understand Getzler rescaling (
[
Get83
]
) in this context.

27
Tangent (
k
-fold) Groupoid of a Multifiltered Manifold

27.1
Multifiltered manifolds

Recall that a filtered manifold is a smooth manifold
M
together with a filtration by subbundles

TM=F_{-r}\supseteq F_{-r+1}\supseteq\cdots\supseteq F_{-1}\supseteq 0

satisfying
[\Gamma(F_{-i}),\Gamma(F_{-j})]\subseteq\Gamma(F_{-i-j})
. This generalizes to a
multifiltered
manifold.

Definitions 7.6

(
[
Yun18
, Definitions 3.4.1 and 3.4.2]
)
.

(a)

Let
V\to M
be a vector bundle. A
k
-multifiltration
on
V
is a family of subbundles
F_{-\mathbf{i}}\subseteq V
indexed by
\mathbf{i}\in\mathbb{N}^{k}
such that

(i)

F_{\mathbf{0}}=0
and
F_{-\mathbf{n}}=V
for some
\mathbf{n}\in\mathbb{N}^{k}

(ii)

F_{-\mathbf{i}}\cap F_{-\mathbf{j}}=F_{-\mathbf{i}\wedge\mathbf{j}}
for all
\mathbf{i},\mathbf{j}\in\mathbb{N}^{k}
, where
\mathbf{i}\wedge\mathbf{i}
is the entry-wise minimum of the two multi-indices.

(b)

A
k
-multifiltered manifold
is a smooth manifold
M
with an
k
-multifiltration of
TM
by subbundles
F_{-\alpha}
satisfying

\displaystyle[\Gamma(F_{-\mathbf{i}}),\Gamma(F_{-\mathbf{j}})]\subseteq\Gamma(F_{-\mathbf{i}-\mathbf{j}}).

(65)

The
k
-multifiltration (
65
) will be referred to as a
Lie multifiltration
.

Examples 7.7

(cf.
[
Yun18
, Section 3.4]
)
.

(a)

Any filtered manifold is a 1-multifiltered manifold.

(b)

The full flag manifold of a complex semisimple Lie group
G
is an
k
-multifiltered manifold, where
k
is the rank of G.

27.2
Multiweightings

Let
M
be an
m
-dimensional manifold. A
k
-
multiweight
sequence for
M
is a list
\mathbf{w}_{1},\dots,\mathbf{w}_{m}\in\mathbb{Z}^{k}_{\geq 0}
. Any upper bound
r\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}
for the sequence
|\mathbf{w}_{a}|
is called its
order
. A monomial

x^{s}=x_{1}^{s_{1}}\cdots x_{m}^{s_{m}}

has multiweight
s\cdot\mathbf{w}=\sum_{a}s_{a}\mathbf{w}_{a}
. For an open set
U\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{m}
and a vector
\mathbf{i}\in\mathbb{Z}^{k}_{\geq 0}
, we define
C^{\infty}(U)_{(\mathbf{i})}
to be the ideal generated by the monomials

x^{s}\quad\text{with }s\cdot\mathbf{w}\geq\mathbf{i},

where we use the standard component-wise partial ordering on
\mathbb{Z}^{k}_{\geq 0}
.

Definition 7.8

(
[
LM23
, Section 9.2]
)
.

A
k
-
multiweighting
of a manifold
M
is a mutlifiltration of the sheaf of smooth functions by ideals
C^{\infty}_{M,(\mathbf{i})}
with the property that each point
p\in M
has an open neighbourhood
U\subseteq M
so that
C^{\infty}(U)_{(\mathbf{i})}
is the ideal defined above.

Let
e_{a}
be the standard basis vectors of
\mathbb{Z}^{k}
. A
k
-multiweighting of
M
determines
k
weightings
C^{\infty}_{M,(i\cdot e_{a})}
of
M
along submanifolds
N_{1},\dots,N_{k}
. We say that
M
is
multiweighted
along
(N_{1},N_{2},\dots,N_{k})
. Completely analogously to the work above, one obtains the
k
-fold weighted normal bundle

\nu_{\mathcal{W}}(M,N_{1},\dots,N_{k})=\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathrm{alg}}(\mathrm{gr}(C^{\infty}(M)),\mathbb{R}),

which is a
k
-fold graded bundle over the intersection
N_{1}\cap\cdots\cap N_{k}
. Similarly, one obtains the
k
-fold weighted deformation space

\delta_{\mathcal{W}}(M,N_{1},\dots,N_{k})\to\mathbb{R}^{k}.

Theorem 5.28
says, in particular, that Lie filtrations of a manifold
M
are the same things as multiplicative weightings of
\mathrm{Pair}(M)
along the units. Therefore, it is reasonable to expect that a
k
-multifiltration corresponds to a
k
-fold multiweighting of the
k
-fold pair groupoid
\mathrm{Pair}^{k}(M)=\prod_{i=1}^{2k}M
.

Problem 7.9
.

Explain the correspondence between
k
-multifiltered manifolds and multiplicative weightings of the
k
-fold pair groupoid
\mathrm{Pair}^{k}(M)
along the various diagonals.

Let us take a minute to speculate how this might look in the case
k=2
. A 2-multifiltration on a manifold
M
is given by a family of subbundles

\begin{array}[]{cccc}TM=F_{(-r_{1},-r_{2})}&F_{(-r_{1}+1,-r_{2})}&\cdots&F_{(0,-r_{2})}\\
F_{(-r_{1},-r_{2}+1)}&F_{(-r_{1}+1,-r_{2}+1)}&\cdots&F_{(0,-r_{2}+1)}\\
\vdots&\vdots&\ddots&\vdots\\
F_{(-r_{1},0)}&F_{(-r_{1}+1,0)}&\cdots&F_{(0,0)}=0.\end{array}

(66)

Within this, there are two Lie filtrations
F^{h}_{\bullet}
and
F^{v}_{\bullet}
of
M
, given by the top row and left column of (
66
). Correspondingly, there are two tangent groupoids
\mathbb{T}_{F^{v}}M
and
\mathbb{T}_{F^{h}}M
. Our conjecture is that the 2-multifiltration (
66
) will determine a 2-multiweighting of the double pair groupoid
\mathrm{Pair}^{2}(M)
along the diagonals

\Delta^{h}=\{(m_{1},m_{0},m_{1},m_{0})\in\mathrm{Pair}^{2}(M)\},\quad\Delta^{v}=\{(m_{1},m_{1},m_{0},m_{0})\in\mathrm{Pair}^{2}(M)\}

such that the double deformation space has the structure of a double groupoid

\textstyle{\delta_{\mathcal{W}}(\mathrm{Pair}^{2}(M),\Delta^{h},\Delta^{v})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\mathbb{T}_{F^{h}}M\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\mathbb{T}_{F^{v}}M\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{M\times\mathbb{R}.}

(67)

It therefore our belief that
\delta(\mathrm{Pair}^{2}(M),\Delta^{h},\Delta^{v})
is the right notion of the tangent (double) groupoid of the filtered 2-multifiltered manifold
M
.

27.3

C^{*}
-algebraic perspective

As mentioned in the introduction, an important feature of Connes’ tangent groupoid is that it defines a continuous field of
C^{*}
-algebras.

Problem 7.10
.

Given a weighted groupoid
G\rightrightarrows M
, determine when the deformation groupoid
\delta_{\mathcal{W}}(G,H)\rightrightarrows\delta_{\mathcal{W}}(M,N)
determines a continuous field of
C^{*}
-algebra

Given a Lie groupoid, one can form the convolution algebra and from that a groupoid
C^{*}
-algebra (
[
Ren06
]
). Some work towards determining the convolution algebra of a double groupoid has been done in
[
RV23
]
, but there appears to be much work to be done in this direction. Related to the discussion in the previous section, we also pose the following.

Problem 7.11
.

Determine what
C^{*}
-algebraic information is contained in a double groupoid.

†
†
margin:

8

Morita Equivalence of Weighted Groupoids

Appendix 8.A
Weighted Morita Equivalence

A Morita equivalence is a special instance of generalized morphisms, called
Hilsum-Skandalis morphisms
, which are invertible in the appropriate sense. The following approach to Morita equivalence is adapted from
[
MM03
,
Ler10
]
.

8.a.1
Definition of Morita Equivalence

Definitions 8.1
.

Let
G\rightrightarrows M
and
H\rightrightarrows N
be Lie groupoids.

(a)

A
right action
of
G\rightrightarrows M
on a manifold
Q
is given by a map
\mu:Q\to M
together with an action map

Q\times_{M}G=\{(q,g):\mu(q)=t(g)\}\to Q,\quad(q,g)\mapsto q\cdot g

such that
\mu(q\cdot g)=s(g)
,
(q\cdot g_{1})\cdot g_{2}=q\cdot(g_{1}\circ g_{2})
, and
q\cdot\mu(q)=q
for all
g_{1},g_{2}\in G
, whenever this is defined. A
left action
is defined analogously, swapping the roles of
t
and
s
.

(b)

A
right principal
G
-bundle over
B
is given by a diagram

\textstyle{P\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\pi}
\scriptstyle{\mu}
\textstyle{G\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{B}
\textstyle{M}

where
(P,\mu)
is a right
G
-space such that

(i)

\pi:P\to B
is a surjective submersion,

(ii)

\pi(p\cdot g)=\pi(p)
whenever
\mu(p)=t(g)
, and

(iii)

the map

P\times_{M}G\to P\times_{B}P,\quad(p,g)\mapsto(p,p\cdot g)

is a diffeomorphism.

(c)

A
Hilsum-Skandalis morphism

P:G\to H
is represented by a diagram

\textstyle{G\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{P\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\mu_{G}}
\scriptstyle{\mu_{H}}
\textstyle{H\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{M}
\textstyle{N}

where
(P,\mu_{G})
is a left
G
-space and
(\mu_{G}:P\to M,\mu_{H})
is a right principal
H
-bundle.

(d)

A
Morita equivalence

P:G\to H
is a Hilsum-Skandalis morphism as above such that
(\mu_{H}:P\to H,\mu_{G})
is a left principal
G
-bundle.

Remark 8.2
.

Hilsum-Skandalis morphisms can be composed, but their composition is only associative up to isomorphism.

Examples 8.3
.

(a)

Let
M
and
N
be spaces (thought of as trivial Lie groupoids), and let
F:M\to N
be a smooth map. Then

N\times_{M}M\to M,\quad(n,m)\mapsto m

is a left action of
N
on
M
along
F
, and

\textstyle{M\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{M\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\mathrm{id}}
\scriptstyle{\mathrm{F}}
\textstyle{N\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{M}
\textstyle{N}

is a Hilsum-Skandalis morphism
M:M\to N
, which is a Morita equivalence if and only if
F
is a diffeomorphism. In fact,
any
Hilsum-Skandalis morphism between spaces is of this form.

(b)

Two Lie
groups

G
and
H
are Morita equivalent if and only if they are isomorphic.

(c)

For any manifold
M
, the canonical action

\mathrm{Pair}(M)\times_{M}M\to M,\quad((m_{0},m_{1}),m_{1})\mapsto m_{0}

is a left action of
\mathrm{Pair}(M)
on
M
and

\textstyle{\mathrm{Pair}(M)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{M\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\mathrm{id}}
\textstyle{\mathrm{pnt}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{M}
\textstyle{\mathrm{pnt}}

is a Morita equivalence.

8.a.2
Weighted Morita Equivalence

We now state the analogue of
8.4
for weighted groupoids.

Definitions 8.4
.

Let
G\rightrightarrows M
and
H\rightrightarrows N
be weighted Lie groupoids.

(a)

A right action of
G\rightrightarrows M
on a weighted manifold
Q
along
\mu
is weighted if both
\mu
and the action map

Q\times_{M}G=\{(q,g):\mu(q)=t(g)\}\to Q,\quad(q,g)\mapsto q\cdot g

are weighted. We say that
Q
is a
weighted right
G
-space
.

(b)

A right principal
G
-bundle over
B

\textstyle{P\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\pi}
\scriptstyle{\mu}
\textstyle{G\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{B}
\textstyle{M}

is weighted if both
P
and
B
are weighted and

(i)

\pi:P\to B
is a weighted submersion,

(ii)

the map

P\times_{M}G\to P\times_{B}P,\quad(p,g)\mapsto(p,p\cdot g)

is a weighted diffeomorphism.

(c)

A
weighted Hilsum-Skandalis morphism

P:G\to H
is a diagram

\textstyle{G\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{P\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\mu_{G}}
\scriptstyle{\mu_{H}}
\textstyle{H\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{M}
\textstyle{N}

where
(P,\mu_{G})
is a weighted left
G
-space and
(\mu_{G}:P\to M,\mu_{H})
is a weighted right principal
H
-bundle.

(d)

A
weighted Morita equivalence

P:G\to H
is a weighted Hilsum-Skandalis morphism as above such that
(\mu_{H}:P\to H,\mu_{G})
is a weighted left principal
G
-bundle.

Remarks 8.5
.

(a)

In the definition of right weighted groupoid action, note that since
\mu:Q\to M
is weighted and
s:G\to M
is a weighted submersion, the fibre product
Q\times_{M}G
inherits a weighting as weighted submanifold of
Q\times G
. Therefore, this definition makes sense.

(b)

In the definition of a weighted right principal
G
-bundle, note that since the action map
P\times_{M}G\to P
factors through

P\times_{M}G\to P\times_{B}P\overset{\mathrm{pr_{2}}}{\longrightarrow}P

is follows that the action map is automatically weighted.

Examples 8.6
.

(a)

If
B
is a weighted manifold and
\mu:B\to M
is a weighted morphism, then

\textstyle{B\times_{M}G\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\mathrm{pr}_{2}}
\scriptstyle{s}
\textstyle{G\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{B}
\textstyle{M}

is the
trivial weighted right principal
G
-bundle over
B
.

(b)

If
G\rightrightarrows M
is any weighted Lie groupoid, then

\textstyle{G\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{G\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{t}
\scriptstyle{s}
\textstyle{G\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{M}
\textstyle{M}

defines a weighted Morita equivalence from
G
to itself.

(c)

If
(M,N)
is a weighted pair, then

\textstyle{\mathrm{Pair}(M)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{M\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\mathrm{id}}
\textstyle{\mathrm{pnt}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{M}
\textstyle{\mathrm{pnt}}

(68)

is a weighted Morita equivalence when
\mathrm{Pair}(M)
is weighted along
\mathrm{Pair}(M)
.

(d)

If
\mathrm{Pair}(M)
is trivially weighted along its units, then (
68
) is
not
a weighted Morita equivalence. This is because

\textstyle{M\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\mathrm{id}}
\scriptstyle{\mathrm{id}}
\textstyle{\mathrm{Pair}(M)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\mathrm{pnt}}
\textstyle{M}

is
not
a weighted principal
\mathrm{Pair}(M)
-bundle as the action map

M\times_{M}\mathrm{Pair}(M)\to M\times_{\mathrm{pnt}}M

is not a
weighted
diffeomorphism. Indeed,
M\times_{M}\mathrm{Pair}(M)
is weighted along the diagonal
M\hookrightarrow M\times_{M}\mathrm{Pair}(M)
, whereas
M\times_{\mathrm{pnt}}M
is weighted along itself, so the inverse map is not a weighted morphism.

We now work towards showing that weighted Morita equivalence is an equivalence relation on the set of (isomorphism classes of) weighted Lie groupoids, the main point being that weighted Hilsum-Skandalis morphisms can be composed. Since the composition of Hilsum-Skandalis morphisms is defined as a quotient, it is not immediately clear that their composition is weighted in any canonical way. The main tool in establishing that the quotient is naturally weighted is the following lemma, which says that weighted principal
G
-bundles are locally trivial in a weighted sense.

Lemma 8.7
.

If

\textstyle{P\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\pi}
\scriptstyle{\mu}
\textstyle{G\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{B}
\textstyle{M}

is a right principal
G
-bundle, then any point
p\in B
has an open neighbourhood
U\subseteq B
together with a weighted morphism
U\to M
such that

P|_{U}=\pi^{-1}(U)\cong U\times_{M}G

as weighted principal
G
-bundles.

Proof.

Since
\pi
is a weighted submersion, we can find a weighted section
\sigma:U\to P
defined near
p
. The map
U\to M
is defined as the composition

U\overset{\sigma}{\longrightarrow}P|_{U}\to M,

which is weighted.

The map

\displaystyle U\times_{B}G\to P|_{U},\quad(u,g)\mapsto\sigma(u).g

(69)

is a weighted, being the composition of weighted morphisms. We will show that it is a weighted diffeomorphism by constructing an explicit weighted inverse. The map

\delta:P\times_{B}P\overset{\cong}{\longrightarrow}P\times_{M}G\overset{\mathrm{pr}_{2}}{\longrightarrow}G

is a weighted morphism and therefore so is

\displaystyle P|_{U}\to U\times_{M}G,\quad p\mapsto(\pi(p),\delta(\sigma(\pi(p)),p)).

(70)

The maps (
69
) and (
70
) are inverse to one another, which proves the claim.
∎

Theorem 8.8
.

Let
G\rightrightarrows G_{0}
,
H\rightrightarrows H_{0}
, and
K\rightrightarrows K_{0}
be weighted Lie groupoids. If
P:G\to H
and
Q:H\to K
are weighted Hilsum-Skandalis morphisms then there is a unique weighting on the composition
P\circ Q=(P\times_{H_{0}}Q)/H
such that

(a)

the quotient map
\pi:P\times_{H_{0}}Q\to P\circ Q
is a weighted submersion and

(b)

P\circ Q:G\to K
is a weighted Hilsum-Skandalis morphism.

Proof.

Let

C^{\infty}(P\circ Q)_{(i)}=\{f\in C^{\infty}(P\circ Q):\pi^{*}f\in C^{\infty}(P\times_{H_{0}}Q)_{(i)}\}.

(71)

In order to show that this defines a weighting of
P\circ Q
, we must produce weighted coordinates.

The work in the previous sections shows that
g_{0}\in G_{0}
has an open neighbourhood
U\subseteq G_{0}
with a weighted morphism
U\to H_{0}
such that

(P\times_{H_{0}}Q)|_{U}=P|_{U}\times_{H_{0}}Q\cong U\times_{H_{0}}H\times_{H_{0}}Q

as weighted
H
-spaces. Moreover, the map

U\times_{H_{0}}H\times_{H_{0}}Q\to U\times_{H_{0}}Q,\quad(u,h,q)\mapsto(u,q)

is an
H
-equivariant weighted submersion which descends to a diffeomorphism
(U\times_{H_{0}}H\times_{H_{0}}Q)/H\cong U\times_{H_{0}}Q
making the following diagram commute

\textstyle{U\times_{H_{0}}H\times_{H_{0}}Q\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{(U\times_{H_{0}}H\times_{H_{0}}Q)/H\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\cong}
\textstyle{U\times_{H_{0}}Q}

In particular, submersion coordinates for
U\times_{H_{0}}H\times_{H_{0}}Q\to U\times_{H_{0}}Q
induced weighted coordinates on
(U\times_{H_{0}}H\times_{H_{0}}Q)/H
for the filtration (
71
) so that the quotient map
P\times_{H_{0}}Q\to P\circ Q
is a weighted submersion.
∎

Corollary 8.9
.

Weighted Morita equivalence is an equivalence relation on the set of isomorphism classes of weighted groupoids.

Appendix 8.B
Weighted Principal Bundles and their Associated Bundles

For completeness, we explain the associated bundle construction in the weighted setting.

Proposition 8.10
.

Let
(G,H)
be a weighted Lie groupoid pair. If
P\to M
is a weighted right principal
G
-bundle, then the submanifold
Q
along which
P
is weighted is a right principal
H
-bundle.

Proof.

Since the action map
P\times G\to P
is weighted, it follows that
H
acts on
Q
. Furthermore, since the projection
P\to M
is a weighted submersion, it follows that the restriction
Q\to N
is a submersion. Finally, since the action map
P\times_{M}G\to P\times_{B}P
is a weighted diffeomorphism, its restriction

Q\times_{N}H\to Q\times_{N}Q

is an isomorphism as well.
∎

Next, we specialize to the case when
(G,H)
is a weighted Lie group pair. Let
V
be a weighted vector space, and suppose that

\rho:G\times V\to V

(72)

is a linear action of
G
on
V
, which is weighted in the sense
\rho
is a weighted morphism between weighted vector bundles. We remark that for any
h\in H
the map

V_{(i)}\to V,\quad v\mapsto h.g

is a weighted morphism, whence
H
acts on
V
by filtration preserving automorphisms. Let
P\times_{G}V\to M
be the associated bundle and let

q:P\times V\to P\times_{G}V

be the quotient map.

Proposition 8.11
.

The filtration

C^{\infty}_{pol}(P\times_{G}V)_{(i)}=\{f\in C^{\infty}_{pol}(P\times_{G}V):q^{*}f\in C^{\infty}_{pol}(P\times V)_{(i)}\}

defines a linear weighting of
P\times_{G}V
. The filtration of
(P\times_{G}V)|_{N}
is given by

((P\times_{G}V)|_{N})_{(i)}=Q\times_{H}V_{(i)}.

Proof.

Given
p\in N
we will construct weighted vector bundle coordinates near
p
. Choose an open neighbourhood
U\subseteq M
containing
p
and an isomorphism
P|_{U}\cong U\times G
of weighted principal bundles. Since the diagram

\textstyle{U\times G\times V\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{q}
\textstyle{(U\times G)\times_{G}V\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\cong}
\textstyle{U\times V}

commutes, it follows that any choice of weighted vector bundle coordinate system for
U\times V
does the trick.

For the filtration statement, let
\sigma:U\to P
be a weighted section, and let
e_{1},\dots,e_{k}
be a weighted frame for
V
. Then the sections

\sigma_{a}:U\to P\times_{G}V\quad u\mapsto[(\sigma(u),e_{a})]

form a weighted frame for
(P\times_{G}V)|_{U}
, hence
((P\times_{G}V)|_{N\cap U})_{(i)}
is spanned by
\sigma_{a}
with
v_{a}\geq i
. Since
Q
is an
H
-principal subbundle of
P
the map

Q\times_{H}G\to P|_{N},\quad[(q,g)]\mapsto q.g

is a reduction of the structure group of
P|_{N}
from
G
to
H
. In particular,
(P\times_{G}V)|_{N}\cong Q\times_{H}V
. Since
\sigma(N\cap U)\subseteq Q
, if follows that
Q|_{U\cap N}\times_{H}V_{(i)}
is exactly the image of
((P\times_{G}V)|_{U\cap N})_{(i)}
under this isomorphism.
∎

Remark 8.12
.

Let
V\to M
be a rank
k
complex vector bundle. One might wonder if a linear weighting of
V\to M
induces a principal weighting of its frame bundle. The answer to this question is yes. This can be seen as an application of
Theorem 5.23
, which we outline now.

The weight vector defines a filtration of
\mathbb{C}^{k}
by subspaces, and this filtration, in turn, defines a Lie filtration of
\mathfrak{gl}(\mathbb{C}^{k})
. The space of filtration preserving endomorphisms of the filtered vector space
\mathbb{C}^{k}
, denoted
\mathfrak{gl}(\mathbb{C}^{k})_{(0)}
, is the Lie algebra of a connected Lie group
\mathrm{GL}(\mathbb{C}^{k})_{(0)}\subseteq\mathrm{GL}(\mathbb{C}^{k})
. Hence, by
Theorem 5.23
,
\mathrm{GL}(\mathbb{C}^{k})
has a canonical multiplicative weighting along
\mathrm{GL}(\mathbb{C}^{k})_{(0)}
. Furthermore, the canonical action

\mathrm{GL}(\mathbb{C}^{k})\times\mathbb{C}^{k}\to\mathbb{C}^{k}

is weighted (using
2.55
).

Next, let
F(V)\to M
denote the frame bundle of
V
, and let

F_{W}(V)=\{(p,f)\in N\times_{N}F(V)|\ f:\mathbb{C}^{k}\to V_{p}\text{ is filtration preserving}\}

be the bundle of filtration preserving frames. This is a principal
\mathrm{GL}(\mathbb{C}^{k})_{(0)}
-subbundle of
F(V)
. The weighting of
V
induces an infinitesimally multiplicative weighting of the Atiyah algebroid
\mathrm{At}(V)\Rightarrow M
such that the follow sequence of weighted vector bundles

0\overset{}{\longrightarrow}\mathrm{End}(V)\overset{}{\longrightarrow}\mathrm{At}(V)\overset{}{\longrightarrow}TM\overset{}{\longrightarrow}0

(73)

is
weighted exact
, in the sense that one can find a weighted splitting. Identifying sections of
\mathrm{At}(V)
with the
\mathrm{GL}(\mathbb{C}^{k})
-invariant vector fields on
F(V)
we see that the linear weighting of
V
induces a singular Lie filtration on
F(V)
. Moreover, using that (
73
) is weighted exact, we have that
F_{W}(V)
is a clean submanifold with respect to this singular Lie filtration. By
Theorem 2.54
, this defines a weighting of
F(V)
along
F_{W}(V)
. Again, using (
73
) one can deduce that this weighting is principal.

†
†
margin:

9

The Spray Exponential

For a Lie group
G
with Lie algebra
\mathfrak{g}
, there is a canonical exponential map
\exp:\mathfrak{g}\to G
which is a diffeomorphism near the origin. For a general Lie groupoid
G\rightrightarrows M
with Lie algebroid
A\Rightarrow M
there is no longer a canonical exponential map relating the two. Nonetheless, it is shown in
[
CMS20
]
that after a choice of a
Lie algebroid spray

V\in\mathfrak{X}
is made an exponential map
\exp_{V}:A\dashrightarrow G
defined on an open neighbourhood of the zero section in
A
can be defined. In this appendix we review this construction. For the following, let
G\rightrightarrows M
be a Lie groupoid with Lie algebroid
A=\mathrm{Lie}(G)\Rightarrow M
.

Appendix 9.A
A
-paths and
G
-paths

Let
J\subseteq\mathbb{R}
be an open neighbourhood of the origin. Since
J
is one dimensional, a Lie algebroid morphism is a vector bundle morphism
\varphi:TJ\to A
with the property that

(a\circ\varphi)\left(\frac{\partial}{\partial t}\bigg|_{t=s}\right)=T_{s}\varphi_{M}\left(\frac{\partial}{\partial t}\bigg|_{t=s}\right)

for all
s\in J
. In particular,
\varphi
defines a path

\gamma:J\to A,\quad s\mapsto\varphi\left(\frac{\partial}{\partial t}\bigg|_{t=s}\right)

which lifts the path
\varphi_{M}
. We summarize these properties in the following definition.

Definition 9.1
.

An
A
-path
is a path
\gamma:J\to A
with the property that

a(\gamma(s))=T_{s}\gamma_{M}\left(\frac{\partial}{\partial t}\bigg|_{t=s}\right)

(74)

for all
s\in J
, where
\gamma_{M}:J\to M
is the base map of
\gamma
.

Thus any Lie algebroid morphism
\gamma:TJ\to A
defines an
A
-path. The converse is also true: given an
A
-path
\gamma:\mathbb{R}\to A
we let
\tilde{\gamma}:TJ\to A
be the vector bundle morphism defined by

\tilde{\gamma}\left(\frac{\partial}{\partial t}\bigg|_{t=s}\right)=\gamma(s).

This is a morphism of anchored vector bundles precisely because of (
74
). Thus, we have established a 1-1 correspondence between
A
-paths
J\to A
and Lie algebroid morphisms
TJ\to A
.

The dual notion of an
A
-path is a
G
-path:

Definition 9.2
.

A
G
-path
is a path
\gamma:J\to G
such that

\mathrm{t}(\gamma(t))=\gamma(0)\in M

for all
t\in J
.

Given a
G
-path
\gamma:J\to G
, the map

\tilde{\gamma}:\mathrm{Pair}(J)\to G,\quad(t,s)\mapsto\gamma(t)^{-1}\gamma(s)

is a Lie groupoid morphism. Conversely, any groupoid morphism
\gamma:\mathrm{Pair}(J)\to G
defined a
G
-path by

t\mapsto\gamma(0,t).

Thus, there is a 1-1 correspondence between
G
-paths
J\to G
and groupoid morphisms
\mathrm{Pair}(J)\to G
.

Appendix 9.B
The Maurer-Cartan Form

Let
T^{t}G=\mathrm{ker}(Tt)
be the tangent space to the
t
-fibres. Given
g\in G
with
s(g)=x
and
t(g)=y
, left multiplication by
g
defines a diffeomorphism
L_{g}:t^{-1}(x)\to t^{-1}(y)
. For each
g\in G
, this defines an isomorphism

T_{g}L_{g^{-}1}:T^{t}_{g}G\to T^{t}_{s(g)}G=A_{s(g)}

which fits together to define a vector bundle map

\theta^{G}:T^{t}G\to A

covering
s:G\to M
.

Definition 9.3
.

The vector bundle morphism
\theta^{G}:T^{t}G\to A
is the (left-invariant) Maurer-Cartan form for
G
.

We can use the Maurer-Cartan form to differentiate
G
-paths to
A
-paths. Let
\gamma:J\to G
be a
G
-path. Since
\mathrm{t}(\gamma(t))=\gamma(0)
for all
t\in J
, it follows that

\dot{\gamma}(t):J\to TG,\quad t\mapsto T_{t}\gamma\left(\frac{\partial}{\partial t}\bigg|_{t}\right)

defines a path
J\to T^{\mathrm{t}}G
. Hence we can define

D^{L}\gamma:J\to A,\quad t\mapsto\theta^{G}(\dot{\gamma}(t)).

Alternatively, we can identify
\gamma
with a groupoid morphism
\mathrm{Pair}(J)\to G
and let
D^{L}\gamma:J\to A
be the
A
-path defined by the induce Lie algebroid morphism
TJ\to A
(see
[
CF03
, Propostion 1.1]
). Using Lie’s second theorem for Lie algebroids (
[
MX00
, Theorem A.1]
), any
A
-path also integrates (uniquely) to a
G
-path. Summarizing,

Proposition 9.4
.

(cf.
[
CF03
, Propostion 1.1]
)
The assignment
\gamma\mapsto D^{L}\gamma
defines a 1-1 correspondence between
G
-paths and
A
-paths.

In particular, the
G
-path
\varphi:J\to G
integrating a given
A
-path
\gamma:J\to A
with
\gamma(0)\in A_{x}
is the unique solution to the initial value problem

\theta^{G}(\dot{\varphi}(t))=\gamma(t),\quad\varphi(0)=x.

(75)

Appendix 9.C
The Spray Exponential

We want to use the correspondence between
A
-paths and
G
-paths to define an exponential map for Lie groupoids. In order to do this, we need a way of defining an
A
-path through a given
a\in A
. The data needed for this is a Lie algebroid spray (cf.
[
CMS20
, Definition 3.1]
).

Definition 9.5
.

A
spray
on a Lie algebroid
A\rightarrow M
is a vector field
V\in\mathfrak{X}(A)
such that

(a)

\kappa_{t}^{*}V=tV
for all
t\neq 0
, where
\kappa_{t}
denotes scalar multiplication by
t
, and

(b)

for all
a\in A
, one has
T\pi(V_{a})=\rho(a)
, where
\pi:A\to M
is the vector bundle projection and
\rho:A\to TM
is the anchor.

Remarks 9.6
.

Let us summarize some facts about Lie algebroid sprays, as explained in
[
CMS20
, Section 3.1]
.

(a)

The first condition implies that
V
vanishes along
M
. Hence, there is an open neighbourhood
M\subseteq U_{V}\subseteq A
for which the flow
\phi_{V}^{t}
up to time
t=1
is defined for all
v\in U_{V}
.

(b)

The second condition implies that for all
v\in U_{V}
the map

\phi_{v}:J\to A,\quad t\mapsto\phi_{V}^{t}(v)

is an
A
-path, where
J\subseteq\mathbb{R}
is an open interval centered at 0 and containing 1.

In particular, a choice Lie algebroid spray
V\in\mathfrak{X}(A)
determines an
A
-path starting at
v
for any
v\in A
sufficiently close to the zero section. This motivated the following definition, cf.
[
CMS20
, Definition 3.20]
.

Definition 9.7
.

Let
A=\mathrm{Lie}(G)
and let
V\in\mathfrak{X}(A)
be a Lie algebroid spray. Given
v\in A
sufficiently close to the zero section, let
\exp_{V}(v)=\phi_{G}(1)\in G
where
\phi_{G}(t)
is the
G
-path integrating the
A
-path
\phi_{V}^{t}(v)
. The
spray exponential
is the map

\exp_{V}:A\dashrightarrow G,\quad v\mapsto\exp_{V}(v)

defined on an open neighbourhood
U_{V}\subseteq A
containing the zero section.

Remark 9.8
.

In
[
CMS20
]
they define the spray exponential to be the solution of the initial value problem (
75
), but by the discussion in the previous section we see they give the same result.

Lemma 9.9
.

Let
H\rightrightarrows N
be a Lie subgroupoid of
G\rightrightarrows M
, and let
B=\mathrm{Lie}(H)
. If
V\in\mathfrak{X}(A)
is tangent to
B
, then
\exp_{V}(v)\in H
for all
v\in B
sufficiently close to the zero section.

Proof.

Since
B
is a Lie subalgebroid, the restriction of
V
to
B
is a Lie algebroid spray for
B
. Thus, for
v\in B
sufficiently close to zero,
\phi_{V}^{t}(v)
is an
A
-path whose image lies in
B
, hence integrates to an
G
-path whose image lies in
H
.
∎

Appendix 9.D
Applying the Higher Tangent Functor

Finally, we are interested in examining how applying the higher tangent functor affects to corresponding exponential map.

Lemma 9.10
.

Suppose that
X\in\mathfrak{X}(M)
with flow
\phi_{X}
, defined for time
|t|\leq\epsilon
. Then

T_{r}\phi_{X}^{t}=\phi_{X^{(0)}}^{t}.

Proof.

For
t,s
sufficiently small we have by functoriality that

T_{r}\phi_{X}^{0}=\mathrm{id}\quad\text{and}\quad T_{r}\phi_{X}^{t+s}=T_{r}(\phi_{X}^{t}\phi_{X}^{s})=T_{r}\phi_{X}^{t}T_{r}\phi_{X}^{s},

hence
T_{r}\phi_{X}^{t}
is the flow of a vector field. To verify that this vector field is
X^{(0)}
, let
f\in C^{\infty}(M)
and observe that

\displaystyle\frac{d}{dt}\bigg|_{t=0}(T_{r}\phi_{X}^{t})^{*}f^{(i)}=\frac{d}{dt}\bigg|_{t=0}((\phi^{t}_{X})^{*}f)^{(i)}=(Xf)^{(i)}=X^{(0)}f^{(i)},

for
i=0,1,\dots,r
.
∎

Remark 9.11
.

In particular,
\phi_{X^{(0)}}^{t}
is also defined for
|t|\leq\epsilon
.

Recall that if
A\Rightarrow M
is a Lie algebroid, then there is a canonical Lie algebroid structure
T_{r}A\Rightarrow T_{r}M
such that

a_{T_{r}A}=T_{r}a\quad\text{and}\quad[\sigma^{(-i)},\tau^{(-j)}]=[\sigma,\tau]^{(-i-j)}.

The canonical identification
T_{r}(TG)\cong T(T_{r}G)
restricts to an identification
T_{r}(T^{\mathrm{t}}G|_{M})\cong T^{T_{r}\mathrm{t}}(T_{r}G)|_{T_{r}M}
. In particular, we see that
T_{r}A
is the Lie algebroid of
T_{r}G
.

Proposition 9.12
.

(a)

If
V\in\mathfrak{X}(A)
is a Lie algebroid spray, then
T_{r}V=V^{(0)}
is a Lie algebroid spray for
T_{r}A
.

(b)

For any Lie algebroid spray
V\in\mathfrak{X}(A)
, the partially defined maps

T_{r}\exp_{V}:T_{r}A\dashrightarrow T_{r}G\quad\text{and}\quad\exp_{V^{(0)}}:T_{r}A\dashrightarrow T_{r}G

agree.

Proof.

(a)

Let
E\in\mathfrak{X}(A)
denote the Euler vector field for
A
. Then
E^{(0)}\in\mathfrak{X}(T_{r}A)
is the Euler vector field for
T_{r}A
and we have that

[E^{(0)},V^{(0)}]=[E,V]^{(0)}=V^{(0)},

so
T_{r}V
satisfies
Definition 9.5
(a). Condition (b) in
Definition 9.5
follows by functoriality.

(b)

Let
U_{V}\subseteq A
be an open neighbourhood of the zero section for which
\phi_{V}^{t}
is defined for
|t|\leq 1
. By applying
Lemma 9.10
to
M=U_{V}
we see that, for any
u\in T_{r}U_{V}
, the
T_{r}A
-paths
T_{r}\phi_{V}^{t}(u)
and
\phi_{V^{(0)}}^{t}(u)
agree. Therefore, they integrate to the same
T_{r}G
-path, which proves the claim. ∎

†
margin:

10

Proofs of Various Lemmas

Appendix 10.A
Proof of
Lemma 3.28

Lemma 10.1

(=
Lemma 3.28
)
.

For
\sigma\in\Gamma(V)_{(i)}
, the
i
-th homogeneous approximation is a smooth section of
\nu_{\mathcal{W}}(V)
which is homogeneous of degree
-i
and which depends only on the class of
\sigma
in
\Gamma(V)_{(i)}/\Gamma(V)_{(i+1)}
. Moreover, for any
f\in C^{\infty}_{[n]}(V)_{(j)}
and
g\in C^{\infty}(M)_{(k)}
one has

\displaystyle\begin{split}f^{[j]}\circ\sigma^{[i]}&=(f\circ\sigma)^{[j+ni]}\in C^{\infty}(\nu_{\mathcal{W}}(M,N))\quad\text{and}\\
g^{[k]}\sigma^{[i]}&=(g\sigma)^{[i+k]}\in\Gamma(\nu_{\mathcal{W}}(V)).\end{split}

(76)

Proof.

The equation
f^{[j]}\circ\sigma^{[i]}=(f\circ\sigma)^{[j+ni]}
follows from (
29
), and it follows from this that
\sigma^{[i]}:\nu_{\mathcal{W}}(M,N)\to\nu_{\mathcal{W}}(V)
is smooth. To see that it is a section, we note that

\nu_{\mathcal{W}}(\pi)\circ\sigma^{[i]}=\nu_{\mathcal{W}}(\pi\circ\sigma)=\mathrm{id}_{\nu_{\mathcal{W}}(M,N)}.

Given
g\in C^{\infty}(M)_{(k)}
,
f\in C^{\infty}_{[n]}(V)_{(j)}
and
\varphi\in\nu_{\mathcal{W}}(M,N)
, using that
f
is homogeneous of degree
n
we compute

\displaystyle((g^{[k]}\sigma^{[i]})(\varphi))(f^{[j]})

\displaystyle=(\nu_{\mathcal{W}}(\kappa_{\varphi(g^{[k]})})\sigma^{[i]}(\varphi))(f^{[j]})

\displaystyle=(\sigma^{[i]}(\varphi))(\kappa_{\varphi(g^{[k]})}^{*}f^{[j]})

\displaystyle=(\sigma^{[i]}(\varphi))(\varphi(g^{[k]})^{n}f^{[j]})

\displaystyle=\varphi(g^{[k]})^{n}\varphi((f\circ\sigma)^{[j+ni]})

\displaystyle=\varphi((g^{n})^{[nk]}(f\circ\sigma)^{[j+ni]})

\displaystyle=\varphi((g^{n}(f\circ\sigma))^{[j+ni+nk]})

\displaystyle=\varphi((f\circ(g\sigma))^{[j+n(i+k)]})

\displaystyle=((g\sigma)^{[i+k]}(\varphi))(f^{[j]}),

which establishes (
76
). To show that
\sigma^{[i]}
only depends on the class of
\sigma\in\Gamma(V)_{(i)}/\Gamma(V)_{(i+1)}
, it suffices to show that if
\sigma\in\Gamma(V)_{(i+1)}
then
\sigma^{[i]}=0
. Specifically, what this means is that for any
f\in C^{\infty}_{pol}(V)_{(j)}
and
\varphi\in\nu_{\mathcal{W}}(M,N)
one has

(\sigma^{[i]}(\varphi))(f^{[j]})=\varphi(\kappa_{0}^{*}f^{[j]}).

(77)

If
f\in C^{\infty}_{[n]}(V)_{(j)}
with
n\geq 1
, one has that

f\circ\sigma\in C^{\infty}(M)_{(j+n(i+1))}\implies(f\circ\sigma)^{[j+ni]}=0,

so (
77
) follows in this case. For
f\in C^{\infty}_{[0]}(V)_{(j)}=C^{\infty}(M)_{(j)}
one has that

(\sigma^{[i]}(\varphi))(f^{[j]})=\varphi((f\circ\sigma)^{[j]})=\varphi(f^{[j]})=\varphi(\kappa_{0}^{*}f^{[j]}),

since
f
is homogeneous of degree zero. Since any polynomial is a sum of monomials, it follows that
\sigma^{[i]}=0
. Finally, to see that
\sigma^{[i]}\in\Gamma_{[-i]}(\nu_{\mathcal{W}}(V))
, we compute

\displaystyle(\alpha_{\lambda}(\sigma^{[i]}(\varphi)))(f^{[j]})

\displaystyle=(\sigma^{[i]}(\varphi))(\lambda^{j}f^{[j]})

\displaystyle=\varphi(\lambda^{j}(f\circ\sigma)^{[j+ni]})

\displaystyle=\varphi(\lambda^{j+ni}(f\circ(\lambda^{-i}\sigma))^{[j+ni]})

\displaystyle=(\alpha_{\lambda}(\varphi))((f\circ(\lambda^{-i}\sigma))^{[j+ni]})

\displaystyle=(\lambda^{-i}\sigma^{[i]}(\alpha_{\lambda}(\varphi)))(f^{[j]}).\qed

Appendix 10.B
Proof of
Lemma 3.46

We now prove the following.

Lemma 10.2

(=
Lemma 3.46
)
.

If
\mathrm{Pair}(P)
,
\mathrm{Pair}(\mathrm{Spin}(k))
are given the doubled trivial weighting along the diagonal, and
\mathbb{C}\mathrm{l}(\mathbb{R}^{k})
is given the linear weighting defined by its filtration by subspaces, then the group action

\mathrm{Pair}(\mathrm{Spin}(k))\times\mathrm{Pair}(P)\times\mathbb{C}\mathrm{l}(\mathbb{R}^{k})\to\mathrm{Pair}(P)\times\mathbb{C}\mathrm{l}(\mathbb{R}^{k})

(78)

is a weighted morphism.

As indicated above, the proof uses a characterization of weighted morphisms in terms of weighted paths. We being by explaining this characterization and then give a proof of the lemma. The contents of this section are based on communications with Gabriel Beiner, Yiannis Loizides, and Eckhard Meinrenken.

Let us briefly recall the set up. Let
V\to M
be a rank
k
vector bundle with inner product and given spin structure. Let
P\to M
be the principal
\mathrm{Spin}(k)
-bundle specified by the spin structure, and recall that the action of
\mathrm{Pair}(\mathrm{Spin}(k))
on
\mathrm{Pair}(P)\times\mathbb{C}\mathrm{l}(\mathbb{R}^{k})
is given by

(g_{1},g_{2}).(p_{1},p_{2},v)=(p_{1}.g_{1},p_{2}.g_{2},g_{1}vg_{2}^{-1}),

where we are making use of the inclusion
\mathrm{Spin}(k)\subseteq\mathbb{C}\mathrm{l}(\mathbb{R}^{k})
. In order to show that this action is weighted, we make use of weighted paths.

The weighting on both
\mathrm{Pair}(P)
and
\mathrm{Pair}(\mathrm{Spin}(k))
is the doubled weighting along the diagonal, where a function has filtration degree
2i
if it vanishes to order
i
along the diagonal. Giving
\mathrm{Pair}(M)
the doubled weighting along the diagonal, one finds that
\mathrm{Pair}(P)
can be identified locally with
\mathrm{Pair}(M)\times\mathrm{Pair}(\mathrm{Spin}(k))
as weighted manifolds. Thus, to show that the action of
\mathrm{Pair}(\mathrm{Spin}(k))
on
\mathrm{Pair}(P)
is weighted, it is sufficient (in fact, equivalent) to show that the action of
\mathrm{Pair}(\mathrm{Spin}(k))
on itself is a weighted morphism.

Lemma 10.3
.

Suppose that
G
is a Lie group and
H\subseteq G
is a closed subgroup. If
G
is given the doubled weighting along
H
, then the map

\displaystyle a:G\times G

\displaystyle\to G

\displaystyle(g_{1},g_{2})

\displaystyle\mapsto g_{1}g_{2}^{-1}

is a weighted morphism.

Proof.

By definition of the doubled weighting, the map
a
is weighted if and only if
a^{*}\mathcal{I}_{H}\subseteq\mathcal{I}_{H\times H}
. From this it follows that
a
is a weighted morphism, since
H
is a subgroup.
∎

It remains to show that the action of
\mathrm{Pair}(\mathrm{Spin}(k))
on
\mathbb{C}\mathrm{l}(\mathbb{R}^{k})
is weighted, which we accomplish by making use of weighted paths. It is sufficient to consider path
\gamma
in
\mathrm{Pair}(\mathrm{Spin}(k))\times\mathbb{C}\mathrm{l}(\mathbb{R}^{k})
of the form

\gamma(t)=\left(g\exp(\xi t)\exp(\xi_{1}(t)),g\exp(\xi t)\exp(\xi_{2}(t)),\sum_{i=0}^{k}c_{i}t^{i}\right),

where
g\in\mathrm{Spin}(k)
,
\xi\in\mathfrak{spin}(k)
,
\xi_{1}(t),\xi_{2}(t)=O(t^{2})
, and
c_{i}\in\mathbb{C}\mathrm{l}_{i}(\mathbb{R}^{k})
. Composing this with the action
\mathrm{Pair}(\mathrm{Spin}(k))\times\mathbb{C}\mathrm{l}(\mathbb{R}^{K})\to\mathbb{C}\mathrm{l}(\mathbb{R}^{k})
gives

\sum_{i=0}^{k}\mathrm{Ad}_{g\exp(\xi t)}\left(\exp(\xi_{1}(t))c_{i}\exp(-\eta_{2}(t))t^{i}\right).

(79)

Using that the adjoint action of
\mathrm{Spin}(k)
on
\mathbb{C}\mathrm{l}(\mathbb{R}^{k})
is filtration preserving, the identification
\mathfrak{spin}(k)=\mathbb{C}\mathrm{l}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{k})
, and the fact that
\xi_{1}(t),\xi_{2}(t)=O(t^{2})
, it follows from the power series expansion of the exponential map that

\sum_{i=0}^{k}\mathrm{Ad}_{g\exp(\xi t)}\left(\exp(\xi_{1}(t))c_{i}\exp(-\eta_{2}(t))t^{i}\right)=\sum_{i=0}^{k}v_{i}t^{i}+O(t^{k+1}),

for some
v_{i}\in\mathbb{C}\mathrm{l}_{i}(\mathbb{R}^{k})
. In particular, (
79
) is a weighted path. By
Theorem 2.35
, this completes the proof of
Lemma 3.46
.

Appendix 10.C
Proof of
Lemma 4.19

Let
V\to M
and
W\to M^{\prime}
be weighted vector bundles.

Proposition 10.4
.

Suppose that
\varphi:V\to W
is a weighted vector bundle morphism such that the base map
\varphi_{M}:M\to M^{\prime}
is a weighted submersion and the maps

(V|_{N})_{(i)}\to(W|_{M^{\prime}})_{(i)}

are fibrewise surjective. The
\ker(\varphi)
is a weighted subbundle of
V
.

Proof.

As in the proof of
Theorem 3.19
, we may assume that the weightings of both
V
and
W
are concentrated in negative degree. In this case, the assumptions on
\varphi
ensure that it is a weighted submersion. Since
M^{\prime}
is a weighted submanifold of
W
, it follows that

\mathrm{ker}(\varphi)=\varphi^{-1}(M^{\prime})

is a weighted subbundle of
V
by
Corollary 2.23
.
∎

Corollary 10.5

(=
Lemma 4.19
)
.

Let
V\to M
be a weighted vector bundle and
W\to R
a weighted subbundle. Then
\mathrm{ann}(W)
is a weighted subbundle of
V^{*}
.

Proof.

Let
i:R\hookrightarrow M
be the inclusion. Then
i^{*}V^{*}\to R
is a weighted vector bundle by
Section 10.2
. The induced map

i^{*}V^{*}\to W^{*}

satisfies the assumptions of
10.4
and has kernel equal to
\mathrm{ann}(W)
.
∎

Remark 10.6
.

By a similar argument, if
V_{1}
and
V_{2}
are weighted vector bundles, and
R\subseteq V_{2}\times V_{1}
is a weighted subbundle, then
\mathrm{ann}^{\sharp}(R)\subseteq V_{2}^{*}\times V_{1}^{*}
is a weighted subbundle. Indeed, it is the kernel of the map

i^{*}(V_{2}^{*}\times V_{1}^{*})\to R^{*},\quad(\xi_{2},\xi_{1})\mapsto\xi_{2}-\xi_{1}.
